- [x] 🟠 M — **donor-rollup-refresh pg_cron dead since 08-03 (job startup timeout) — official_donor_totals stale; break-glass sweep gate always refuses** — Found during the FIX-965 investigation (2026-08-06 ~02:20 UTC). Three consecutive daily donor-rollup-refresh pg_cron runs died AT STARTUP: cron.job_run_details 08-03/08-04/08-05 09:00 UTC all show status=failed, return_message=job startup timeout, ending ~10s after start — the FIX-944 resumable procedure never began, so data_sync_log has NO row for those days (last: 08-02 complete) and pipeline_state.donor_rollup_watermark is parked at last_indexed_at=2026-08-02 05:54. Consequence, measured: official_donor_totals understates live FR for the FIX-934/954-remediated officials — Guthrie 1101469200 vs 1150146700 live, Mike Johnson 1135238100 vs 1178098800, Emmer 1096423800 vs 1220121700 (officials.total_received_cents matches live for all three; ONLY odt and its five sibling rollups lag) — every surface reading odt serves pre-08-05 numbers, and the FIX-944 self-heal never fires because the job never starts. Probable cause: post-indiv22 the nightly refresh_derived_mvs window stretched to 06:00-09:29 UTC (3.5h) so the box is saturated at 09:00 and the pg_cron worker cannot launch inside its startup window — diagnose before rehoming the schedule. Second blocker, found while building the FIX-965 sweep: the FIX-944 break-glass sweep (donor-rollup-sweep.ts) has a broken refusal gate — SHOW statement_timeout returns a column NAMED statement_timeout, the script reads .st (always undefined) and therefore ALWAYS refuses to run; treemap-global-sweep.ts carries the corrected current_setting() form — port it. Fix shape: repair the sweep gate, diagnose/rehome the 09:00 schedule clear of the nightly tail, then one catch-up run (sweep or cron) to converge odt. **⚠ CORRECTED 2026-08-09 — the 12:00 backstop's central design premise is falsified.** What shipped under this FIX was a same-day 12:00 backstop firing for jobid 24, justified on the stated assumption (migration `20260806010000_fix968_donor_rollup_backstop_and_cron_health.sql`, header comment) that "09:00 still draining → 12:00 hits the advisory lock and logs `status='skipped'`, which is already a handled state". **The advisory lock is never reached.** pg_cron keeps **one task per jobid** and QUEUES the next firing behind a still-running one; `start_time` in `cron.job_run_details` is the real start and lands ~1.0s after the prior run's `end_time` — measured on 08-06, 08-07 AND 08-08. `cron.max_running_jobs` is 32, so this is not a slot cap either. **No `skipped` row is ever written**, which is exactly why the chaining was invisible in `data_sync_log` and cost a diagnostic pass to find ([[FIX-1002]]). Consequence worth stating plainly: **a split schedule provides NO isolation once a run overruns** — 09:00 and 12:00 chain into one continuous block rather than acting as two bounded windows — so **a per-run budget must sit BELOW the gap between firings**, or the second firing simply inherits the first's overrun. The same erroneous premise is repeated in migration `20260806230000_fix972_donor_rollup_chunk_quantum.sql`; both migrations are applied and therefore frozen, so this bullet is the correction of record. Cross-ref [[FIX-944]], [[FIX-951]], [[FIX-965]], [[FIX-952]], [[FIX-934]], [[FIX-1002]]. <!--id:FIX-968-->
- [x] 🟠 M — **BULK regime for the six donor rollups — to_id-range, set-based, one index-only FR scan per chunk (cleared the 9,086 backlog in 15m26s)** — Built and RUN on prod 2026-08-07 02:14-02:29 UTC. Full write-up: docs/audits/2026-08-07-fix974-bulk-regime.md. **The per-recipient regime is the wrong shape for a backlog, and this is the measurement that proves it.** [[FIX-973]] measured refresh_official_donor_rollup_incremental() at 19.0 s/recipient on 08-06 (~33 ms per FR row, about one random page per row); the 9,086 recipients then outstanding needed ~48 h of pure compute, naive ETA ~Aug 12, with Sunday fec_bulk due to refill the dirty set first. **New procedure donor_rollup_rebuild_bulk()** (migration 20260807000000) generalises the [[FIX-965]] resumable-global-rebuild pattern to the whole six-arm family: to_id RANGE chunks (default 32, GUC civitics.donor_rollup_bulk_chunks) instead of scattered to_id = ANY(uuid[]) probes, and ONE scan per chunk landing in UNLOGGED staging from which ALL SIX arms are derived, instead of six arms each re-scanning FR for the same recipients through a 256 MB shared_buffers. Mode via civitics.donor_rollup_bulk_mode (dirty default, full), budget via civitics.donor_rollup_bulk_budget_seconds; cursor advances inside each chunk transaction, predictive between-chunk budget is the only clean stop (status=partial, resumable), a chunk failure ABORTS rather than skipping, and it takes the SAME advisory lock as the incremental. **⚠ CLARIFIED 2026-08-09 — the original wording said a 09:00/12:00 firing "defers behind it", which names the wrong outcome.** Both procedures acquire the lock with `pg_try_advisory_lock` (NON-blocking), so a scheduled firing that finds the lock held by a bulk run does NOT wait: it INSERTs a `data_sync_log` row with `status='skipped'` and `skip_reason='advisory lock held by a concurrent donor-rollup refresh'`, then RETURNs. **That firing's work is LOST until the NEXT scheduled firing — it is not delayed into this one.** Three distinct behaviours are being described with one word across this family, and that conflation has now produced two falsified bullets ([[FIX-968]], and this one), so name the one you mean: **(a) QUEUED** — same jobid, a firing due while the previous one still runs: pg_cron keeps ONE task per jobid and runs them back-to-back, `cron.job_run_details.start_time` landing ~1.0s after the prior `end_time`, NO `skipped` row written and nothing at all visible in `data_sync_log` ([[FIX-968]], [[FIX-1002]]); **(b) SKIPPED** — a DIFFERENT session holds the advisory lock (the `data:donor-rollup:bulk` driver, or any manual sweep): `pg_try_advisory_lock` returns false, a `status='skipped'` row IS written, and that firing is forfeited; **(c) "defers"** — used in this repo for both (a) and (b), and precise about neither. Do not use it. **This bullet's case is (b), not (a).** The same conflation is frozen into the header comment of migration `20260807000000_fix974_donor_rollup_bulk_regime.sql` (~line 88: "defers behind it exactly as the two firings already chain behind each other"), which states the equivalence between (a) and (b) outright and cites the [[FIX-972]] chaining premise that [[FIX-1002]] later falsified; the live `COMMENT ON PROCEDURE` text is clean (it claims only that the lock is shared). Migrations are applied and therefore frozen, so this bullet is the correction of record. Driver packages/data/src/scripts/donor-rollup-bulk.ts (data:donor-rollup:bulk / :prod) lifts statement_timeout, re-CALLs while partial, and VACUUM (ANALYZE)s all six rewritten arms ([[FIX-943]] rule; VACUUM cannot run inside a procedure). **The unlock is an equivalence, measured not assumed:** arms 2-6 filter fr.to_type = official, and to_type is NOT in financial_relationships_donor_rollup_idx, so every such row would need a heap visit — but across all 6,457,535 rows in that index scope there are ZERO rows with to_type=official lacking an officials row and ZERO rows with another to_type whose to_id IS an official. Resolving official-ness through the target table instead of the heap column keeps the whole scan INDEX-ONLY (FR was 94.1% all-visible). Prod EXPLAIN of chunk 0: Index Only Scan, 184,067 rows to 150,437 groups in 21.8 s = **0.118 ms/FR row vs FIX-973 33.0 ms/row**. **Result: all 15,316 recipients (6,947 officials) in 926 s, one CALL, 32/32 chunks, slowest chunk 59 s, 1,006,285 arm-1 rows, 13 s vacuum tail — 0.060 s/recipient vs 19.0, about 314x.** The backlog was cleared as a superset (a full pass, not just the 9,086 owed) and the dirty set the next firing will build is 0. **Correctness is equality, not deltas:** locally the bulk output is BYTE-IDENTICAL to the live per-recipient path over the same 827 recipients — row-wise set difference both directions, 0/0 on every arm including the 50,733-row arm 1 — and identical again after a forced-budget park at chunk 0 plus resume. On prod: named-three published==live to the cent (Mike Johnson had been understating by $524,106, Emmer by $1,349,979), a 60-official sample from the reconstructed pre-run backlog with 0 mismatches on all six arms (arm 5 row-identical over 10,216 rows), all five platform-level sums exactly equal to a live FR aggregation, and financial_relationships itself byte-identical before/after (10,280,280 rows / 377,497,303,748,122 cents / same max updated_at) so nothing here touched attribution. GLOBAL treemap scope preserved at 3,034 rows. **Deliberate deviation from FIX-965: no whole-table publish swap.** FIX-965 needs one because its chunks are keyed by donor while its output is partitioned by state; here the chunk key IS the output partition key (every arm is keyed by official_id/scope_id = to_id), so a chunk output is final and there is no cross-chunk merge to protect. Writing straight into the live arms per chunk halves the write volume and the dead-tuple load on a box whose measured problem is I/O, and one chunk is one transaction so an official is either wholly old or wholly new consistently ACROSS all six arms. **dirty vs full was measured, not assumed:** locally full 13,139 targets 86 s vs dirty 4,885 targets 88 s — dirty is marginally SLOWER because the range walk dominates and the semi-join removes none of it. Chose full. **One latent divergence found and guarded** (migration 20260807010000): arm 2 official_donor_totals is the only arm whose live body has NO from_type predicate, so it is defined more broadly than the rollup index can see. Measured empty on prod (all 4,098,213 donation-to-official rows are from_type=financial_entity, single group), which is why byte-identity passed — but donor_rollup_bulk_assert_invariants() now asserts it BEFORE any arm is written, so a future violation fails loud instead of silently understating that official. **This is the bulk half of [[FIX-969]] regime split**; auto-selecting bulk vs trickle by dirty-set size is still 969 PR. Does NOT close [[FIX-973]] — it routes around the regression rather than explaining it, and the per-recipient path is still what the daily trickle uses; see the dated measurement line added to that bullet. [[FIX-970]] untouched (arm 1 deliberately still not scoped to officials, matching the live body). Cross-ref [[FIX-965]], [[FIX-944]], [[FIX-969]], [[FIX-970]], [[FIX-972]], [[FIX-973]], [[FIX-943]], [[FIX-884]], [[FIX-589]], [[FIX-836]]. <!--id:FIX-974-->
- [x] 🔴 M — **financial_entities has no VACUUM owner anywhere on the schedule — 53.7% all-visible and falling ~2,800 pages/min, 3.7 points from the canary's escalation floor** — **Found by the 2026-08-07 site-wide efficiency audit (`docs/audits/2026-08-07-efficiency-audit.md`), class 2 — heap-forced read shapes.** [[FIX-943]] landed the standing convention *"any script that bulk-rewrites a table ends by vacuuming what it rewrote"* and named `financial_entities` as **"the one that matters most"**. Nobody was ever assigned to it, and the table has degraded ever since. **Enumerated by mechanism, not by site:** every `public` table over 200 MB (18 tables) joined against `pg_class.relallvisible/relpages`, `pg_stat_user_tables.vacuum_count` and `pg_stat_user_indexes`. Sorting by *whether any script, cron job or procedure ever issues a manual `VACUUM`* separates them completely — `entity_connections` (vacuum_count 6) **98.4%** all-visible, `financial_relationships` (5) **96.8%**, `official_donor_rollup_mv` (1) 100%, `treemap_individuals_rollup` (1) 100%; and with **vacuum_count = 0**: `financial_entities` **53.7%**, `donor_party_rollup_mv` **74.9%**, `external_relationships_review_queue` **65.0%**. `entity_tags` is the one clean exception (97.1% at vacuum_count 0) because it is DELETE-dominated — 14,785,744 deletes clear its trigger constantly (61 autovacuums) — and that exception is what makes the rest a mechanism rather than a coincidence. **The degradation is live and fast.** Two readings the same session, `relpages` **unchanged** at 208,903: relallvisible **145,898 → 112,162** (69.8% → 53.7%) in ~13 minutes, i.e. **33,736 heap pages lost their all-visible mark**, ~2,800 pages/min, while `n_dead_tup` went 140,224 → 164,110. FIX-943 measured this table at **77.3% on 2026-08-01**; it is 53.7% six days later, *after* being tuned. **Why the FIX-943 tuning cannot hold it:** FE is UPDATE-dominated — `n_tup_upd` **3,410,204** vs `n_tup_ins` 739,201 — and its trigger is `50 + 0.05 × 3,630,602` = **181,580** against 164,110 dead, i.e. *under* the line, so autovacuum will not fire. The trigger also **grew 25% as the table grew** (FIX-943 computed 145,784 against 2.9M rows): a table that steps raises its own tolerance for bloat, which is [[FIX-972]]'s stale-quantum defect (playbook D5) applied to autovacuum itself. **Writers with no tail, enumerated from prod `pg_proc`:** 13 live functions mass-UPDATE `financial_entities` (`rebuild_financial_entity_donation_totals`, `rebuild_financial_entity_ie_totals`, `refresh_spending_totals`, `financial_entity_recipient_count_rebuild`, `reconcile_financial_entity_totals`, `rebuild_all_primary_sources`, …) and **none contains `VACUUM`**; `grep -rn "VACUUM" packages/data/src/pipelines/` returns **zero matches**, so no ingest pipeline vacuums anything; the only pg_cron vacuum job, `ec-vacuum-analyze`, covers `entity_connections` alone. **Measured consequence** — the covering index that exists *specifically* to enable index-only scans, `financial_entities_nonindividual_id` = `btree(id) INCLUDE (display_name, entity_type) WHERE entity_type <> 'individual'` (33 MB, ~4,224 pages): `Index Only Scan … actual rows=226640, **Heap Fetches: 108419** (47.8% of rows), Buffers: shared hit=205784 read=50837, Execution Time: **47,042.959 ms**` — **256,621 buffers to return 226,640 rows, 61× the index's own size**, all of the excess heap. Against the tables that DO have vacuum owners, same box same night: EC's `entity_connections_donation_to_official_idx` 26.5% heap fetches, FR's `financial_relationships_donor_rollup_idx` (the [[FIX-974]] path) **4.8%** — a **10× spread tracking vacuum ownership exactly**. **Blast radius:** `financial_entities_pkey` is the **most-scanned index on the instance**, 84,990,056 scans at 80.6% heap-fetched; every donor rollup, graph query, tagger pass and treemap build joins through it. **It is 3.7 points from paging someone** — `check_rebuild_autovacuum_status()` escalates `vm_degraded` at `relpages > 1000 AND pct_all_visible < 50.0`, and FIX-943 called `donor_party_rollup_mv` at 42.7% "the worst table on the instance". **Fix shape:** give the three ownerless tables a vacuum owner the way [[FIX-974]]'s `donor-rollup-bulk.ts` owns its six arms — cheapest first: (a) add `financial_entities` to the FEC/USASpending/LittleSis/IRS990/EDGAR pipeline tails and to `merge-cross-source-fe-collisions.ts`; (b) add a `financial_entities` arm to the monthly `ec-vacuum-analyze` cron (rename it — the job name already lies about its scope); (c) `donor_party_rollup_mv` should be vacuumed by `refresh_donor_party_rollup_incremental`'s driver, which deletes 1,903,409 and inserts 1,883,817 rows per full pass with no tail; (d) `external_relationships_review_queue` (709 MB, `autovacuum_count` **0** — never autovacuumed in its life, no `reloptions` at all, 82,682 inserts since its last vacuum) needs tuning at minimum, and FIX-943 explicitly left it alone as "pipeline-only" when it was smaller. **Methodological note worth keeping:** `relallvisible` is a *lagging* proxy — it is only refreshed by `VACUUM`, so a table dirtied since its last vacuum reads better than it is (`entity_connections` shows 98.4% all-visible yet 26.5% real heap fetches). `Heap Fetches` in `EXPLAIN` is ground truth; `relallvisible` is the early warning. Playbook B1 says watch both — this pass confirms they disagree by a wide margin and in which direction. Measurement conditions: prod, 04:29–04:48 UTC 2026-08-07, box NOT idle (an `INSERT INTO financial_relationships` ran throughout), clear of the nightly and of the 09:00/12:00 firings. Cross-ref [[FIX-943]], [[FIX-884]], [[FIX-974]], [[FIX-885]], [[FIX-650]], [[FIX-589]]. <!--id:FIX-975-->
- [ ] 🟠 S — **Both full-table walks over financial_entities carry sizing comments that are 2.9x and 7.6x stale — 226,640 non-individual rows against a documented ~78k / ~30k** — **Found by the 2026-08-07 site-wide efficiency audit (`docs/audits/2026-08-07-efficiency-audit.md`), class 3 — stale guard quanta.** Enumerated by ranking `pg_stat_statements` by `total_exec_time` over statements touching `financial_entities`, then reading each caller. Two sites walk the whole table, and **both are sized by a code comment that the table has outgrown**. **(1) `packages/data/src/pipelines/tags/rules.ts:1402-1409`** — `selectDirect("SELECT id, display_name, entity_type FROM public.financial_entities WHERE entity_type <> 'individual' ORDER BY id")`, then `for (const entity of entities)` over the whole result. **No `LIMIT`, no chunking** — the entire set is materialised in Node memory. The comment directly above says *"The ~78k PAC/super_pac/party/union/corp/nonprofit/other rows"*; the actual count today is **226,640**, i.e. **2.9× stale**. Measured (`pg_stat_statements`): **19 calls, mean 32,153 ms, 611 s total**, and the `EXPLAIN` in the audit shows where it goes — `Index Only Scan … Heap Fetches: 108419`, 47.0 s (see [[FIX-975]] for why the heap fetches are there). This is the driver of `rule-taggers-daily`/`rule-taggers-weekly`; [[FIX-969]] owns the *symptom* (weekly fails 4 of 5 runs at the 6 h ceiling) and prescribes "chunk the remaining arms" — this bullet supplies the **mechanism and the number**, and is deliberately NOT a second copy of the ceiling bug. **Note the scope is load-bearing correctness, not just cost:** [[FIX-437]] narrowed this to non-individuals because un-scoping it keyword-matches 1.05M individual donors by surname (anyone named "Koch" → oil_gas, "Wells" → finance). Any chunking fix must preserve `entity_type <> 'individual'`. **(2) `packages/data/src/pipelines/littlesis/matcher.ts:143-169`** — a keyset walk, `const CHUNK = 100_000`, building in-memory `personsBySortKey` / `orgsByCanonical` maps over every row. Its comment says *"keyset-chunked to bound memory (~2.45M rows)"* and *"Individuals (~2.37M FEC indiv donors) … Org count is ~30k"*. Actual today: **3,630,602 rows (1.48×)** and **226,640 orgs (7.6×)**. The org map is the one the estimate governs and it is the one that drifted 7.6×; the stated *"~1 GB peak RSS"* bound was sized against 30k orgs. Measured: **89 calls, mean 33,676 ms, 2,997 s total** at 63.3% cache hit — roughly **50 minutes of every LittleSis run** spent building the match index. **Both are the [[FIX-972]] lesson (playbook D5) in its plainest form: record the table size a quantum was tuned against and re-derive when the table steps.** Neither is broken today; both are sized by a number that is no longer true, which is exactly the state the 600-recipient ceiling was in before it bit. **Fix shape:** (a) chunk the rules.ts keyword pass by `id` range (it needs no cross-row context — it is a pure per-row keyword match — so it chunks trivially); (b) re-derive both comments from a live count and add the count's as-of date, per playbook D5; (c) consider whether the LittleSis org map needs the individual rows at all — `personsBySortKey` is built from 3.4M individuals to serve person matching, and its memory bound is the reason `CHUNK` exists. Cross-ref [[FIX-975]], [[FIX-969]], [[FIX-972]], [[FIX-437]], [[FIX-443]], [[FIX-444]]. <!--id:FIX-976-->
- [ ] 🟠 S — **refresh_treemap_individuals_global writes its chunk cursor with NOW() inside the per-chunk COMMIT loop — the byte-identical twin of the FIX-972 defect, in a sibling procedure FIX-972 never enumerated** — **Found by the 2026-08-07 site-wide efficiency audit (`docs/audits/2026-08-07-efficiency-audit.md`), class 5 — lying timestamps; SURVIVED adversarial verification.** This is playbook **E5** in its purest form: [[FIX-972]] diagnosed the defect, fixed the one site it was standing on, and nobody grepped for the mechanism. **The defect, verbatim FIX-972's.** `public.refresh_treemap_individuals_global` (live `prosrc:145-148`) writes its chunk cursor's `updated_at` with `NOW()` from inside the per-chunk `COMMIT` loop. `NOW()` is `transaction_timestamp()`; every chunk runs in its own transaction that BEGINs immediately after the previous chunk's `COMMIT`, so the stamp records when chunk N *started* — **one whole chunk early**. The cursor VALUE is never wrong; only its timestamp. FIX-972's proof was exact — a run's last cursor write read 13:31:23 against a 15:20:29 end, a phantom **1h49m** of apparent stall equal to that run's `slowest_chunk_seconds` (6,546 s) to the second — and it cost a full diagnostic pass to chase. **Measured here:** prod `data_sync_log` run 2026-08-06 02:23:09 → 03:09:28 UTC, `status=complete`, span 2,779 s, `metadata->>'slowest_chunk_seconds' = 59`. So today's lie is bounded at **59 s** — small, because this procedure's chunks are currently fast. That is precisely why it should be fixed now rather than after it bites: [[FIX-965]] chunked this refresh at 64 windows against a 2400 s budget, [[FIX-964]] records it already blowing that budget mid-tail once, and the same procedure appears in [[FIX-969]]'s over-ceiling set at **21,615.9 s**. At a 6 h blowout the cursor's lie grows to one chunk of a six-hour run, which is exactly the magnitude that made FIX-972 expensive. **Related, from the same pass and same mechanism** (filed here rather than separately because the fix is one sweep): three more rollup freshness columns are stamped at chunk-transaction START inside the very loop FIX-972 audited but were left untouched by it — `public.small_dollar_rebuild_officials:15`, `public.sector_affinity_rebuild_officials:47`, and `official_donor_totals.updated_at`'s `DEFAULT now()`, all executed inside `refresh_official_donor_rollup_incremental`'s per-chunk transaction, where the measured `slowest_chunk_seconds` has reached **6,546 s**. **Fix shape:** `clock_timestamp()` at every one of these sites (playbook E7 — `NOW()` for transactional consistency, `clock_timestamp()` for anything read as progress), and then **grep the mechanism rather than the site**: `SELECT proname FROM pg_proc WHERE prosrc ~* '\\mNOW\\s*\\(\\s*\\)' AND prosrc ~* '\\mCOMMIT\\M'` is the enumeration that would have caught all of them in one pass, and should be the standing check when any new chunked writer lands. Cross-ref [[FIX-972]], [[FIX-965]], [[FIX-964]], [[FIX-969]], [[FIX-944]], [[FIX-974]]. <!--id:FIX-981-->
- [ ] 🟠 M — **entity-connection-stats-rebuild sits at 86% of the 6h ceiling with no budget guard, no cursor and no partial status — bimodal 101s..18,568s, never failed, so FIX-969 cannot see it** — **Found by the 2026-08-07 site-wide efficiency audit (`docs/audits/2026-08-07-efficiency-audit.md`); three independent enumeration passes converged on it, and it SURVIVED adversarial verification at 🟠** — the cron-envelope pass (class 4), the live-plpgsql-loop pass (class 1) and the tuned-constant pass (class 3). **It is NOT one of [[FIX-969]]'s seven**, because FIX-969 enumerated by *failure* and this job has **0 failures in 8 retained runs** — it is the job most likely to join that list next, and the clearest instance of the audit's whole premise. **Measured, `cron.job_run_details` by jobname (playbook D3), full retained window:** p95 **17,296 s = 80.1%** of the 21,600 s ceiling; max **18,568 s = 86.0%**; headroom **3,032 s (50.5 min)**. Trail for `0 11 * * 1,3`: 07-13 Mon 7,112 s · 07-15 Wed 136 s · 07-20 Mon 128 s · 07-22 Wed 132 s · 07-27 Mon **18,568 s** · 07-29 Wed 7,095 s · 08-03 Mon **14,933 s** · 08-05 Wed 101 s. **This is bimodal, not trending** — 101–136 s or 7,095–18,568 s, a **184× spread** with no rising curve; Monday mean 10,185 s vs Wednesday mean 1,866 s (**5.5×**). It fires at 11:00, three hours after the `rebuild-ec-incremental*` jobs at 08:00 that FIX-969 shows dying at ~14:00 on the ceiling — so on a blowout day it runs *concurrently with a six-hour squatter*. Note this is a **distinct mechanism from FIX-969's**: those jobs are starved *before they start* (the ~10 s libpq window → `job startup timeout`); this one **starts fine and runs 5.5× slower**. Contention, not starvation. **The structural defect:** the live body of `public.rebuild_entity_connection_stats` (migration `20260710000000_fix717_718_incremental_stats_rollup_tables.sql:477 c_bounds[16]` / `:555 FOR i IN 1..16`) has **no predictive budget guard, no reservation, no chunk cursor and no `partial` status** — verified against the live body: `has_budget=false`, `has_cursor=false`. Its 16-window quantum governs only the cheap *apply* phase; the dominant cost, the **stage build**, is a **single unchunked statement** aggregating `2 × 5,059,971 = 10,119,942` rows (UNION ALL over `entity_connections`). So the 6 h `statement_timeout` is its **only stop**, and per playbook C2 that timer **arms once at CALL start** and is not re-armed by per-chunk `COMMIT`s. When it fires, the entire stage build is discarded and the next run restarts at window 1 — the exact shape [[FIX-944]] and [[FIX-965]] were built to eliminate elsewhere. **Consequence if it crosses:** `entity_connection_stats_mv` is a 2,418,981-row / 470 MB table read on the request path ([[FIX-774]]); a silent 6 h blowout leaves it stale with nothing watching — `check_cron_job_health()` reports blowouts as REPORT-ONLY by design, and the freshness canary's `ROLLUP_PIPELINES` does not enumerate it. **Fix shape:** apply the playbook A4 chunked-writer skeleton — chunk the **stage build** by `to_id`/`from_id` RANGE aligned to the read index (A2, not an entity list), advance a cursor inside each chunk transaction, add the predictive `elapsed + slowest × 1.25 > budget` stop with a session-scoped GUC override and a `partial` status, and abort-not-skip on chunk failure. Take the [[FIX-974]] `donor_rollup_rebuild_bulk` body as the reference implementation rather than re-deriving it. Separately worth deciding: whether the 11:00 Mon/Wed slot should move off the tail of the 08:00 EC rebuild window at all. **Cross-cutting note:** `refresh_contract_flow_rollups` has the same shape (single un-COMMITted transaction, no chunking, no budget) and grew **7.2× in one week** — filed separately. Cross-ref [[FIX-969]], [[FIX-944]], [[FIX-965]], [[FIX-974]], [[FIX-717]], [[FIX-718]], [[FIX-774]], [[FIX-968]]. <!--id:FIX-982-->
- [ ] 🟠 M — **All 68 bulk-read sites paginate by OFFSET via seven independently-written helpers — measured 175x row-visit amplification on the largest walk, and the page quanta are tuned to table sizes that have since grown** — **Found by the 2026-08-07 site-wide efficiency audit (`docs/audits/2026-08-07-efficiency-audit.md`), classes 1 and 3 converging; SURVIVED adversarial verification with its scale claims corrected downward — read the corrections, they matter.** Playbook **A2** (*chunk by key RANGE aligned to the read index, never by ordinal position*) and **D5** (*guard constants are tuned to a table size and go stale when the table steps*). **Enumerated by mechanism:** `grep -rnE "\.range\((from|offset|f|sFrom|page \*|\(page)" apps packages` returns **68 bulk-read sites**, driven by **seven independently-written paging helpers** (`selectAllOrThrow`, two separate `fetchAllPaged` definitions, `fetchAll`, `fetchAllPages`, two separate `fetchAllRows`). **Every one is OFFSET paging.** Counter-check: keyset IS a known pattern in this repo — `littlesis/matcher.ts:137` does it correctly — but it appears in **zero of the 68**. **Why OFFSET is quadratic:** page *k* generates and discards the first *k × pageSize* rows, so a full walk is O(n²/pageSize) against O(n) for keyset. **(1) The stale quantum, verified 🟠 — this is the half with a live consequence.** `packages/data/src/pipelines/tags/rules.ts:675` is `const PAGE_SIZE = 500` and `packages/db/src/read-helpers.ts:69` defaults to 1000, both tuned when the tables were smaller; `rules.ts`'s own comment still says *"proposals is ~73k rows"* against an actual **87,403**. Planner cost for the exact query it issues, measured on prod (plan only, no ANALYZE): page 0 = **129.07**, page 57 = **7,462.20 (57.8×)**, last page = **22,476.08 (174.1×)**. There are **three recorded production statement-timeout failures on deep pages**. **(2) The largest walk, verified but downgraded to 🟡 on frequency.** `packages/data/src/pipelines/littlesis/writer.ts:51` (`preloadKnownLittleSisIds`) reads **348,508** `external_source_refs` rows through **349 OFFSET pages** — `pgss: calls=1045, mean 374.63 ms, total 391.5 s, 289,313 buffers/call`. Row-visit amplification is **175×** (`349 × 350 / 2 × 1000` = **61.08M** filtered row-visits against **348,508** for a keyset walk), and the keyset form already exists one file over. **But the original finding's framing was wrong and is corrected here:** this is **WEEKLY, not nightly** — `pg_stat_statements_info.stats_reset = 2026-07-17`, so 1045 calls / 349 pages = **exactly 3 runs in 21 days**, confirmed against `data_sync_log` (littlesis rows 07-19, 07-26, 08-02). The nightly enrichment job *does* fire, but `runLittleSisPipeline` short-circuits at the SHA256 freshness gate (`littlesis/index.ts:94-98`, `skipSync('dumps_unchanged')`) **before** the preload runs. Real cost: **130.7 s per run, ~18.6 s/day amortised, ~6% of the ~35-min LittleSis stage.** **And it carries no PR 3 growth term** — `external_source_refs` is keyed to imported source records, not to the donor population. **What this finding is and is not.** It is not "the pipelines are burning hours on OFFSET" — that claim was tested and died. It is: a repo-wide pattern with **one measured live failure mode** (deep-page timeouts on `proposals`, already observed three times) and a **175× structural inefficiency** on the largest walk, sitting behind **seven duplicated helpers** so any future large table inherits it by default. The cost of fixing is small and the cost of not fixing grows with whichever table crosses the threshold next. **Fix shape:** (a) convert the seven helpers to keyset (`WHERE id > $last ORDER BY id LIMIT n`) — one change at the helper layer covers most of the 68 sites, which is the mechanism-level fix rather than site-by-site; behaviour-preserving only where the `ORDER BY` column is unique and non-null (`id` is — audit any caller ordering by something else); (b) re-derive the two page quanta and record the table size they were tuned against, per D5; (c) `littlesis/writer.ts:51` is the cheapest single win and its keyset twin already exists in the sibling module. **Explicitly refuted and NOT part of this bullet:** `tags/ai-classifier.ts:241-251` — its paged subset is far smaller than claimed and does not justify a finding. Cross-ref [[FIX-974]] (A2, the 33.0 → 0.118 ms/row unlock), [[FIX-976]], [[FIX-443]], [[FIX-444]], [[FIX-902]]. <!--id:FIX-984-->
- [x] 🟠 S — **homepage_stats_mv is the only MV in the daily refresh list without a unique index, so it is the only one refreshed non-CONCURRENTLY — holding ACCESS EXCLUSIVE on a homepage-read MV, and it is the exact object of the 0.7s-local / 22-min-prod receipt** — **Found by the 2026-08-07 site-wide efficiency audit (`docs/audits/2026-08-07-efficiency-audit.md`), class 1 (live plpgsql bodies); SURVIVED adversarial verification, which found the playbook receipt binds tighter than the finding claimed.** **The defect.** Of the **13** `relkind='m'` relations in `public`, `homepage_stats_mv` is the **only one** with `indisunique = false`. The other **7 of 7** MVs in `refresh_derived_mvs('daily')`'s unit array — `proposal_trending_24h`, `proposal_popularity_24h`, `official_homepage_stats_mv`, `entity_engagement_rollup_mv`, `homepage_agency_counts_mv`, `commons_active_threads`, `pipeline_runtime_stats_mv` — all carry a unique index and are all refreshed **CONCURRENTLY** in the live procedure body. Unit 3 alone is not: `refresh_homepage_stats_mv()`'s prosrc is literally `REFRESH MATERIALIZED VIEW public.homepage_stats_mv`. **`REFRESH ... CONCURRENTLY` requires a unique index**, so the missing index is the *cause* and the `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` lock is the *consequence* — for the whole duration of the refresh, on a materialized view the **homepage reads**. **Why this is not hypothetical.** This exact object is the receipt behind playbook **C4** (*"runtime measurements are floors — design to survive the miss"*): `refresh_homepage_stats_mv()` takes **0.7 s on local** and ran **22 minutes** against an otherwise-idle prod on 2026-07-31, pushing the homepage to **18.5 s**. That incident is already on record; what this audit adds is **why it blocked** rather than merely ran long — a `CONCURRENTLY` refresh of the same view would have held no exclusive lock and the homepage would have kept serving the previous snapshot. The containing job is not small either: jobid 9 has 34 retained runs with a slowest of **3h29m06.623s** (2026-08-05, whole 13-unit job) against a typical 5–20 minutes. **The fix is unusually cheap for the risk it removes.** `homepage_stats_mv` is a **single row, 8,192 bytes** — a unique index on it costs nothing, and adding one lets the refresh become `CONCURRENTLY` like its seven siblings. That is a one-line migration plus a one-word change in the procedure body. **Sequence:** add the unique index first (it must exist before `CONCURRENTLY` will run), then flip the refresh; verify the homepage stays served during a refresh rather than blocking. Note `CONCURRENTLY` trades the lock for a longer refresh and needs the MV to be populated, so confirm the first post-change run on local before prod. Cross-ref playbook **C4**, [[FIX-933]], [[FIX-943]], [[FIX-969]], [[FIX-683]]. <!--id:FIX-986-->
- [ ] 🟡 S — **refresh_agency_staffing_rollup computes no dirty set — it re-aggregates 3.8M index entries / 575 MB every week to produce 128 rows / 72 kB, at up to 19m43s** — **Found by the 2026-08-07 site-wide efficiency audit (`docs/audits/2026-08-07-efficiency-audit.md`), class 1 (live plpgsql bodies); SURVIVED adversarial verification with its normalisation corrected — read the correction, it is the interesting part.** **The shape.** `public.refresh_agency_staffing_rollup` (pg_cron jobid 25, `agency-staffing-rollup-refresh`, Tue 07:30 UTC) has **no dirty set and no watermark**. Every run re-aggregates the full agency contract/grant slice — **3,822,690 index entries / 575 MB** on `financial_relationships_agency_spend_lateral` — to produce an output table of **128 rows / 72 kB**, against `agencies` = **129**. Measured runtimes from `cron.job_run_details`: **00:19:43.262714**, 00:09:06.698344, 00:03:16.057629 (median 9m06.70s). **The correction that matters (playbook E4 — normalise before naming a cause).** The finding originally framed this as *"9.24 s per output row"*, which is a rhetorically strong but analytically wrong unit: the work is not per output row, it is per **input** row. Correctly normalised the worst run is **0.31 ms per input FR row**, which is an entirely reasonable per-row cost — *the job is not slow, it is doing too much work*. That distinction decides the fix: there is no plan pathology to chase and no index to add; the only lever is **reading fewer rows**. **Why it is 🟡 rather than higher:** at ~9–20 minutes weekly on a job with no downstream dependants in its window, the direct cost is modest. What earns it a bullet is the **growth term** — the input is the agency contract/grant slice of `financial_relationships`, which PR 3 multiplies, while the output is pinned at 128 rows by the number of agencies. So the ratio of work-done to work-needed gets strictly worse with every ingest, and this is the clearest instance in the audit of playbook **E8** (*bound derived-data cost by PRODUCT semantics, not source row counts*) pointing at the **input** side rather than the stored side. **Fix shape:** (a) give it a watermark like its siblings — `financial_relationships.updated_at` is already indexed and already drives the incremental rollups (note [[FIX-983]] first: those watermarks have a correctness defect of their own, so fix that before copying the pattern); (b) or, since the output is only 128 rows keyed on `from_id` over 129 agencies, recompute only agencies whose spend rows changed, which is a small dirty set by construction; (c) either way record the input size the design was sized against, per playbook **D5**. Cross-ref [[FIX-778]], [[FIX-983]], [[FIX-974]], [[FIX-969]], [[FIX-838]]. <!--id:FIX-987-->
- [ ] 🟡 S — **The nominations pass looks each nominee up on an unindexed jsonb expression — officials carries 15 indexes and not one covers source_ids, so every nominee costs a sequential scan of the 37k-row table** — **Found by the 2026-08-07 site-wide efficiency audit (`docs/audits/2026-08-07-efficiency-audit.md`), class 1 (TypeScript pipeline loops, population 230 loop sites over 645 files); SURVIVED adversarial verification with the cost corrected down by roughly an order of magnitude.** **The shape.** `packages/data/src/pipelines/agency-leadership/index.ts:323-340` looks each nominee up one at a time on `source_ids->>'congress_nomination_id'`. Verified on prod: `officials` carries **15 indexes** and **not one** is on `source_ids` or any expression over it (`idx_officials_fts`, `idx_officials_name_trgm`, `officials_current_term_end`, `officials_governing_body_id`, `officials_is_active`, `officials_is_synthetic_idx`, `officials_is_up_for_election`, `officials_jurisdiction_id`, …). So every probe is a **sequential scan of all 37,018 rows / 20 MB**. The same file demonstrates the correct pattern — a bulk pre-fetch into a Map — **130 lines earlier**, which is what makes this a real oversight rather than a design choice. **Corrected cost (playbook E3 — re-derive before quoting).** The finding originally implied minutes per run. Measured from prod `pg_stat_statements` over 2026-07-17 → 08-07: the seq-scan shape ran **325 calls / mean 87.39 ms / 28.40 s total**, and that total is **shared** with `executive/seed.ts` (~42 calls). The agency-leadership share is roughly **94 calls per run ≈ 8.2 s per weekly run** — real, but small. The `ilike` fallback the finding also flagged is **not** a second full scan: it is trigram-index-served at **1.43 ms / 45 buffers**. **Why it is still worth a bullet at 🟡:** it is a one-line fix (an expression index on `(source_ids->>'congress_nomination_id')`, or the bulk pre-fetch the file already uses elsewhere), and `officials` is a table whose scan cost grows with every candidate ingest while the nominee count grows independently — so the product of the two is the term that moves. It is filed at its **measured** size, not its imagined one, deliberately: the audit's dominant failure mode was overstating reach, and this bullet is calibrated to 8.2 s/week rather than to the shape. **Fix shape:** add the expression index (cheapest, and it also serves any other `source_ids` lookup — check whether the [[FIX-761]] promotion surface wants the same), or pre-fetch the nomination-id → official map once per run the way the same file already pre-fetches its other lookups. Cross-ref [[FIX-761]], [[FIX-246]], [[FIX-984]]. <!--id:FIX-988-->
- [ ] 🟡 S — **Small high-churn relations never reach the default autovacuum threshold — six sit at 20-100% dead** — Generalization of the tiny-relation half of [[FIX-1003]]. The autovacuum trigger is `threshold + scale_factor x reltuples`. On a large table the default threshold (50) is rounding error and only the scale factor is a lever — but on a relation of a few dozen rows the scale-factor term is ~1-5 and the THRESHOLD is effectively the entire trigger, so a small, frequently-rewritten relation can sit indefinitely just under it. Measured on prod 2026-08-09, every public relation with no autovacuum override, ranked by dead ratio: `proposal_popularity_24h` 100% (0 live / 6 dead), `supabase_prometheus_state` 75.0%, `platform_alert_state` 70.3%, `pipeline_runtime_stats_mv` 47.2%, `platform_usage` 29.6%, `platform_limits` 22.6% — several at 0.0% all-visible. [[FIX-1003]] fixed five instances of this class (four `chord_*_flows_mv` and `pipeline_state`) by setting `autovacuum_vacuum_threshold = 20` alongside the scale factor, and prod confirmed the lever within seconds: all five went from 24.9-62.0% dead / 0.0% all-visible to 0 dead / 100% all-visible at 2026-08-09 14:36:42, on the first autovacuum evaluation after the override landed. The rest of the class is untreated. Honest scoping: the direct cost of bloat on a 1-5 page relation is near zero, so this is a hygiene/consistency item, not a performance one — the value is that these are the relations where the [[FIX-884]] all-visible mechanism is invisible in monitoring precisely because the absolute numbers look harmless. Fix shape: a threshold-led override on the enumerated set, no new framework and no per-table cron jobs (autovacuum genuinely suffices once the trigger is reachable). Deliberately NOT a vacuum registry or dashboard — see [[FIX-1003]]'s scope note. Cross-ref [[FIX-943]], [[FIX-884]]. <!--id:FIX-1006-->
- [x] 🔴 M — **donor_rollup_refresh completed 2 of 2,889 recipients in its full 2h budget — ~2,887 officials carry stale official_donor_totals** — Measured on prod 2026-08-10: the 12:00 UTC `donor_rollup_refresh` firing ran the whole [[FIX-1002]] 2-hour budget (12:00:03 → 14:36:04) and logged status `partial` with error_message 'budget exhausted — resumable at recipient **2 of 2889** (cursor 003a1690-a…)'. Two recipients. The 09:00 firing did not log at all. Consequence: `official_donor_totals` is stale for essentially every official the sweep never reached — surfaced incidentally while verifying [[FIX-953]], where Lucy McBath (NOT in that manifest, untouched by the merge) reconciled at $776,207,400 from `financial_relationships` against $770,121,900 in `official_donor_totals`, a $60,855 gap, while the three officials the merge explicitly rebuilt via `donor_rollup_rebuild_recipients(affected)` reconciled to the cent. So the rollup's own scheduled path is not keeping up while the targeted path works fine, which points at per-recipient cost rather than at the cron plumbing. This is a materially worse symptom than [[FIX-1003]]'s dead-tuple story predicts (a healthy post-[[FIX-1002]]/[[FIX-1003]] sweep was ~24 minutes for the whole set on 2026-08-09 09:00, and the 2026-08-09 12:00 firing took 137.2 min but COMPLETED) — something regressed hard between 2026-08-09 and 2026-08-10. Start by re-reading `pg_stat_user_tables.last_vacuum` on `financial_relationships` and its six write-target arms per [[FIX-1003]], then EXPLAIN one recipient's rebuild. Note the same window also shows `entity_connections_rebuild` stranded (started 08:00, still 'running' with zero backends 17h later, see [[FIX-990]]) and a burst of pg_cron 'job startup timeout' failures 14:05–14:18, so treat this as one instance-wide episode rather than three unrelated faults. Cross-ref [[FIX-1002]], [[FIX-1003]], [[FIX-990]], [[FIX-944]], [[FIX-953]]. <!--id:FIX-1018-->
- [ ] 🟠 L — **Own-seat double-claim backlog: 117 elected/candidate pairs share one CAND_ID and BOTH hold money ($815M on the stubs)** — Surfaced by the [[FIX-953]] census (docs/audits/2026-08-10-fix953-own-seat-manifest.md + .tsv, 122 rows). Platform-wide there are **146** (elected survivor, candidate stub) pairs where the stub's `fec_candidate_id` describes the seat the elected official actually occupies — same surname, same office, same state, and for House the same DISTRICT digits. They split three ways: **class A double-claim = 117 pairs / 80,546 stub rows / $815,433,444 all-types ($209,691,577 donation)**, class B orphan-survivor = 5 (which [[FIX-953]] merged 3 of), class C survivor-holds-a-DIFFERENT-id = 24 (excluded → [[FIX-956]]). Class A is exactly the population [[FIX-933]]'s step 0 deliberately REFUSES, because its predicate only auto-retires a duplicate claim when the duplicate holds NO money: measured today the refused set is 151 pairs / $926,589,966, and it decomposes as **117 (pass the own-seat gate) + 34 (fail it: 29 Rep-vs-Rep office/district mismatch, 4 Senator-vs-Rep, 1 Senator-vs-Senator)** — exactly 151, so the two censuses reconcile. Class A's evidence is STRONGER than class B's and does not depend on the seat gate at all: a CAND_ID names exactly one FEC candidate, the stub was minted FROM that CAND_ID by the cn{yy} stage, and the survivor's identical binding came from the [[FIX-952]] authoritative bioguide backfill, so neither claim passed through a surname; corroborating, all 117 also agree on the 3-letter first-name key and all 117 have non-zero donor-cycle overlap. The machinery already exists — `pnpm --filter @civitics/data data:merge:official-dupes:own-seat --own-seat-class=double` selects precisely this set and the decision-5 attachment gate refuses any stub carrying non-money records. It was deliberately NOT run under [[FIX-953]] because it is ~200x that FIX's stated scope and would visibly move very large numbers on 117 sitting members' pages (e.g. Raphael Warnock $177.7M → ~$406M), so it needs its own sign-off and its own prod window. Do NOT run the tail during active hours — see [[FIX-1017]]. Cross-ref [[FIX-953]], [[FIX-933]], [[FIX-952]], [[FIX-956]], [[FIX-1017]]. <!--id:FIX-1019-->
- [x] 🟠 M — **MV-refresh loops need a between-refresh degradation breaker** — Neither MV-refresh loop on the platform can stop itself when the instance degrades mid-loop, so both keep issuing the heaviest I/O on the box precisely when the box is least able to serve it. Two loops, same shape: (a) `refresh_derived_mvs('daily')` (pg_cron jobid 9, 06:00 UTC) and (b) `runMvsAndVacuum()` in merge-same-person-official-dupes.ts. Receipts for (a), same six-ish MV set, same nightly-fed data, measured from cron.job_run_details: 2026-08-05 12,546.6s (3h29m), 08-07 4,032.6s (1h07m), 08-08 7,373.6s (2h03m), 08-09 353.8s (5.9 min), 08-10 816.7s (13.6 min) — a 35x spread on identical work, and the 3h29m firing ran straight through, refresh after refresh, with nothing evaluating whether continuing was sane. Receipt for (b), the [[FIX-953]] prod apply 2026-08-10: refresh_official_sector_dollars_mv 119.6s, refresh_official_homepage_stats_mv 326.2s, refresh_homepage_stats_mv 122.1s, refresh_chord_industry_flows_mv 205.1s — 25-70x their local timings (4.6s/4.7s/0.8s/20.3s) — and prod went fully unresponsive (pooler ECHECKOUTTIMEOUT, Cloudflare 522 for 30+ min, manual project reset at 01:08:19 UTC per pg_postmaster_start_time()). Fix shape: store a per-MV healthy baseline duration (a new pipeline_state key, or a small table seeded from a healthy run), and in BOTH loops compare each completed refresh against its baseline; if one runs more than N x baseline (start at N=5), STOP the remaining refreshes, log which ones were skipped and why, and return a degraded status. Per playbook C3 the check can only sit BETWEEN refreshes — a single REFRESH MATERIALIZED VIEW cannot be budget-guarded mid-statement — which is exactly why the between-refresh point must be used rather than left empty. Per playbook C8 the breaker must PROPAGATE: (b)'s caller already skips its tail on abort after [[FIX-1017]], and (a) must mark its data_sync_log row 'partial' rather than 'complete'. Skipping is cheap here for the same reason [[FIX-1017]] gives — these MVs are re-refreshed on the next daily firing — but note the VACUUM tail in (b) is NOT covered by another owner, so a breaker that skips must still name it outstanding. Cross-ref [[FIX-1017]], [[FIX-953]], [[FIX-1018]], [[FIX-943]]. <!--id:FIX-1021-->
- [x] 🟠 M — **official_donor_rollup_mv holds 438k rows for 8,369 NON-official recipients that FIX-1018 freezes (no longer refreshed, never deleted)** — Surfaced while implementing [[FIX-1018]]. Arm 1 (`official_donor_rollup_mv`) is the one rollup arm written for **every** recipient rather than only officials — neither regime filters it: `donor_rollup_rebuild_recipients()` deletes/inserts on `official_id = ANY(p_recipients)` with no `to_type` predicate, and `donor_rollup_rebuild_bulk()`'s arm-1 DELETE/INSERT pair omits the `is_official` filter that arms 2-6 all carry. Measured on prod 2026-08-11: `official_donor_rollup_mv` = **1,004,949 rows, of which 438,094 across 8,369 ids that are not in `officials`** — 6,047 `pac`, 1,904 `super_pac`, 415 `party_committee`, 3 `other`. The other five arms hold **zero** non-official ids (official_donor_totals 6,778/0, official_small_dollar_rollup 6,778/0, official_sector_affinity_rollup 26,636/0, official_donor_bracket_totals 16,388/0, treemap_individuals_rollup 928,064/0), so this is arm 1 alone. This is a deliberate [[FIX-704]] property, documented at apps/civitics/app/api/graph/connections/route.ts:392 ('the rollup keys recipients of BOTH kinds (officials and super-PAC financial_entities)'). **What FIX-1018 changed:** it added `AND fr.to_type = 'official'` to every dirty-set enumeration site, so those 8,369 recipients are never enumerated again. Their 438k rows are therefore **no longer refreshed — and not deleted either**, because `reconcile_donor_rollup_orphans()` only removes recipients with NO surviving qualifying FR row at all. They freeze at their 2026-08-08 values and drift silently from here. **Why it was accepted rather than fixed inline:** the prod audit's premise was 'the excluded work was wasted, not wrong', which holds for arms 2-6 but not for arm 1; correcting it needs a behaviour decision, not a predicate. The request path does not appear to read the frozen rows — `/api/graph/connections` gates the rollup behind `isOfficialFocus` (route.ts:472) and falls back to a capped raw `entity_connections` read for a non-official focus (route.ts:567), and `/api/graph/treemap-pac` entityId mode keys on an official — but `/api/graph/treemap` entity mode does NOT probe `officials` before reading `official_donor_rollup_mv` by `entityId`, so a PAC-focused call there would serve frozen data. **Decide one of:** (a) DELETE the 438,094 non-official rows and accept that a financial-entity focus always takes the raw fallback — simplest, and matches what every other arm already does; (b) keep maintaining them by giving arm 1 its own broader dirty-set enumeration, separate from the official-scoped one — restores today's behaviour at the cost of re-introducing the PAC enumeration FIX-1018 removed, so it must not share the incremental procedure's dirty set; (c) confirm nothing reads them and delete, treating (a) as the answer. Whichever is chosen, add the corresponding assertion to `donor_rollup_bulk_assert_invariants()` ([[FIX-974]]) so the two regimes cannot diverge silently again — that function exists for exactly this class of bug. **⚠ RE-SCOPED 2026-08-12 (cc-prompt-58, docs correction — no code change, execution NOT performed).** The 2026-08-11 recon downgraded this from urgent to deferred and the framing above overstates the harm. **(1) The choice is stale-vs-empty, not correct-vs-wrong.** Reader census, re-verified 2026-08-12: `/api/graph/treemap` entity mode reads `official_donor_rollup_mv` via `.eq("official_id", validEntityId)` at route.ts:119 **without probing `officials` first**, so a PAC id passed there would indeed be served frozen rows — but no caller supplies one (PAC subjects go to `/api/graph/treemap-pac`, whose `entityId` mode keys on an official, and `/api/graph/connections` gates the rollup behind `isOfficialFocus` at route.ts:472 with a raw `entity_connections` fallback at route.ts:567). So **there is no confirmed reader of the 438,094 frozen rows**, and if they were deleted that same hypothetical call returns EMPTY rather than STALE — from the route's perspective the two states are indistinguishable. **(2) The 08-16 deadline pressure is gone.** It came from treating frozen rows as actively-wrong output; they are unread output. **(3) Recommendation stands at option (a)** — DELETE the 438,094 non-official rows, accept that a financial-entity focus always takes the raw fallback (which is what every other arm already does), and add the matching assertion to `donor_rollup_bulk_assert_invariants()` ([[FIX-974]]) so the two regimes cannot diverge silently again. **(4) Execution deliberately DEFERRED pending Craig's sign-off** — it is a 438k-row delete on a live MV and the FIX-943 vacuum tail applies, so it wants a supervised off-peak window, not an autonomous one. **⏳ PROGRESS 2026-08-13 (cc-prompt-60): the DURABLE half shipped, the DELETE did NOT — FIX STAYS OPEN.** Migration `20260813020000_fix1023_arm_official_only_invariant.sql` extends `donor_rollup_bulk_assert_invariants()` ([[FIX-974]]) with a per-arm no-non-official assertion covering **all six** arms, so the incremental and bulk regimes cannot diverge silently again. The 438k-row prod DELETE was deliberately NOT executed: the supervised [[FIX-1021]] firing that shared this window ended with a **prod postgres restart at 01:45:27 UTC** (see the restart FIX), and a 438k-row delete plus its [[FIX-943]] vacuum tail on a just-restarted cold-cache box is not a supervised-window operation — it is now the first item for the next off-peak session. **Consequence to know before then: the bulk regime is GATED.** The assertion is live on prod and prod still holds the non-official rows, so `donor_rollup_rebuild_bulk()` will now REFUSE with the FIX-1023 message until the delete runs. That is the intended behaviour and it is safe — no scheduled job calls the bulk regime (verified prod 2026-08-13: cron 24 = `refresh_official_donor_rollup_incremental`, cron 15 = `reconcile_donor_rollup_orphans`, cron 32 = a vacuum; nothing CALLs the bulk procedure) — but a manual break-glass bulk run will hit it. **Two premises corrected by measurement.** (1) **The bullet's 'treemap_individuals_rollup 928,064/0' is wrong.** That arm keys on `scope_id`, not `official_id`, and carries a legitimate GLOBAL scope row-set under the all-zeros sentinel uuid — 3,034 rows on prod, maintained by `refresh_treemap_individuals_global()` / cron 26. The assertion therefore exempts the all-zeros uuid **by value** rather than skipping the arm, so the one arm with a non-official-keyed scope still gets guarded. An assertion written from the bullet as stated would have fired on every bulk run. (2) The delete was rehearsed end-to-end on the local prod clone: pre 1,018,455 rows / 442,421 non-official / 8,483 ids → `DELETE 442421` (1.7 s) → `VACUUM (ANALYZE)` (0.7 s) → post 576,034 / 0 → assertion passes. Local's counts run slightly above the 2026-08-11 prod measurement (438,094 / 8,369), so **re-count live on prod before deleting** rather than reusing either figure. Note the assertion costs ~32 s (six anti-join scans) — fine as a pre-flight for a multi-hour bulk rebuild, but it is not free. Cross-ref [[FIX-1018]], [[FIX-974]], [[FIX-704]], [[FIX-943]], [[FIX-1021]]. <!--id:FIX-1023-->
- [x] 🟠 S — **The burn-rate RPC scanned every snapshot payload — 427ms and ~12k buffer blocks per tick** — Self-inflicted by [[FIX-1044]] and caught within hours because Craig noticed a Supabase spike and asked. **MEASURED ON PROD from pg_stat_statements 2026-08-16:** get_platform_daily_cost_deltas — calls 9, mean 427.4 ms, max 966.7 ms, 109,087 buffer blocks total, i.e. **~12,000 blocks (~95 MB of buffer traffic) PER CALL** — against 115 calls of everything else touching that table at 18.1 ms mean. It runs on EVERY snapshot tick, forever. **THE CAUSE:** the function read every platform_usage_snapshot row in a 14-day window (measured: 276 rows, 4.6 MB of payload, 17 kB average, so nearly all TOASTed) and ran jsonb_array_elements over the metrics array of each one, purely to take max() per MTD day at the end. On an instance with 256 MB shared_buffers and a ~54 pct cache hit rate ([[project_prod_buffer_churn_baseline]]) that is exactly the churn packages/db/CLAUDE.md warns about under "Cost note: profile the COMPUTE, not just the read" — the read path was considered and the cron-side compute was not. **THE FIX:** Vercel counters are cumulative and step ONCE PER DAY, and consecutive snapshots inside a day are byte-identical to 16 decimal places (established by the 2026-08-15 audit). So ~19 of every 20 rows carry information already present in another, and the whole series is recoverable from a couple of rows per Vercel billing day. Bucket by Pacific day using ONLY fetched_at — no payload is detoasted to make the selection — take the first and last row of each bucket via DISTINCT ON, and only THEN do the jsonb extraction, on ~28 rows instead of ~276. Same output; if anything more correct than max(), since the last snapshot of a day is the settled end-of-day value whereas max() would have preferred a larger mid-day reading had one occurred. **WHY BOTH ENDS AND NOT JUST THE LAST:** insurance against boundary jitter, not a fix for an observed skip. The audit dates the step at "~06:00-07:00 UTC" and the bucket boundary is Pacific midnight = 07:00 UTC. Measured on the clone the step lands cleanly (last row with an old window_days is 03:45-06:29 UTC, first with the new one is 07:07-09:32), but a step at 06:00 — the early end of the audit own range — would put a row bearing the NEW window_days into the OLD bucket where, being last, it would become that bucket sole representative and silently drop a whole MTD day. Bracketing costs one extra detoast per day and removes the failure mode. **METHOD NOTE worth keeping:** during this fix MTD day 1 appeared to vanish from the local series and was briefly misdiagnosed as bucket masking. The real cause was the rolling 14-day window moving past it in the two hours between runs. Verified by widening p_days to 16, which returned days 28-31 and 2-9 all consecutive — no skip. Check the window before blaming the query. Cross-ref [[FIX-1044]], [[FIX-281]]. <!--id:FIX-1049-->

## GENERAL / CROSS-CUTTING

- [x] 🟡 S — **Missing set_updated_at trigger on public.financial_entities (lost in shadow→public promotion)** — `public.financial_entities` has **no `updated_at` trigger** on prod. Migration 0004 created `CREATE TRIGGER financial_entities_updated_at BEFORE UPDATE ON financial_entities ... EXECUTE FUNCTION set_updated_at()`, but the shadow→public promotion (migration 20260422000000) promoted/recreated `public.financial_entities` WITHOUT re-creating the trigger. Confirmed on prod 2026-06-30: `pg_trigger` shows zero non-internal triggers on `public.financial_entities`. `set_updated_at()` is unconditional (`NEW.updated_at = NOW()`), so with the trigger gone, `updated_at` is set only on INSERT (column DEFAULT NOW()) and is **never bumped on UPDATE** — it has been stale on every updated row since the 2026-04-22 cutover. The `financial_entities_updated_at` index consequently points at insert-time, not last-modified, so any freshness/recency consumer of `financial_entities.updated_at` is wrong. **Scope (verified prod 2026-06-30):** only `financial_entities` lost the trigger — `financial_relationships`, `votes`, `proposals`, `officials`, `agencies`, `career_history` all RETAIN their `set_updated_at` trigger, so the incremental `entity_connections` rebuild (keys on `financial_relationships.updated_at` / `votes.updated_at` dirty sets) is NOT affected (the worst case checked for). `entity_connections` also lacks the trigger but is TRUNCATE-rebuilt wholesale, so `updated_at` is cosmetic there. **How found:** FIX-677 backfill monitoring — the donor-entity upsert was mostly UPDATEs yet `count(updated_at >= run_start)` tracked only `n_tup_ins` (~37k) while `n_tup_upd` was ~1.5M. **Fix:** migration recreating `CREATE TRIGGER financial_entities_updated_at BEFORE UPDATE ON public.financial_entities FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION public.set_updated_at();` (optionally also on `public.entity_connections`); apply local + prod; audit promotion migration 20260422000000 for any other dropped triggers. Optional one-time `updated_at` backfill is low priority. Cross-ref [[FIX-677]]. <!--id:FIX-699-->

Adds public.abuse_events: append-only log of authed content WRITES (position_set, statement_create, statement_vote, comment_create, flag_create, investigation_create, evidence_write), auth-callback landings, the four 53400 cap-hit rejections, and Turnstile challenge outcomes on the first-write routes ([[FIX-569]]). - [ ] 🟢 M — **Sybil ↔ brigade corroboration join (data-gated)** — PR3 follow-up to [[FIX-571]] (Sybil detection layer). Cross-reference the two shadow candidate logs: flag `brigade_candidates` clusters whose account sets intersect `sybil_candidates` clusters (a coordinated pile-on that is ALSO shared-infrastructure-linked is a materially stronger review signal than either alone).
