- [x] 🟠 S — **fixes-sync drops stacked Fixes:/Closes: trailer lines** — scripts/fixes-sync.mjs parseCommitTrailers matches Fixes:/Closes: with /m but not /g, so body.match() keeps only the FIRST trailer line and any stacked Fixes: lines silently drop their IDs. The 2026-07-20 lens-family commit ccd0f3f3 carried Fixes: FIX-656 / FIX-657 / FIX-658 on three separate lines — only FIX-656 reached done.log; 657/658 had to be logged retroactively. Fix: collect ALL Fixes:/Closes: lines and union the IDs, preserving comma-splitting and the Verified[FIX-NNN] bracketed override. Sibling of [[FIX-465]] (case-sensitive trailer-shadow fix) and [[FIX-369]] (per-FIX Verified). <!--id:FIX-874-->
- [x] 🟢 S — **session:worktree teardown: backoff retry loop for Windows node_modules EPERM** — Windows worktree teardown (session:worktree:done) intermittently EPERM-stranded the worktree dir: the AV (Bitdefender/Defender) + indexer hold a transient lock on freshly-hardlinked node_modules, and the FIX-480 single 2s retry was too short (the [[FIX-841]] teardown stranded the dir). Fix: retry git worktree remove with a short backoff (2+4+8+10+10s, each clamped to sleepSync 10s ceiling) before the contained fs.rm fallback. Durable complement: an AV folder exclusion for ../civitics-worktrees. See [[FIX-480]]. <!--id:FIX-876-->
- [x] 🟡 S — **session:worktree:done strands merged branch when the dir removal loses to a Windows lock** — When `git worktree remove` + the FIX-876 backoff loop + the contained `fs.rm` fallback all lose to a transient AV/indexer lock on the freshly-hardlinked node_modules, `done()` used to `die()` inside the fs.rm catch — BEFORE the branch-deletion block. Result: a fully-MERGED `feature/fix-*` branch pointer was left behind alongside the stranded dir (observed on fix-447), and the failure message only said 'fs.rm fallback failed … left intact' without telling the operator the git side could still be finished or that a re-run clears it. Fix (this FIX): branch deletion now runs regardless of the dir outcome — a branch confirmed an ancestor of origin/main is force-deleted (`-D`, a stronger guarantee than `-d`'s local-only merge check which wrongly refuses when the primary checkout's main is behind origin/main); the registry is pruned; and on a stranded dir the script prints a clear 'DIR is LOCKED / git side is otherwise clean / re-run `session:worktree:done <id>` once the lock clears or delete <path> manually' message and exits non-zero. Directory deletion itself stays gated on the removal actually succeeding (no fake success). Backoff retry was already added by [[FIX-876]]; this closes the residual half-succeed + orphaned-merged-branch gap. <!--id:FIX-877-->
- [x] 🟡 S — **session:worktree:done recovery re-run fails-closed on a de-registered orphan dir** — Follow-up to [[FIX-877]], surfaced by its own live teardown test. When the first teardown strands the dir on a Windows lock, git has ALREADY de-registered the worktree (git worktree remove succeeds at the admin level before the fs.rm loses to the lock), so the dir is a de-registered ORPHAN still on disk. The FIX-877 stranded-dir message says 're-run pnpm session:worktree:done <id>' — but that plain re-run FAILS: done()'s porcelain safety check runs 'git -C <dir> status --porcelain', which errors 'not a working tree' on the orphan, so cleanIgnoringArtifacts is false and it refuses without --force ('could not read git status / refusing to remove a dirty worktree'). The working recovery is --force (per the FIX-876 memo), which the message did NOT mention. Also, on that path the backoff loop wastes ~34s pointlessly retrying 'git worktree remove' against a permanent 'not a working tree' error. Fix: detect the de-registered-orphan case (path absent from 'git worktree list') and, for it, skip the git-worktree-remove retries and go straight to the containment-guarded fs.rm (no --force needed) — making the plain recovery re-run in FIX-877's message actually work. Confirmed live: the FIX-877 teardown of fix-sweep-0722 stranded, plain re-run refused, --force succeeded. <!--id:FIX-879-->
- [x] 🟢 S — **Retire get_official_donors(uuid) — DROP after FIX-835 revoke + FIX-875 correction** — DROP FUNCTION public.get_official_donors(uuid). Zero callers (FIX-835 audit: no .rpc/cron/proc/direct-pg/view/RLS refs; only generated database.ts). anon/authenticated EXECUTE revoked 20260714010000; corrected + flagged retirement candidate in 20260722010000. Soak gate lifted by Craig 2026-07-24. Cross-ref [[FIX-835]] [[FIX-875]]. <!--id:FIX-882-->
- [x] 🟠 S — **Proposal complexity tags mis-filed as topics — re-categorize to quality** — 38% of proposal topic rows are not topics. The drain path wrote the complexity classification (`technical`/`accessible`) into `tag_category='topic'`; on prod they are the #1 and #2 'topics' by volume (2,288 + 2,273 = 4,561 of 12,104 topic rows), ahead of every real topic. Consequence: `/api/graph/tag-groups` offers 'Technical' and 'Accessible' as topic groups, and any consumer of proposal topics gets a plurality of non-topics — including a future per-official issue-area rollup, where the #1 'focus area' for a US Senator computes as *accessible, 25%*. Fix is re-categorization, never deletion: move the affected rows to `tag_category='quality'`, already a first-class rendered category in `EntityTags.tsx` (CATEGORY_STYLES + CATEGORY_ORDER position 6), so no UI change is needed. Idempotent script with an explicit `--allow-prod` gate and a BEGIN/ROLLBACK dry-run mode. <!--id:FIX-889-->
- [x] 🟠 M — **Drain tag producer: per-tag tag_category + write-boundary vocabulary guard** — Root cause behind [[FIX-889]], and the reason it would recur. Two producers disagree: `pipelines/tags/ai-tagger.ts` writes the complexity tag correctly with `tag_category: 'quality'`, while the drain path derives ONE category for the whole batch from the entity type (`apply.ts`: `entity_type === 'financial_entity' ? 'industry' : 'topic'`) and stamps it on every tag. That shape makes it structurally impossible for a drain batch to emit two categories, so the bug returns the moment anyone adds a second non-topic tag. The DB proves only the drain path has ever run — zero `tag_category='quality'` rows exist and every AI proposal topic row carries `pipeline_version='drain-v1'`. Fix: (a) `drain/prompts/tag.md` carries `tag_category` per tag with the complexity tag set to `quality`; (b) `drain/apply.ts` honors the per-tag value, falling back to the old derivation for result files already in flight; (c) a write-boundary vocabulary guard rejects and COUNTS any `(entity_type, tag_category, tag)` outside the allowed vocabulary instead of inserting it silently — the prompt already tells workers to drop out-of-vocab tags, but that instruction was unenforced, which is exactly how `aviation` (329 rows) and four singleton tags got in. <!--id:FIX-890-->
- [x] 🟡 S — **entity_tags.ai_model records the same Haiku model two ways** — `claude-haiku-4-5` (3,589 rows) and `claude-haiku-4-5-20251001` (7,211 rows) are the same model recorded under two identifiers, so any per-model cost or provenance audit double-counts the pipeline. All 3,589 undated rows are `entity_type='proposal' AND generated_by='ai'` — the drain path — so the normalization is exactly scoped. Normalize to the dated identifier; folded into the [[FIX-889]] script as a second, separately-reported statement. NOT touched: 4 rows carrying `claude-opus-4-7` (2 `accessible`, 1 `housing`, 1 `other`, all created 2026-04-24) — an expensive model reaching a Haiku-only drain pipeline is a separate question worth its own investigation. <!--id:FIX-891-->
- [x] 🔴 M — **Correct Haiku 4.5 pricing constants, fail closed on unknown models, and collapse duplicated price literals into one source of truth** — Every AI cost estimate, gate and recorded actual was computed from Haiku-3-era prices ($0.25/M input, $1.25/M output) applied to a Haiku 4.5 model id. Claude Haiku 4.5 actually bills $1.00/M input and $5.00/M output — every cost figure the platform has ever produced is ~4x low, so COST_CONFIG.monthly_hard_limit_usd of $3.50 was really permitting ~$14 of spend. Eight sites carried a private copy of the price: cost-config.ts, client.ts (inline + header), ai-tagger.ts (HAIKU_*_COST_PER_M), the two per-entity summary routes, the claude status dashboard, /api/dashboard/anthropic-cost, and packages/db anthropic-usage.ts + platform-snapshot.ts. The six aggregation sites also ignored the model column entirely, pricing every api_usage_logs row as Haiku regardless of what actually ran. model_pricing default was Haiku-priced, so any model missing from the map billed as the cheapest model available. Fix: one dependency-free pricing table, calculateCostUsd throws on an unrecognized model, aggregation reads price unknown models at the highest known rate (fail-closed for spend). <!--id:FIX-893-->
- [x] 🟠 M — **Gate enrichment enqueue on actual source-text presence so tag/summary tasks are never created for text-free proposals** — The enqueue path staged a tag and a summary task for every proposal regardless of whether the platform held any text to classify from. 96% of the pending proposal backlog was Legistar municipal matters with zero summary_plain, whose source API supplies no abstract, so they will never acquire text by waiting. Draining them means paying a model to invent topics and summaries from titles alone. Governing principle: classify only where the classification is derived from text we hold. The gate keys on the entity actually having usable text NOW (not on a source-name blocklist, which goes stale the moment a source starts supplying abstracts), so a Legistar matter that later acquires a summary becomes eligible automatically. Scoped to the proposal path only; officials and financial entities have their own context shapes and are governed separately. Skipped counts are logged per source per run so the gate's effect is visible rather than silent. See [[FIX-895]] for the one-time sweep of the existing backlog. <!--id:FIX-894-->
- [x] 🟠 S — **One-time sweep marking the existing text-free proposal enrichment backlog as skipped_no_source_text** — The [[FIX-894]] enqueue gate stops NEW text-free tasks but does not touch the ~127k pending proposal rows already staged. Idempotent, re-runnable sweep script (local first, explicit --allow-prod) that derives the target set by query (no hardcoded ids) and marks currently-pending proposal tag/summary rows failing the text predicate with a new status value. Rows are MARKED, never deleted, so they stay recoverable: the same script carries a reverse sweep behind --reverse that returns marked rows to pending once text actually arrives. enrichment_queue.status is plain text NOT NULL with no CHECK constraint and no enum, so the new value needs no migration — but every reader of status was audited first to confirm an unfamiliar value cannot break a claim loop or a dashboard count. <!--id:FIX-895-->
- [x] 🟠 M — **Shared chunked-by-ids fetch helper (fetchChunkedByIds); migrate three call sites** — `.in()` over an unbounded id list is the most-repeated bug in this codebase — four independent instances ([[FIX-509]], [[FIX-514]], [[FIX-774]], [[FIX-899]]). Mechanism is always the same and always silent: a large id list builds a request URL past the gateway's header limit, Kong 414s (`URI too long`) before PostgREST sees it, `withDbTimeout` returns `{data:null}`, and a `?? []` fallback turns the failure into an empty result. **The bound is URL LENGTH at roughly 200 ids, not the 1,000-row PostgREST cap.** [[FIX-899]] is the case that forced a structural fix: `app/officials/page.tsx` fetched tags with one `.in()` over ~1,000 officials (~40 KB URL) and every official card rendered zero tag pills, silently, for months — surfacing only because [[FIX-897]] added industry pills to that exact surface and they did not appear. Two facts set the shape: (a) a warning comment 15 lines ABOVE the bug already described this precise trap for a different fetch, and the fetch below it did it anyway — comments do not enforce; (b) the pattern had already been hand-written three times locally ([[FIX-732]] `ID_CHUNK` + local helper in `api/graph/connections/route.ts`, [[FIX-778]] in `api/graph/agency-staffing/route.ts`, [[FIX-899]] inline in `officials/page.tsx`) — not a knowledge problem. **Deliverable:** one shared `fetchChunkedByIds` in `apps/civitics/src/lib/paginate.ts`, beside `fetchAllRows`, so 'how do I read a lot of rows' and 'how do I read by a lot of ids' are found together. Contract: default chunk size 200 via an exported named constant (`ID_CHUNK_SIZE`); **returns a result OBJECT carrying rows AND failed-chunk info, never a bare array** — a helper that returns `T[]` and swallows a failed chunk reproduces the bug class one layer down; does NOT throw by default (some surfaces legitimately prefer partial data) but offers opt-in `strict` for callers where partial is worse than nothing; dedupes and drops empty/nullish ids before chunking; short-circuits an empty list to zero requests rather than issuing `.in("id", [])`; optional per-chunk timeout label so it composes with `withDbTimeout`. **Three migrations only** — the three sites above, where correct behaviour is already known and verified so migration is provably behaviour-preserving. The remaining id-list surface is a separate audit. <!--id:FIX-901-->
- [ ] 🟠 M — **Trigger weekday fec_bulk from the FEC indiv watermark, not just from pending resume state** — **Landed unverified — leave OPEN until a live nightly proves it.** nightly-sync is scheduled 02:00 UTC but GHA queues it ~3.5h late, so the fec phase actually starts ~05:30 UTC. FEC publishes `indiv{yy}.zip` on Sundays ~15:20 UTC. The Sunday heavy run therefore always probes LAST week's file. Measured 2026-07-26: `fec_indiv_watermark['2026']` = `Sun, 12 Jul 2026 15:23:58 GMT`; the killed run's `fec_bulk_run_state` lm = `Sun, 19 Jul 2026 15:25:22 GMT`; live HEAD = `Sun, 26 Jul 2026 15:18:36 GMT`. Two failures compound: (1) when last week's file was already ingested the `watermarkUnchanged` gate in `packages/data/src/pipelines/fec-bulk/index.ts` skips cycle 2026 outright — the Jul 19 run re-upserted an UNCHANGED watermark at 05:37:51Z, ~15 min in, far too early for a completed indiv ingest; (2) on those weeks there is no run state, and `packages/data/src/pipelines/index.ts` gated weekday fec_bulk on `fecResumeState !== null`, so Mon–Sat never invoked fec_bulk at all and Sunday's file sat uningested for a full week. Net: one full ingest per two weeks, one ~2.5h writer run discarded per two weeks, prod donor data permanently 1–2 weeks behind FEC. **Fix shipped:** new `fec-bulk/drop-check.ts` (`currentFecCycle` + `indivDropIsAhead` pure; `indivDropPending` best-effort wrapper) adds a third trigger so the gate is `isWeekly || fecResumeState !== null || fecDropPending`. Fails closed — a failed HEAD never launches a ~2.5h ingest. **Durable proof required before closing:** the next weeknight after a Sunday drop, fec-phase must invoke fec_bulk with no pending resume state, and `fec_indiv_watermark['2026']` must advance to the current FEC Last-Modified within the same week it was published. Report the actual watermark value and the run's fec_bulk `duration_ms`. Same stranded-behind-a-calendar-gate class as the IRS-990 item filed alongside it. <!--id:FIX-903-->
- [x] 🟠 S — **IRS-990 is stranded behind the Sunday gate when fec_bulk overruns the fec-phase budget** — IRS-990 runs after fec_bulk inside the `if (isWeekly)` block of the fec phase in `packages/data/src/pipelines/index.ts`, so a fec_bulk overrun that SIGTERMs the job at its GHA budget means IRS-990 never runs that week — and no weekday path reaches it, because the only off-Sunday fec-phase triggers ([[FIX-754]] resume state, [[FIX-903]] drop-check) invoke fec_bulk alone and stop before the `if (isWeekly)` block. Same stranded-behind-a-calendar-gate class as [[FIX-903]]: a stage whose only invocation path is gated on the calendar, sitting downstream of a stage that reliably exhausts the budget. Unlike FEC bulk, 990s file annually with a ~1-month bulk lag and the index HEAD-watermark short-circuits unchanged years cheaply, so the cost of a missed week is lower — but the failure is silent and unbounded (it recurs every week fec_bulk overruns). Options: give IRS-990 its own weekly GHA job (mirrors the FIX-740 phase split and the FIX-291 entity-connections extraction), or add a watermark-driven weekday trigger of its own. Do NOT simply move it before fec_bulk — the ordering is load-bearing: `resolveGrantRecipient` needs fec-bulk's freshly-upserted PAC entities in the same wave when a 990 grants to a PAC. **✅ DURABLE VERIFY LANDED 2026-08-18 (cc-prompt-66).** This FIX was reopened 2026-08-11 on an explicit condition: it stays open until a real firing produces a durable verify. Both halves now exist. THE EXTRACTION was proven by the 2026-08-17 scheduled firing (GHA run 32042442990, workflow irs990.yml, schedule '0 15 * * 1'), which fired on its OWN weekly schedule entirely independent of the Sunday `isWeekly` gate inside the fec phase — precisely the stranded-behind-a-calendar-gate defect this FIX existed to close. That run then failed on a DB call for reasons unrelated to the extraction ([[FIX-1055]]). THE MISSING REAL RUN landed tonight: GHA run **32085175163**, 2m2s, conclusion success, with the pipeline's own `irs990` data_sync_log row closing `complete` 00:37:09 → 00:38:38 — so the pipeline itself is proven green end-to-end against prod, including the officials name-map load, the index HEAD-watermark short-circuit, and its own startSync/completeSync bookkeeping. Scheduling proven separately, pipeline proven separately, both on prod. Cross-ref [[FIX-1055]], [[FIX-1058]]. <!--id:FIX-904-->
- [x] 🟠 M — **Repeatable, date-stamped local prod-clone refresh (pnpm db:clone:prod)** — The local prod-clone refresh existed only as a hand-rolled multi-hour procedure in prior-session memory plus a misleading two-line recipe in docs/OPERATIONS.md that omitted the pg_cron, matview, auth-schema and stamp steps. Nobody could say how stale the clone was because the restore date was never recorded, so three workstreams paid for it: the SLD choropleth could not be developed locally and the money_vote_influence screening pass had to stamp every number 'prod-verify before authoring'. scripts/db-clone-prod.mjs makes it one command: local-only target guard, pg_dump public data-only from prod, preflight of the privileges the destructive half needs, park pg_cron via cron.alter_job, TRUNCATE plus pg_restore --disable-triggers as supabase_admin, un-park cron, refresh the 13 true matviews via refresh_derived_mvs, VACUUM ANALYZE, then write a pipeline_state 'local_clone_restore' stamp LAST so it survives the dump's own pipeline_state rows. The stamp is the durable deliverable: staleness becomes a query instead of a guess. Cross-ref [[FIX-859]]. <!--id:FIX-912-->
- [x] 🟠 S — **fetchChunkedByIds fans out unbounded; cap concurrency before FIX-902 points it at five-figure id lists** — `fetchChunkedByIds` (FIX-901) issued every chunk through one unbounded `Promise.all`. Fine for today's three call sites; not fine once [[FIX-902]] starts migrating the remaining ~100 unbounded `.in()` sites, some fed by five-figure id lists — the largest plausible input is the full active-officials set at 27,193 ids, i.e. 136 chunks = 136 simultaneous PostgREST requests from ONE request handler. Fixed: `ID_CHUNK_CONCURRENCY = 6` (exported beside `ID_CHUNK_SIZE`) plus a per-call `opts.maxConcurrency`, clamped to ≥ 1, implemented as a hand-rolled worker pool (no new dependency). The two constants in the file guard DIFFERENT ceilings and the JSDoc now says so explicitly: `ID_CHUNK_SIZE` is how WIDE one request may be (URL length / 414), `ID_CHUNK_CONCURRENCY` is how MANY may be open at once (connection/socket budget) — not a latency budget, not a row bound. The result contract is unchanged for any input: rows in chunk order, `failed` in chunk-index order, `chunkCount` unaffected, and `strict` still issues EVERY chunk before throwing (a pool that cancelled the queue on first error would change failure semantics, not just pacing; `paginate.test.ts` pins it). KNOWN LIMIT, documented in the JSDoc rather than fixed: the cap is per CALL, so a route issuing N concurrent `fetchChunkedByIds` calls can have N × maxConcurrency in flight — `app/api/graph/connections/route.ts` does exactly that with seven concurrent calls. A shared module-level limiter was considered and left out of scope. Measured: all three existing call sites are at or under 6 chunks on their heaviest local inputs (officials index ≤ 1,000 ids = 5; agency-staffing 124 active federal agencies = 1; connections at depth 2 on the top-degree official = 5 raw-financial / 3 official / 3 proposal), so none of their timing changes. <!--id:FIX-926-->
- [ ] 🟠 S — **Local prod-clone's pg_cron schedule diverges from prod — clone-based scheduling/ownership reasoning is invalid** — **Found while investigating [[FIX-944]], where a clone reading nearly sent the fix in the wrong direction.** The local prod-clone and prod disagree about `cron.job` in two ways, and both are load-bearing. (1) **Different jobids for the same jobs**: `donor-rollup-refresh` is jobid **24 on prod** but **34 on the clone**; `refresh-derived-mvs-daily` is **9 on prod**, **21 on the clone**; `ec-vacuum-analyze` is **6 on prod**, **15 on the clone**. Any runbook, query or note that identifies a job by jobid is silently wrong against one of the two environments. (2) **Different `active` flags**: on the clone **13 of 21 jobs are `active=false`** (`rebuild-ec-incremental`, `ec-vacuum-analyze`, `refresh-derived-mvs-daily`, `refresh-derived-mvs-weekly`, both `rule-taggers-*`, `financial-entity-totals-incremental`, `donor-rollup-orphan-sweep`, `entity-connection-stats-rebuild`, `donor-party-rollup-refresh`, `donor-party-rollup-orphan-sweep`, `entity-connection-stats-orphan-sweep`, `donation-edge-orphan-sweep`), whereas on prod **all 21 are `active=true`** (verified 2026-07-31 04:19 UTC). The FIX-944 brief reasoned from the clone that ~half the prod schedule was disabled and that the six per-official money rollups were therefore stranded on a job that could not run — a conclusion that would have justified re-enabling prod jobs that were never off. Prod's schedule is healthy; the rollups were stranded by a livelock inside the procedure instead. **The work:** establish why the clone diverges — most likely `db-clone-prod.mjs` restores `cron.job` rows without their `active` state (or deliberately parks them off so a dev clone doesn't fire scheduled writes, which would be correct-but-undocumented) — and then either (a) document the divergence loudly so `cron.job` is treated as a **prod-only read**, or (b) make the clone reproduce prod's flags. Either way, add a line to `scripts/db-clone-prod.mjs` and the CLAUDE.md active-environment section saying that cron state must be read from prod. Cross-ref [[FIX-944]]. <!--id:FIX-946-->
- [ ] 🟠 M — **No coordination mechanism between a supervised prod session and the nightly — twice the resolution has been 'cancel the nightly'** — Observed while repairing [[FIX-945]]. There is no way for a human working on prod to tell the nightly to stand down, and no way for the nightly to know a supervised session is in progress. The only lever available is cancelling the GHA run, and that has now been used twice: 2026-07-30 05:30 UTC (a supervised FEC same-person-merge session needed the DB) and the 07-30 13:21 dispatch. The first of those is what corrupted prod — it killed `tag_rules` mid-`upsertTags()` with both industry DELETEs already committed, truncating 40,148 `rule` rows to 24,000 and all 425 `curated` rows to zero, and the loss then propagated into `official_sector_affinity_rollup` via the [[FIX-704]]/[[FIX-832]] incremental path before anyone noticed. [[FIX-949]] made the tagger writes atomic, so a cancellation is no longer *destructive* — but it is still a cancelled nightly, the work still does not happen, and the underlying scheduling conflict is untouched. Note the cancellation was legitimate each time: two heavy writers on a Pro Small genuinely do contend (this is the same contention behind [[FIX-944]]'s `donor_rollup_refresh` failing daily and being reaped). **Precedent exists in this codebase:** `refresh_official_donor_rollup_incremental` already takes a session advisory lock, so a shared advisory-lock protocol — a supervised session claims the lock, the nightly's heavy phases check it and either defer or skip with a logged `data_sync_log` row instead of being cancelled — would fit the existing patterns without new infrastructure. A lighter first step would be a `pipeline_state` flag plus a `pnpm session:claim-prod` / `:release-prod` pair that the nightly consults. FILED NOT FIXED — this is a scheduling/ops design decision, not a bug with an obvious patch, and it should not ride along with an unrelated PR. Cross-ref [[FIX-945]], [[FIX-949]], [[FIX-944]], [[FIX-290]]. <!--id:FIX-950-->
- [ ] 🟠 M — **No trustworthy per-cycle 'last full FEC pass' watermark — attribution audits cannot ask 'is this row still endorsed by the current binding?'** — Blocking generalisation of [[FIX-954]], and referenced from its header (`packages/data/src/scripts/remediate-bound-cross-person.ts`). **The question every FEC attribution audit actually wants to ask is 'was this row written by the most recent full pass for its cycle?' — a row the current binding no longer produces is by definition mis-bound.** That question is not answerable today, and three separate attempts to derive an answer were measured and rejected on the local clone 2026-08-01: (1) **`max(updated_at)` per cycle** — marks **1,622,392 of 1,734,219 cycle-2024 rows stale**, because the recent runs were not full passes (the last full one was 2026-07-13 at 2,040,556 rows; 2026-07-19 wrote 251,902 and 2026-08-01 wrote 302,538). Useless as a boundary. (2) **`data_sync_log`** — has a `pipeline='fec_bulk'` row per run but **records no cycle scope at all** (`metadata` carries only `{peak_rss_mb}`), and the history is not clean: repeated `failed` rows through July and a row still stuck at `status='running'` from 2026-07-27 that no one reconciled. So 'last complete run covering cycle X' cannot be computed. (3) **Row timestamps as a proxy for pipeline writes** — invalid, because `financial_relationships` carries a `BEFORE UPDATE` trigger `financial_relationships_updated_at → set_updated_at()`, so ANY maintenance UPDATE bumps it. [[FIX-955]]'s repair moved 30,564 rows with `SET to_id = …` and stamped every one of them, which is indistinguishable afterwards from a pipeline write. **Consequence:** [[FIX-954]]'s absolute-staleness gate had to be re-expressed RELATIVE to the holder's own rows ('does this official have rows newer than its contaminated set?'). That is sound and it shipped, but it is strictly weaker — it needs the holder to still be actively written, so it cannot decide an official whose binding is legitimately dormant, and it cannot tell 'this cycle was never re-run' apart from 'this official stopped receiving'. It also means the rule silently degrades if a cycle goes unprocessed for a long stretch. **The fix is small and the hook already exists:** `pipeline_state.fec_bulk_run_state` ALREADY tracks per-cycle, per-stage progress (the pipeline logs `⟳ Pending fec_bulk_run_state: cycle=2026 … indiv-to-committee=0/514958 …` on startup). Extend it to record, per cycle and per stage, the timestamp of the last pass that ran to COMPLETION, and expose a helper (e.g. `fec_cycle_watermark(cycle int)`) the audits can join against. Then 'not endorsed by the current binding' is a direct comparison and [[FIX-954]] can drop its relative proxy. Two things to do alongside: reconcile the stuck `running` `data_sync_log` row and add a guard so an abandoned run cannot stay `running` indefinitely; and consider whether bulk maintenance scripts should suppress the `updated_at` trigger (or write a separate `last_pipeline_write_at`) so a row's provenance stays legible after a merge or repair. Cross-ref [[FIX-954]], [[FIX-955]], [[FIX-952]], [[FIX-930]], [[FIX-934]]. <!--id:FIX-957-->
- [ ] 🟠 M — **fec-backfill GHA OOMs on cycle-2020 indiv stream — 12 GB heap insufficient for the presidential-2020 file** — Surfaced by the prompt-39 FIX-952 prod backfill (2026-08-02): run 30767905956 (cycles=2020,2022) died exit 134 / V8 'Reached heap limit' at ~53M of ~69M lines streaming indiv20 (13,109 MB extracted; 1,706,212 cand pairs / 871,540 cmte pairs / 1,539,420 donors at death; NODE_OPTIONS=--max-old-space-size=12288 on the 16 GB ubuntu-latest runner). streamIndiv (packages/data/src/pipelines/fec-bulk/indiv.ts) builds all three aggregation maps unconditionally — FEC_INDIV_STAGES gates only the post-stream WRITE phases, so stage-splitting does NOT reduce peak streaming memory; FEC_INDIV_TX_TYPES is the only input-level lever (it filters during streaming; [[FIX-677]] precedent cycles=2024,tx_types=10). packages/data/CLAUDE.md's '12 GB for presidential cycles' was calibrated on 2024/2026 (Pro verified 2026-05-02); 2020 is materially bigger (presidential + ActBlue-era itemization) and the workflow had never run a pre-2024 cycle. Prod state after the crash is coherent: cn20 + weball20 match + pas220 landed (idempotent upserts, both FIX-960 damage detectors still zero), indiv20 wrote NOTHING (OOM precedes the write phase), 2022 dispatched separately as its own run; mark-killed wrote the nightly_killed marker for the orphan sync-log row. Candidate fixes: (a) raise heap to ~13.5-14 GB — marginal, projected live heap ~13 GB+ BEFORE the write phase materializes its input arrays, kernel OOM-kill risk on a 16 GB runner; (b) two scoped tx-type passes for 2020 (tx_types=15,15E then tx_types=10) + totals re-derive; (c) make streamIndiv stage-aware or spill-to-disk so each pass holds fewer maps; (d) run pre-2024 cycle backfills from the local machine against prod (proven envelope — the 2026-08-01 local clone pass completed indiv20). Cross-ref [[FIX-952]], [[FIX-690]], [[FIX-181]], [[FIX-236]], [[FIX-677]], [[FIX-960]]. <!--id:FIX-961-->
- [x] 🟡 S — **fec-backfill mark-killed backstop no-ops on timeout-cancelled runs — 4h lookback shorter than the 350-min job cap** — Measured 2026-08-03: run 30769510735 (cycles=2022) hit the workflow's 350-min timeout-minutes cap at 04:00 UTC; the 'Mark killed if no completion row' step then logged '[mark-killed] no fec_bulk row in last 4h — no-op (workflow may not have started)' and left sync-log row bef255c7 stranded at status='running'. Cause: mark-killed.ts defaults --window-hours to 4, but the job cap is 350 min (~5.9h) — so the backstop fails EXACTLY when the job runs longest, which is also when a stranded 'running' row is most likely. The stranded row was repaired manually the same night (mark-killed --window-hours 7 from the local machine). Fix shipped alongside this bullet: fec-backfill.yml now passes --window-hours 7. Audit note: nightly.yml's mark-killed calls keep the 4h default — its phases are minutes-long, so 4h is fine there; any future workflow with a long timeout-minutes must pass a window exceeding its cap. Cross-ref [[FIX-961]], [[FIX-754]], [[FIX-690]]. <!--id:FIX-962-->
- [x] 🟡 S — **mark-killed no-ops when ANY same-pipeline run finished in the window — success-then-crash strands the crashed run's sync row** — Second mark-killed gap, measured 2026-08-05 (first was [[FIX-962]]): the 01:03 UTC fec-backfill dispatch (cycles=2020, run 30965259079) OOM'd at 01:17; its mark-killed step (now --window-hours 7 per FIX-962) no-op'd because the PREVIOUS dispatch's success (22:43 UTC, row 6ce38a1f) counted as 'a fec_bulk run completed in the window'. The no-op predicate matches ANY finished same-pipeline row in the lookback, not THIS job's run — so any success-then-crash sequence inside one window strands the crashed row at status='running' (repaired manually with --window-hours 2, which excluded the stale success). Fix: bind the no-op check to the job's own run — only no-op when the finished row's started_at is >= the current GHA job's start time (pass GITHUB_RUN_STARTED or job start epoch as an arg), or record the sync-row id at pipeline start and check that row's terminal status directly. The 'orphan row' selection already picks the newest running row; it is only the no-op guard that misfires. Cross-ref [[FIX-962]], [[FIX-961]], [[FIX-754]]. <!--id:FIX-963-->
- [x] 🟡 S — **remediate-cross-person --rollups-only is unusable after --apply commits — re-derives an empty manifest and exits before the rollups** — Measured on prod 2026-08-05 during the prompt-39 FIX-934 apply: refresh_treemap_individuals_global() blew its 2400s budget mid-tail (nightly contention + the donor population tripled by the indiv22 backfill) and the script's own abort message said 'Resume with --rollups-only (prod)' — but that mode re-runs the FIX-930 SUSPECT_SQL derivation, which post-commit finds 0 CROSS suspects (the deletes already happened) and exits 'Nothing to remediate' WITHOUT running any rollup step. The advertised resume path can never work after the commit it is meant to resume. --vacuum-only and --mvs-only are immune (they short-circuit before derivation). Recovery used: direct CALL refresh_treemap_individuals_global() with the same 2400s budget (treemap is the LAST phase-2 step, so nothing else was skipped), then --mvs-only for the two chord MVs the phase-3 fatal left behind. Fix options: (a) have --rollups-only skip derivation like the other two modes (the phase-2 global steps -- official totals, fe totals, donor-party chunks, group rollup, search index, treemap -- do not need the affected-officials list, or can take it from a --officials arg), or (b) persist the affected-set to a state row at apply time and have the resume modes read it. Also resize the treemap/chord budgets for the post-indiv22 population (refresh_chord_donor_type_party_flows_mv died at 1200s the same night). - [ ] 🟠 M — **Seven pg_cron jobs blow the 6h statement_timeout; each blowout starves every other job that fires in its window (rule-taggers-weekly fails 4 of 5 runs)** — **Found while diagnosing [[FIX-968]]; the envelope data is filed at `docs/audits/2026-08-06-fix968-cron-window-envelopes.md`.** Measured across the full retained `cron.job_run_details` window (2026-06-29 → 08-06, 177 runs), **seven distinct jobs have been cancelled by the 6h `statement_timeout` that the `postgres` role carries via pg_db_role_setting**: jobid 22 rebuild-ec-incremental-mon (2 of 3 runs), 26 treemap-individuals-global-refresh, 17 donor-party-rollup-refresh, 24 donor-rollup-refresh (4 consecutive, 07-27..07-30), 13 financial-entity-totals-incremental (2 of 5), the retired jobid 1, and worst — **jobid 12 rule-taggers-weekly, which has failed 4 of its 5 retained runs at exactly 21601-21613s with nothing watching it at all**, i.e. the weekly industry tagger has essentially not completed since late June. **Two compounding harms.** (1) Each of these is silent: `cron.job_run_details` is the only place the failure is recorded, nothing reads it except the [[FIX-968]] `check_cron_job_health()` shipped today, and that detector deliberately treats blowouts as REPORT-ONLY (escalating on them would fail the canary most Tuesdays and train the alert to be ignored — the FIX-943 cause-vs-consequence rule). So they stay greppable but unalerted by design, pending this fix. (2) **A blowout does not fail fast — it runs flat out for six hours and then dies**, and on a 2-vCPU Pro Small that sustained load is the single largest cause of the `job startup timeout` class: pg_cron runs with `cron.use_background_workers=off`, so it opens a fresh libpq connection per firing with a ~10s window, and under a 6h squatter that window is blown. Measured directly — on 08-03 jobid 24 was starved by jobid 22 mid-blowout; on 08-04 jobids 13, 24 AND 12 were all starved by jobids 26+17 both mid-blowout. So the blowouts are self-amplifying: a job that cannot finish takes out the jobs scheduled after it. **Fix shape — ⚠ CORRECTED 2026-08-09:** the original shape below is necessary but NOT sufficient, and it names the wrong severity. **jobid 24 was ALREADY chunked ([[FIX-944]]) and still took the site down on 08-08** — so the harm that matters is **request-path saturation**, not job-vs-job starvation, and a fix that merely reschedules cron jobs so they stop colliding does nothing for it. Each arm needs a per-run cost/time budget that keeps a firing off the request path at all, not just a cursor that lets it resume. Original shape, still correct as far as it goes: chunk the remaining arms the way [[FIX-944]] and [[FIX-965]] chunked theirs (per-chunk COMMIT + a resume cursor in `pipeline_state`, so a 6h cancel keeps its progress instead of discarding it), starting with jobid 12 since it is the one that essentially never succeeds — but pair every chunking with a budget. **Scope note 2026-08-09:** jobid 24 has since been addressed by [[FIX-1002]]/[[FIX-1003]]; FIX-969 remains OPEN for jobids 12, 13, 17, 22 and 26. Note `SET statement_timeout` inside a procedure body does NOT re-arm the running timer (measured 2026-07-31, documented in donor-rollup-sweep.ts) — chunking is the only lever, not a timeout bump. Prerequisite input for the rollup-capacity work; the per-job p50/p95/max envelope table is already filed in the audit above so it need not be re-derived. Cross-ref [[FIX-968]], [[FIX-944]], [[FIX-965]], [[FIX-951]], [[FIX-943]], [[FIX-950]], [[FIX-1002]], [[FIX-1003]]. <!--id:FIX-969-->

Arms 2–6 (`official_donor_totals`, small_dollar, sector_affinity, treemap_individuals, donor_bracket_totals) all filter `fr.to_type = 'official'`. **Arm 1 — the `official_donor_rollup_mv` per_donor CTE — does not**; it filters only `relationship_type IN ('donation','ie_support','ie_oppose') AND from_type = 'financial_entity' AND to_id = ANY(p_recipients)`. The [[FIX-944]] dirty-set query in `refresh_official_donor_rollup_incremental()` carries the **same omission**. **Measured on prod 2026-08-06:** the dirty set held **9,562 recipients of which only 4,305 are officials** — i.e. **~55% of every 200-recipient chunk is committee / super-PAC recipients**, for which arms 2–6 correctly produce nothing while arm 1 does full per-donor ranking work. The residue is visible in the table: of a 20,000-id sample of `DISTINCT official_id` in `official_donor_rollup_mv` (776,137 rows total, `relkind=r`, PK `(official_id, relationship_type, rank)`, **no FK to `officials`**), **7,241 are not in `officials` at all**. **Nothing reads those rows** — every consumer is per-official and passes an official id: `apps/civitics/app/api/graph/treemap-pac/route.ts` documents its entityId mode as "official_donor_rollup_mv (rollup #1), *the PAC donors to one official*", plus `api/graph/treemap`, `api/graph/connections` ([[FIX-802]]) and `officials/[id]/page.tsx`. So it is dead weight on both the write and the storage side. **Why it matters now:** the FIX-968 drain measured **~65 min per 200-recipient chunk** (600 recipients in 3h16m) against [[FIX-951]]'s measured 269 s/chunk, and the diagnosis found **no plan pathology** — EXPLAIN at chunk sizes 200/100/50/25/10 shows `Index Only Scan using financial_relationships_donor_rollup_idx` with cost scaling perfectly linearly (78,251 / 27,782 / 10,477 at 200/50/25), no seq scan, no missing index, no spill cliff. The cost is genuine I/O on a cache-starved Pro Small (256MB shared_buffers; the backend sat pinned on `IO/DataFileRead` for 64+ min, and the Supavisor pooler intermittently refused connections outright). **Scoping the dirty set to officials would therefore cut the work by roughly half** — the single largest available lever short of more compute ([[FIX-589]]). **⚠ CORRECTED 2026-08-06, measured after this bullet was filed: the saving is ~30%, NOT ~50% — non-officials are 51% of the recipient COUNT but only 30.2% of the ROW WORK (785,707 rows / 149 per recipient, vs 1,815,180 / 361 for officials), so scoping halves the chunk count while each surviving chunk gets ~1.4× heavier; a ~30% gain does NOT justify discarding an in-flight sweep to re-scope it. The 7,241-dead-id correctness case below is unaffected. Numbers: `docs/audits/2026-08-06-fix968-cron-window-envelopes.md` §7b.** **Confirm intent before changing anything:** if any surface is meant to show "top donors to this super PAC" off this table, then arm 1 is correct and only the DIRTY SET should be scoped. The grep above suggests not, but the check is cheap and the blast radius is a 776k-row table. **Do NOT change dirty-set scope while a sweep is mid-flight** — it invalidates the [[FIX-944]] resume cursor. Sequence: finish the drain, verify convergence, then scope, then one pass to garbage-collect the 7,241 orphan ids (check whether the [[FIX-705]] monthly `reconcile_donor_rollup_orphans` already covers this before writing a new sweep). - [x] 🟡 M — **No launcher-side runtime record for GHA-launched pipelines — reaped data_sync_log spans are read as runtimes and there is no second source to correct them** — **Found while correcting [[FIX-944]]'s runtimes during [[FIX-968]] (2026-08-06).** There is an asymmetry in how we can know when a pipeline actually stopped. For anything **pg_cron** launched, `cron.job_run_details` records `start_time` / `end_time` / `status` / `return_message` **from the launcher's side, independently of anything the job wrote about itself** — so a job that died without closing its own log row can still be timed exactly. For anything **GitHub-Actions** launched (`nightly.yml`, `rebuild-entity-connections.yml`, `usaspending-bulk.yml`, `fec-backfill.yml`, …) there is **no equivalent**: the only record is `data_sync_log`, which is *self-reported*. When the process is SIGTERM'd at the GHA cap, OOMs, or the runner is evicted, it never writes a terminal row, the row stays `status='running'`, and the only thing that ever touches it again is the reaper. **And the reaper stamps its own clock** — `packages/data/src/scripts/mark-killed.ts` writes `completed_at: now` (line ~146) — so the resulting `started_at`..`completed_at` span is bounded by **when the reaper ran**, not by when the work stopped. **This has already produced a materially wrong diagnosis.** [[FIX-944]]'s bullet reported its failing runs as 20h15m / 20h22m / 20h09m and concluded "the nightly's startup reaps the still-running rollup … the job can never win: it needs longer than the gap to the next nightly" — a causal story that shaped [[FIX-950]]'s framing too. `cron.job_run_details` (runids 105/116/123/129) shows those runs were **6h08m / 6h00m / 6h00m / 6h00m**, every one starting 09:00 and dying at ~15:00 on the `postgres` role's 6h `statement_timeout` — already dead ~14 hours before the nightly that supposedly reaped them. The nightly never killed that job. **That was correctable ONLY because `donor-rollup-refresh` happens to be a pg_cron job.** Had the same reap landed on a GHA-launched pipeline — which is where the reaper primarily operates, on `nightly_cron` / `fec_bulk` rows — there would have been **no second source and the wrong duration would be permanently uncorrectable**. Note `data_sync_log` already carries several `reaped` / `reaped_orphan` GHA rows in exactly this shape (e.g. `fec_bulk` on 08-02/08-03/08-05, `nightly_cron` 08-03), whose spans are being read as runtimes today. **The truth does exist externally** — the GHA API returns `run_started_at`, `updated_at` and `conclusion` per run (`gh api /repos/{owner}/{repo}/actions/runs/{id}`, plus per-job timings via `/jobs`) — it is simply never joined to `data_sync_log`, and cannot be joined after the fact because **no run identifier is recorded**: neither `packages/data/src/pipelines/sync-log.ts` nor `mark-killed.ts` reads `GITHUB_RUN_ID`, `GITHUB_RUN_ATTEMPT` or `GITHUB_WORKFLOW`. **Fix shape, cheapest first:** (1) record `GITHUB_RUN_ID` / `GITHUB_RUN_ATTEMPT` / `GITHUB_WORKFLOW` into `data_sync_log.metadata` at `startSync()` — a few lines in `sync-log.ts`, no migration, and it makes every future row joinable to the authoritative external record forever; (2) stop overloading `completed_at` for reaped rows — either a distinct `reaped_at` column or a `metadata.reaped=true` marker plus leaving `completed_at` NULL, so a span can never again be mistaken for a runtime (note `check_rollup_freshness()` and the [[FIX-234]] canary both read these columns, so change them together); (3) optionally a `metadata.last_progress_at` heartbeat written per chunk/phase, which bounds the true stop time to one heartbeat interval even with no external source. **Rule worth encoding wherever these get read:** a `reaped_orphan` row's `started_at`..`completed_at` span is an upper bound on *reaper latency*, never a runtime — `cron.job_run_details` is authoritative for pg_cron work and the GHA API for workflow work. See `docs/audits/2026-08-06-fix968-cron-window-envelopes.md` §6b for the corrected FIX-944 run table and the full derivation. Cross-ref [[FIX-944]], [[FIX-968]], [[FIX-950]], [[FIX-290]], [[FIX-462]], [[FIX-234]], [[FIX-969]]. <!--id:FIX-971-->
- [x] 🟠 M — **donor-rollup drain stops at exactly 600 recipients per window with 27–32% of its budget unspent, and its cursor timestamp reports the chunk's START not its end** — **Found auditing the first day of the [[FIX-968]] `0 9,12 * * *` schedule (prod runids 181/182, 2026-08-06).** Two defects, unrelated causes. **(1) The 600 ceiling.** Both windows cleared EXACTLY 600 recipients and stopped at 601. Not a `LIMIT`, not an array cap, not a dirty-set fetch that is never re-fetched — the fetch is uncapped and correctly built sets of 10,286 and 9,686. 600 is `3 × c_chunk_size`, produced by the [[FIX-944]] predictive budget guard `v_elapsed + (v_max_chunk × 1.25) > c_budget` doing exactly what it was designed to do at a quantum that no longer fits: runid 181 `11,770 + 1.25×4,247 = 17,079 > 16,200`; runid 182 `11,054 + 1.25×6,546 = 19,236 > 16,200`. It repeats at exactly 600 because it is arithmetic, not coincidence — for any roughly uniform per-chunk cost `c`, three chunks complete and the fourth is refused whenever `3,812 s < c < 4,985 s`, and prod sits at `c ≈ 3,900 s`. The cost is **quantization**: the guard can only stop BETWEEN chunks, so the least budget it can leave unspent is one reservation (`1.25 × slowest chunk`), which at a 65-min chunk is **27–32% of the window**. Those constants were correct when FIX-944 set them — the 07-31 sweep (runid 135) ran 42 chunks with a slowest chunk of 749 s, where the same reservation costs 936 s, under 6%. **(2) The phantom 1h49m tail.** runid 182's last cursor write is stamped 13:31:23 while the run ended 15:20:29, reading as 1h49m of stall after the final commit. It is **chunk 3 running**. Proof by exact equality: `15:20:29 − 13:31:23 = 6,546 s`, precisely the run's recorded `slowest_chunk_seconds`. The cursor UPDATE wrote `updated_at = NOW()`, and `NOW()` is `transaction_timestamp()`; in this procedure every chunk runs in its own transaction that BEGINS immediately after the previous chunk's COMMIT, so `NOW()` inside chunk N's cursor write returns the moment chunk N *started* — one whole chunk early. The cursor VALUE was never wrong; only its timestamp, by up to one chunk. That is a live observability defect, not cosmetics: `pipeline_state.updated_at` is the natural thing for an operator or a future stall detector to read, and during a slow chunk it reports a working sweep as idle for over an hour. It cost a full diagnostic pass. **Fix (migration `20260806230000_fix972_donor_rollup_chunk_quantum.sql`):** `c_chunk_size` 200 → 50 so the quantum is ~7% of the budget instead of ~24%; chunk size becomes overridable via a `civitics.donor_rollup_chunk_size` SESSION GUC mirroring the existing `civitics.donor_rollup_budget_seconds` contract (session-scoped so it can never strand a widened setting into the scheduled job, and clamped to [1,1000] because 0 would make the loop never advance while holding an advisory lock); `NOW()` → `clock_timestamp()` on both cursor-write paths; `chunk_size` + `recipients_done` added to run metadata so a future rate detector has something to read. Smaller chunks also shrink the blast radius of the outer 6h role-level `statement_timeout` backstop — a run cancelled mid-chunk discards that chunk's uncommitted work, which was up to 109 min and is now ~27 min. Body rebuilt from the CURRENT live prod definition (`pg_get_functiondef` md5 `b21f8f65452415e4dcd55d725691e6ef`, verified byte-identical to the FIX-944 migration text and to local) so the FIX-776/777/779/836/868 layering is preserved rather than reverted. **Deliberately NOT changed: `c_budget` stays 4h30m.** The 5,400 s between it and the 6h `statement_timeout` is real and unused, but spending it is a SCHEDULING decision, not a defect fix — prod runs **chain back-to-back** (runid 182 began 1 s after 181 ended; the 12:00 firing was deferred while 181 held the job, so the FIX-968 backstop does NOT log `status='skipped'` as its migration predicted, it runs a second full window), so a longer window pushes the second run deep into US active hours. Left for a human call. Cross-ref [[FIX-944]], [[FIX-951]], [[FIX-965]], [[FIX-968]], [[FIX-973]], [[FIX-589]]. <!--id:FIX-972-->
- [ ] 🔴 L — **donor-rollup per-chunk cost regressed ~9× per FR row between 07-31 and 08-06 with FR vacuum-clean — this, not the chunk ceiling, sets the drain ETA** — **Found while diagnosing the [[FIX-972]] 600-recipient ceiling (2026-08-06). This is the finding that contradicts the premise that removing that ceiling would give 2–3× throughput.** Measured on prod: the **07-31** sweep (runid 135, `complete`) cleared **8,381 recipients in 42 chunks in 3h08m** = **1.35 s/recipient**; the **08-06** sweeps (runids 181+182, both `partial`) cleared **1,200 recipients in 6 chunks in 6h20m** = **19.0 s/recipient**. **Density is not the explanation** — counted as they actually were at the time, the 07-31 dirty set carried 3,127,537 rows over 7,402 recipients (**422 rows/recipient**) and today's carries **584**, a 1.38× difference against a 14× wall-clock difference. Normalizing for it still leaves **~9× per FR row (3.6 ms → 33.0 ms)**. The documented playbook for "this job went slow again" ([[FIX-943]], recorded in the header of `packages/data/src/scripts/donor-rollup-sweep.ts`) is `financial_relationships` dead tuples / visibility-map decay — **checked and clean**: FR is 0.72% dead, 94.1% all-visible, last `VACUUM (ANALYZE)` 2026-08-06 00:56. **So this is not FIX-943 recurring, and the existing playbook has no next step.** Leading hypothesis is **working set vs cache**: FR donation rows grew **+52% in six days** (4,253,944 → 6,457,535; 2,203,591 created since 07-31, from the 08-03/08-04 FEC bulk loads of ~1.1M rows each). The table is now 10.27M rows / 448,249 pages / ~3.5 GB with a 616 MB `financial_relationships_donor_rollup_idx`, against **256 MB `shared_buffers`** on the Pro Small ([[FIX-589]]). `donor_rollup_rebuild_recipients()` has six arms that each re-scan the same recipients' rows, so one 200-recipient chunk moves on the order of GBs of buffer traffic through a 256 MB cache — consistent with the FIX-968 audit finding the backend pinned on `IO/DataFileRead` for 64+ min. **Explicitly NOT yet proven:** an `EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)` of arm 1 over 25 real remaining recipients returned in **47 ms** with `shared hit=11674 read=2` — but against a cache warmed by the preceding density query, so it measures only the warm path and does not settle the cold-cache question. Proving it needs per-arm cold-cache profiling, which cannot be done read-only on prod; local Docker (4.37M FR donation rows) can host it. **Why it matters:** at 19.0 s/recipient the 9,086 remaining recipients need ~48 h of pure compute. Daily throughput is `wall-clock spent × rate`, and prod runs chain (see [[FIX-972]]), so the box works continuously 09:00→15:20 and clears ~1,200/day — meaning **~1,200/day is close to what the current window genuinely allows, and the 600-per-window ceiling was costing ~33%, not 50–66%.** At the 07-31 rate the entire remaining set would clear in a single ~3.4 h run. So the cost regression, not the chunk quantum, is the lever worth the next unit of effort, and it is the strongest concrete evidence yet for the [[FIX-589]] instance-size conversation. Sequence: profile per-arm cost under a cold cache, attribute it, then choose between (a) more `shared_buffers` / a larger instance, (b) [[FIX-970]]'s dirty-set scoping (~30% of row work), (c) [[FIX-969]]'s delta-apply. **⚠ MEASUREMENT 2026-08-07, from the [[FIX-974]] bulk run — the regression is ACCESS PATTERN, not the box, so option (a) is NOT indicated by this evidence.** The same work in a `to_id`-RANGE, set-based regime ran on the SAME instance the SAME night at **0.060 s/recipient** (all 15,316 recipients in 926 s, one CALL) against the 19.0 s/recipient measured here — **~314×** — and the per-chunk `EXPLAIN` is an `Index Only Scan` at **0.118 ms/FR row vs the 33.0 ms/row above**. Two causes, both properties of the per-recipient regime rather than of the hardware: (1) `to_id = ANY(uuid[])` over scattered uuids is N independent index descents plus scattered heap access, where a range predicate on the same index is one dense walk; (2) `fr.to_type='official'` is not in `financial_relationships_donor_rollup_idx`, so arms 2–6 forced a heap visit per row — and that predicate turns out to be **exactly** `to_id IN (SELECT id FROM officials)` (measured: 0 exceptions in either direction across all 6,457,535 rows in the index scope), so resolving it off the index keeps the whole scan index-only. Cache warming was visible in-run (62.5 s/chunk over the first 10, **13.7 s/chunk** over the last 22), which is corroborating evidence for the working-set hypothesis in this bullet but *not* evidence that the box is undersized for this work. **This bullet stays OPEN**: FIX-974 routes around the regression without explaining why the per-recipient path went 3.6 ms → 33.0 ms per row in six days, and that path is still what the daily trickle uses. The per-arm cold-cache profiling asked for above is still unwritten. Cross-ref [[FIX-974]], [[FIX-972]], [[FIX-943]], [[FIX-951]], [[FIX-968]], [[FIX-969]], [[FIX-970]], [[FIX-589]], [[FIX-884]]. <!--id:FIX-973-->
- [x] 🔴 M — **The freshness canary watches 1 of 14 scheduled derived-data pipelines — four unwatched ones are 1.1-2.4 cadence cycles behind right now, including FE totals at 16.8 days and the graph rebuild at 8.9 days** — **Found by the 2026-08-07 site-wide efficiency audit (`docs/audits/2026-08-07-efficiency-audit.md`), class 6 — freshness-only monitoring; SURVIVED adversarial verification, measurement widened.** Playbook **D4 signature B** ("a detector covers only what it enumerates") and **E5** ("a guard covers only the sites it enumerates"), the same shape as [[FIX-968]]'s finding that the canary watched 1 of 21 pg_cron jobs while seven were starved. **The defect is one literal.** `packages/data/src/scripts/canary-check.ts:35-40` is `const ROLLUP_PIPELINES: {pipeline: string; maxAgeHours: number}[] = [{ pipeline: "donor_rollup_refresh", maxAgeHours: 48 }]` — **length 1**. The RPC it calls, `check_rollup_freshness(p_pipeline text)`, is fully generic; the narrowing is purely the caller's array. **Measured on prod 2026-08-07 04:53 UTC** — hours since the last `status='complete'` row, against each job's own cron cadence: `financial_entity_totals_refresh` **403.8 h** (weekly `0 9 * * 2` → **2.4 cycles**; last complete 2026-07-21) **UNWATCHED** · `donor_party_rollup_refresh` **236.1 h** (weekly) **UNWATCHED** · `entity_connections_rebuild` **212.8 h** (Mon+Wed = 84 h cadence → **2.5 cycles**) **UNWATCHED** · `contract_flow_rollups_rebuild` **182.8 h** (weekly; its 08-06 firing was dropped as `job startup timeout`, so it reads 336 h by 08-13) **UNWATCHED** · `donor_rollup_refresh` **115.8 h** — **WATCHED, and the freshest of the five.** The one entry on the list is the one least in need of it. **User-visible, not internal hygiene:** `financial_entity_totals_refresh` populates `financial_entities.total_donated_cents` / `total_received_cents`, which render on **every donor and financial-entity page** — **16.8 days** behind source. `entity_connections_rebuild` is what the **graph** renders — **8.9 days** behind. Neither has any detector. **Coverage arithmetic:** 36 distinct derived relations exist in `public`; the single watched pipeline covers 6 (the [[FIX-974]] arms) and `check_sector_affinity_tag_staleness` covers 1 more already inside that 6 — so **29 of 36 derived relations have no freshness detector of any kind**. **Fix shape:** (a) **derive `ROLLUP_PIPELINES` from the schedule instead of maintaining a literal** — every pg_cron job that writes `data_sync_log` gets watched with `maxAgeHours` computed from its own cron cadence (× ~1.5 slack). That is the mechanism-level fix; "add four more entries" is not, and would go stale the same way. (b) Apply the [[FIX-943]]/[[FIX-968]] escalate-vs-report split so the canary does not page every Tuesday: one cycle late = report, two-plus = escalate. **Two sibling literal-lists found by the same pass, same mechanism, both verified:** `check_rebuild_autovacuum_status()`'s watched set is a hand-maintained 9-row `VALUES` list (`20260801000000_fix943_…sql:237-251`), so eight large bulk-rewritten derived relations — `entity_connection_stats_mv` (2,418,981 rows), `official_donor_rollup_mv` (1,005,465), `treemap_individuals_rollup` (929,230), `group_donor_rollup` (530,311) + four smaller — can collapse their visibility maps with no signal (all eight measure 100% all-visible today, so this is a coverage bound, not an observed degradation). And `packages/data/src/pipelines/integrity-audit/index.ts:25-30` is a literal 4-module `CHECKS` array (officials, proposals, votes, referential) whose modules reference **zero** derived relations — a `grep -cE 'rollup|_mv|_totals|_stats|treemap|entity_connections'` over all four returns **0** — so the only scheduled equality assertion against prod audits **0 of 36** rollups. That is playbook **E1** applied one-sided: base tables get invariant checks, the rollups computed from them get nothing, which is the precise shape that let $2,079,909,441 of understatement sit behind a stable-looking table in [[FIX-968]]. Cross-ref [[FIX-968]], [[FIX-943]], [[FIX-234]], [[FIX-975]], [[FIX-969]], [[FIX-971]], [[FIX-885]]. <!--id:FIX-977-->
- [x] 🔴 M — **0 of 7 scheduled detectors measures a RATE — every one reads an AGE or a STATE, and FIX-972's per-run rate substrate is transported to the canary and dropped by the TypeScript destructure** — **Found by the 2026-08-07 site-wide efficiency audit (`docs/audits/2026-08-07-efficiency-audit.md`), class 6; SURVIVED adversarial verification.** Playbook **D4 signature A**: *a throughput regression that converges inside the freshness window is invisible by construction.* The arc paid for this once — the canary fired correctly for the stale rollup and could not see the same job's per-row cost regress **~9× in six days** ([[FIX-973]], still open) — and [[FIX-972]] responded by recording `chunk_size` and `recipients_done` per run **specifically so a rate detector would have something to read**. Nothing reads them. **Measured by mechanism.** A repo-wide grep for consumers of the rate substrate — `recipients_done`, `chunk_size`, `last_metadata`, `slowest_chunk_seconds`, `per_row`, `rows_per_second`, `seconds_per` across `apps/`, `packages/`, `scripts/` — returns **ZERO consumers**. The only near-miss is `donor-rollup-sweep.ts:137`, which *declares* `last_metadata` in its `Freshness` interface and never dereferences it (`reportState` prints only `last_complete_at` / `hours_since_complete` / `stale` / `last_status` / `sweep_in_progress`). Writers: 4 hits, all inside the FIX-972 migration. **The transport layer is intact and the consumer is absent** — `check_rollup_freshness` returns the metadata, `canary-check.ts:266-283` destructures the result, and the destructure simply does not take those fields. **The substrate is available and rich.** Of 12 scheduled rollup pipelines on prod, **11 record both a duration and a row count on every retained run** (`donor_rollup_refresh` 22/22, `refresh_derived_mvs` 33/33, `run_rule_taggers` 32/30, `official_vote_stats_rebuild` 19/19, `entity_connection_stats_rebuild` 9/9) — a per-unit rate is **derivable today for 11 of 12 and computed for 0 of 12**. The richest case is one division: `donor_rollup_bulk`'s 2026-08-07 02:14 run recorded `targets=15316, elapsed_seconds=926` → **0.0605 s/recipient**, against the 19.0 s/recipient the per-recipient path measured the night before. Nothing divides. **Why this is the audit's most structural finding:** every detector on the box answers *"is it stale?"*; none answers *"is it getting slower?"*. A job that doubles in cost but still lands inside its window is invisible until it crosses the 6 h ceiling — at which point it becomes a [[FIX-969]] blowout, and blowouts are report-only by design. So the platform has no way to see a regression coming, only to see it arrive. **This audit's own class-4 findings are that blind spot cashing out:** `entity-connection-stats-rebuild` at 86% of its ceiling and `contract_flow_rollups_rebuild` at 7.2× week-over-week were both found by hand here; nothing running would have surfaced either. **Fix shape:** (a) extend the `canary-check.ts` destructure and its `Freshness` type to read `last_metadata`, and compute `elapsed_seconds / rows_done` per run — the transport already carries it; (b) store a trailing baseline (median per-unit cost over the previous N runs) and **REPORT, not escalate**, when a run exceeds it by a factor, per the [[FIX-943]] cause-vs-consequence split; (c) the cheapest universal version needs no new writes at all — `data_sync_log` already carries `completed_at - started_at` and `rows_inserted + rows_updated` for 11 of 12 pipelines, so a generic rate panel is a view, not a migration. **But it must exclude the zero-span writers first**: 8 of 46 pipelines currently report a 0 ms duration (filed separately) and would divide to an infinite rate. Cross-ref [[FIX-972]], [[FIX-973]], [[FIX-968]], [[FIX-969]], [[FIX-943]], [[FIX-951]]. <!--id:FIX-978-->
- [ ] 🟠 S — **8 of 46 pipelines report p50=p95=max=0 ms on the admin pipeline-health page — their data_sync_log rows stamp started_at AND completed_at with now(), bound at transaction entry** — **Found by the 2026-08-07 site-wide efficiency audit (`docs/audits/2026-08-07-efficiency-audit.md`); the class-5 (lying timestamps) and class-8 (status-as-liveness) passes converged on it, and it SURVIVED adversarial verification.** Same `NOW()` root cause as [[FIX-972]] — `NOW()` is `transaction_timestamp()`, frozen at transaction start — surfacing somewhere FIX-972 never looked, which is the playbook **E5** lesson: after fixing one site, grep for the mechanism. **The defect.** Several rollup procedures close out with a single terminal row — `20260721010000_fix873_contract_flow_tag_dedup.sql:391`, `20260719000000_fix837_materialize_official_vote_stats.sql:243`, `20260715020000_fix778_materialize_agency_staffing_rollup.sql:162`: `INSERT INTO data_sync_log (pipeline, status, started_at, completed_at, …) VALUES ('…','complete', now(), now(), …)`. The whole procedure body runs in one transaction, so **both** `now()` calls return the *entry* timestamp: `completed_at` records when the run STARTED and the recorded duration is **exactly zero**. **Precision on the failure mode** (an earlier framing of this finding was refuted for overreaching): the row is not visible until `COMMIT`, so it does **not** advertise completion early to other readers, and per playbook **D2** a missing `running` row is not itself a liveness defect. The defect is the **timestamps**. **Measured on prod:** every retained row for `official_vote_stats_rebuild`, `agency_staffing_rollup_refresh` and `contract_flow_rollups_rebuild` has `completed_at - started_at = 0.000000`. Exact-alignment proof: `contract_flow_rollups_rebuild` runid 130 ran **14:00:02.448368 → 15:58:48.014711 = 7,125.6 s** per `cron.job_run_details`, while its `data_sync_log` row reads `started_at = completed_at = 14:00:02.448486` — **understating the finish by 1h58m**. Agency-staffing likewise: cron says 00:19:43.262714 / 00:09:06.698344 / 00:03:16.057629; `data_sync_log` says 00:00:00 / 00:00:00 / 00:00:00. **The consequence is rendered as fact.** `pipeline_runtime_stats_mv` (`20260510000002_pipeline_runtime_stats_mv.sql:31-38`) derives every duration percentile from `completed_at - started_at`, and `apps/civitics/app/admin/pipeline-health/page.tsx:85` renders it. Measured on prod: **8 of 46 pipelines report `p50_duration_ms = p95_duration_ms = max_duration_ms = 0`** — `canary_check` (32 runs), `official_vote_stats_rebuild` (17), **`treemap_individuals_global_refresh` (5)**, `agency_staffing_rollup_refresh` (4), `contract_flow_rollups_rebuild` (4), `sector_affinity_rollup_backfill` (4), `small_dollar_rollup_backfill` (1), `treemap_individuals_focused_backfill` (1). **`treemap_individuals_global_refresh` is one of [[FIX-969]]'s ceiling-blowing jobs — its 21,615.9 s run renders as 0 ms** on the only runtime surface the platform has. So the single runtime-rate view is structurally blind to a daily job and to two of the longest-running weekly ones, which is part of why [[FIX-973]]'s ~9× regression had to be reconstructed by hand. **Fix shape:** (a) `clock_timestamp()` for `completed_at` in every one of these terminal-row writers — playbook **E7**, `NOW()` is for transactional consistency, not observability; (b) better, write the row in two steps as `sync-log.ts` does (a row at entry, a terminal `UPDATE` at exit) so the span is real; (c) have `pipeline_runtime_stats_mv` exclude or flag zero-span rows so a future rate detector (see the rate-measure FIX) cannot silently read 0 as truth. The reaped-row hazard from [[FIX-971]] lands on the same two columns — fix them together. Cross-ref [[FIX-972]], [[FIX-971]], [[FIX-969]], [[FIX-973]], [[FIX-837]], [[FIX-838]], [[FIX-778]], [[FIX-873]]. <!--id:FIX-979-->
- [x] 🟠 S — **Nothing watches the watchman — canary_check is in no detector's coverage, is not a pg_cron job, and produced zero rows on 2026-08-06, the day with the most incidents in the retained window** — **Found by the 2026-08-07 site-wide efficiency audit (`docs/audits/2026-08-07-efficiency-audit.md`), class 6; SURVIVED adversarial verification — every load-bearing fact re-derived, all refutation attempts failed.** Playbook **D4** (coverage is enumeration — the watchman is in no enumeration) plus **C3** (a guard must be able to fire in the case it exists for). **The exposure.** `canary_check` is a GHA workflow (`.github/workflows/sync-canary-check.yml`, `cron: 0 5 * * *`), so `check_cron_job_health()` structurally cannot see it — that function reads `cron.job_run_details`, and there is no pg_cron job for the canary. It is also absent from `ROLLUP_PIPELINES`, and no workflow asserts on another workflow. Coverage check by mechanism: `grep -rn "canary_check" --include=*.ts --include=*.sql apps packages supabase` returns **only the writer itself**. **Measured on prod:** `canary_check` rows in `data_sync_log` are present on **19 consecutive UTC days, 2026-07-18 → 08-05**, then **ZERO rows on 2026-08-06**, giving 45.3 h since last complete against a daily cadence. That gap is not random — it is **the single day with the most incidents in the retained window** (the FIX-968 migration and `canary-check.ts:68-85` both document GHA run 31081114924 on 08-06 07:30 dying on `service_role`'s 8 s statement timeout). **So on the day the platform most needed a health signal, it emitted none, and nothing noticed.** **Why the [[FIX-968]] remediation did not close it:** that work made each individual sub-read non-fatal, but left uncovered (a) the meta-row INSERT itself — `db.from("data_sync_log").insert(...)` at `canary-check.ts:399-459`, subject to the same 8 s `service_role` cap and only `console.warn()`ing on failure — and (b) whole-process failure before `main()` ever reaches `writeMetaRow`. **The blast radius is the whole detector fleet:** 6 of the 7 scheduled detectors in the audit's class-6 population run *only* inside this one process — `check_cron_job_health`, `check_rollup_freshness`, `check_rebuild_autovacuum_status`, `check_sector_affinity_tag_staleness` and the rest. When the canary does not run, **all of them go dark simultaneously**, and the silence is indistinguishable from health. **Fix shape:** (a) make the meta-row write the thing that cannot fail quietly — non-zero process exit on insert failure, so GHA marks the run failed and the workflow's own failure surface catches it; (b) add a dead-man check that reads the canary's OWN freshness — cheapest as a pg_cron job (the one scheduler that can observe the GHA side), escalating when `max(started_at)` for `pipeline='canary_check'` exceeds ~30 h; (c) note this is the same class as [[FIX-971]]'s GHA-has-no-launcher-record gap and shares its fix substrate — recording `GITHUB_RUN_ID` at `startSync()` makes the canary's own runs joinable to the authoritative external record. Cross-ref [[FIX-968]], [[FIX-971]], [[FIX-234]], [[FIX-943]], [[FIX-962]]. <!--id:FIX-980-->
- [x] 🟡 S — **Three operator-facing 'is anything in flight on prod?' probes read data_sync_log.status='running' as liveness — one of them gates a prod close-out** — **Found by the 2026-08-07 site-wide efficiency audit (`docs/audits/2026-08-07-efficiency-audit.md`), class 8 — status-as-liveness (population 60 sites); SURVIVED adversarial verification at 🟡 after its siblings were refuted.** Playbook **D2**: *a `running` row is not liveness — orphan rows outlive dead processes; liveness comes from `pg_stat_activity`, history from `cron.job_run_details` (pg_cron) or the GHA API (workflows).* [[FIX-971]] filed the *recording* half of this problem; this is the *reading* half, and it is the part that can make a decision. **Three probes decide something on a status column:** `scripts/verify-fix916-closeout.sql:33-35` — a **gate on a prod close-out**, 12 h window; `scripts/db-clone-prod.mjs:249-251` — 6 h window, gates a clone; and the prod-merge runbook step recorded at `docs/audits/2026-07-30-fix933-same-person-merge-local.md:202-203`. All three infer in-flight-ness from `status='running'`, which is **wrong in both directions**: a dead process leaves the row set (false positive), and a process that never wrote a start row is invisible (false negative). **The expensive failure mode is the refusal, not the false clear** — a guard that declines to run because a dead row says `running` is a self-inflicted outage, and one of these three gates a prod close-out. That is playbook **C3** territory (a guard must be able to fire — and must not fire spuriously — in the case it exists for). **Scoped honestly.** Verification measured the current orphan population and it is **not** alarming: `data_sync_log` spans 107.3 days, orphan arrival is **0.87/day**, and **resident orphans on prod right now: 0** — the single `status='running'` row at the time of measurement was a live cron run, not a corpse. [[FIX-944]] also stopped stamping `completed_at` on reaped rows, so the older long-lived-orphan numbers no longer describe the current regime. **So this is filed as a latent correctness-of-mechanism defect at 🟡, not as an active incident** — the probes are wrong about what they measure, and the cost lands the day an orphan and a close-out coincide. **Explicitly NOT part of this bullet (both refuted on verification, recorded so nobody re-files them):** `reap_stale_sync_log()` having no pg_cron schedule is *not* a defect — `mark-killed.ts` runs as an `if: always()` post-step in **every** GHA workflow (nightly ×4 phases, fec-backfill, rebuild-entity-connections, usaspending-bulk) and writes an authoritative `nightly_killed` row minutes after a kill (40 such rows on prod), covering exactly the three pipelines that produce most orphans. And `startSync()` swallowing its insert error is not a defect either, because D2 inverts it: a `running` row was never liveness, so its absence masks nothing. **Fix shape:** replace all three probes with the mechanism that cannot go stale — `pg_stat_activity` for "is a backend alive right now", `cron.job_run_details` for pg_cron history, the [[FIX-971]] `GITHUB_RUN_ID` join for GHA work once it lands. The cheap intermediate is an **advisory-lock probe**, which this repo already uses correctly in the chunked writers (`pg_try_advisory_lock`) and which dies with its session by construction. Cross-ref [[FIX-971]], [[FIX-944]], [[FIX-962]], [[FIX-924]], [[FIX-290]], [[FIX-462]]. <!--id:FIX-989-->

Measured on prod 2026-08-08 01:30 UTC via list_scheduled_rollup_pipelines(): last status='complete' 232.0h ago against a combined 84h cadence (jobid 2 rebuild-ec-incremental '0 8 * * 3' + jobid 22 rebuild-ec-incremental-mon '0 8 * * 1'), i.e. 2.8 cadence cycles and ESCALATING under the new two-plus-cycles rule. - [ ] 🔴 M — **The FE-totals watermark is a RATCHET — a failed run hands its whole dirty set to the next run plus another week, so it can never converge** — Structural cause behind [[FIX-977]]'s named 403.8h staleness case, filed separately because the drain is small and this is not. refresh_financial_entity_totals_incremental() (pg_cron jobid 13, weekly '0 9 * * 2') reads pipeline_state.financial_entity_totals_watermark, builds the dirty set of financial_relationships donation rows written since, and advances the watermark ONLY on a clean finish. Measured on prod 2026-08-08: 07-21 complete with dirty_donors=28,884 in 7m04s; 07-28 FAILED at 6h00m11s statement timeout with dirty_donors=827,122 (29x the prior week); 08-04 produced no data_sync_log row at all ('job startup timeout', 10s); by 08-08 the dirty set had grown to 1,879,245 and the watermark still read 2026-07-20 05:46:53. Each retry is STRICTLY LARGER than the one that just failed, against a fixed 6h ceiling — so once a run misses, the job is mathematically guaranteed to keep missing. Note 07-14 processed 828,855 dirty donors in 43 MINUTES, so the same volume was not inherently a 6h job; the 07-28 blowup was the [[FIX-975]] bloat (the run died inside 'UPDATE public.financial_entities', the table that had vacuum_count 0) making the identical workload 8x slower — a feedback loop where the failure prevents the vacuum that would have made the next run cheap. Fix shape: advance the watermark PER COMMITTED WINDOW rather than only at the end, so partial progress is kept and a failed run resumes instead of restarting — the resumable-cursor shape [[FIX-944]]/[[FIX-965]] already established. Cross-ref [[FIX-977]], [[FIX-975]], [[FIX-969]], [[FIX-972]], [[FIX-944]]. <!--id:FIX-991-->
