Revert = delete the one env line. Cross-ref [[FIX-995]], [[FIX-996]]. <!--id:FIX-998-->
- [x] 🟠 M — **fec_bulk indiv resume cursor is an array offset, so a FEC republish laps all in-flight progress** — [[FIX-754]]'s cursor is an OFFSET into a rebuilt rows array (`StageProgress.cursor` + `total_rows`, resolved by `resolveResumeCursor`), not a content key. Row order is not stable across a republish, so `planCycleResume` correctly returns 'stale' and `clearRunState` discards the cursor whenever FEC republishes the cycle's indiv file — which it does WEEKLY, Sundays ~15:20 UTC. That makes the loop structurally non-convergent for any stage whose remaining work exceeds one night's budget: the republish always laps the progress. MEASURED 2026-08-08: prod `fec_bulk_run_state` holds cycle 2026 donor-entities at cursor 484,000/840,338 (started 08-06T06:42, updated 08-08T04:30) at a measured 52.3-67.7 s per 4,000-row chunk — ~237 min for the full stage against a 150-min nightly job budget, i.e. ~2 nights for stage 1 of 4 — and the 2026-08-09 republish resets it to 0. Committed work is NOT invalidated by a republish: the republished file is cumulative for the cycle and every write is an idempotent upsert on a stable arbiter (`donor_fingerprint` / `financial_relationships_relcycle_unique`). Only the OFFSET is invalidated. Direction: checkpoint the last-committed CONTENT KEY (donor fingerprint under a stable sort order) and resume by key, which survives a superset file; `bulkUpsert` would sort rows by that key and binary-search the resume point rather than slicing at an integer. Same family as [[FIX-991]]'s watermark ratchet — progress must be durable against the INVALIDATION cadence, not merely against process death. Note this also removes the `total_rows` mismatch reset, which fires on any night when DB churn changes the rebuilt array length. Cross-ref [[FIX-995]], [[FIX-754]], [[FIX-991]], [[FIX-903]], [[FIX-998]]. <!--id:FIX-999-->
- [x] 🟡 S — **nightly.yml and fec-backfill.yml run the same heaviest pipeline under divergent, unlinked resource budgets** — `.github/workflows/nightly.yml` (fec-phase, `data:nightly:fec:ci`) and `.github/workflows/fec-backfill.yml` (backfill, `data:fec-bulk:ci`) both reach `runFecBulkPipeline()` and the same `streamIndiv` on the same `ubuntu-latest` 16 GB runner class, but their resource envelopes were set at different times for different reasons and NOTHING ties them together: nightly is `NODE_OPTIONS=--max-old-space-size=12288` / `timeout-minutes: 150` ([[FIX-254]] May 2026, [[FIX-689]]); backfill is `14336` / `350` ([[FIX-961]], 2026-08-05). FIX-961 diagnosed the indiv stage heap ceiling and remediated the workflow where the OOM was OBSERVED, not the mechanism's other site — the classic enumeration gap, and the one [[FIX-995]] fell through two days later. The divergence is actively mis-documented: `fec-backfill.yml:70` still asserts that its env block mirrors nightly.yml's fec-phase verbatim, which FIX-961 falsified in the same commit that raised the value. Direction: either a single source of truth for the indiv-stage resource envelope (a composite action, or a repo-`vars.` constant both workflows read), or a CI check that fails when two workflows invoking the same package script diverge on `NODE_OPTIONS` / `timeout-minutes`. NOTE this is a PARITY item, not a tuning item: [[FIX-995]] deliberately did NOT copy 14336 across, because the 2026-08-03..08-08 nightly deaths were two job timeouts, one runner-communication loss, one unrelated statement timeout and one human cancel — with no V8 heap-limit exit in any log. Cross-ref [[FIX-995]], [[FIX-961]], [[FIX-254]], [[FIX-689]], [[FIX-998]]. <!--id:FIX-1000-->
- [x] 🔴 S — **fixes-sync/clean/housekeep capped FIX IDs at 3 digits — FIX-1000 silently resolved to FIX-100** — The repo crossed FIX-999 on 2026-08-08 (FIX-1000 allocated by this session). `scripts/fixes-sync.mjs`, `scripts/fixes-clean.mjs` and `scripts/fixes-housekeep.mjs` all matched FIX IDs with a three-digit-capped pattern, which does not merely MISS a four-digit ID — it matches the first three digits, so `Fixes: FIX-1000` resolved to `FIX-100`, an unrelated already-shipped item. That is silent cross-contamination of done.log plus a wrong checkbox flip in FIXES.md, in the one file the whole workflow treats as append-only ground truth. Six live sites widened to an unbounded digit run: fixes-sync's `verifiedPerFixRe`, its trailer `idRe` and its bullet-marker `idRe`; fixes-clean's `ID_RE`; fixes-housekeep's `ID_RE`, its id-marker sweep and its loose body scan. Observed symptom before the fix: `pnpm fixes:sync` reported `bulletsMissingIdMarker: 1` against the freshly-added FIX-1000 bullet whose marker was present and well-formed — the bullet-marker regex failed CLOSED (safe), while the trailer regex would have failed OPEN (corrupting). Exactly the class `scripts/fix-add.mjs` was hardened against in [[FIX-362]]; its own comment already says the unbounded form future-proofs past 999, but only the ALLOCATOR was fixed and the CONSUMERS were not, so the enumeration stopped at the file where the bug was observed — the same mechanism-vs-site gap as [[FIX-1000]] itself. Regression coverage added to `pnpm fixes:test`: four-digit `Fixes:`/`Closes:` IDs survive intact alongside a two-digit one, and a four-digit per-FIX Verified override binds to the right ID instead of being dropped as unknown. Cross-ref [[FIX-362]], [[FIX-369]], [[FIX-874]], [[FIX-1000]]. <!--id:FIX-1001-->
- [x] 🔴 M — **The donor-rollup budget guard is structurally incapable of firing on the run that takes the site down** — On 2026-08-08 jobid 24 run 195 ran 12:28:39 to 18:28:46 (6h00m06s), was killed by the 6h role statement_timeout, and committed ZERO chunks: pipeline_state.donor_rollup_watermark.updated_at is still 12:28:38 holding the cursor written by the PREVIOUS run. Six hours of flat-out I/O, no durable progress, and the public site served statement timeouts and 503s throughout; by 18:00 the Supabase SQL editor could not open a connection at all. This is a different and more severe failure than the job-vs-job starvation [[FIX-969]] describes, and rescheduling cron jobs does nothing for it. Cause: the FIX-944 predictive guard reads IF v_chunk_no is greater than 0 AND v_elapsed + v_max_chunk * 1.25 is greater than c_budget THEN EXIT. It is skipped entirely before chunk 1 and can only be evaluated BETWEEN chunks, so a run whose FIRST chunk exceeds the whole window never evaluates it even once. Run 195's chunk 1 was 50 recipients and ran past 21,600s against a slowest-ever chunk of 5,447s recorded three hours earlier. The guard is not broken; it cannot fire in the case it exists for (rule C3 -- cf. [[FIX-972]] which retuned the same guard's quantum, [[FIX-965]] and [[FIX-944]] which built it). Underlying defect: the chunk is quantised by RECIPIENT COUNT while the real cost driver is per-recipient financial_relationships fan-out, which varies by orders of magnitude across the uuid-ordered dirty set, so a fixed 50-recipient quantum has unbounded cost. Second finding, which falsifies a stated FIX-968 premise: pg_cron QUEUES the 12:00 firing behind a still-running 09:00 one rather than letting it hit the advisory lock and log skipped -- measured on 08-06, 08-07 and 08-08, the second run starts within ~1s of the first ending, chaining two full budget windows into one continuous 9.5h block. Fix shape: chunk by estimated FR-row weight (official_donor_totals.donor_count is already maintained by this same procedure and costs 117 pages to read) instead of by count; arm the guard from chunk 1 with a durable cross-run slowest-chunk seed; cut c_budget from 4h30m to 2h so a firing cannot still be running when the next is due; yield between chunks. Cross-ref [[FIX-969]], [[FIX-944]], [[FIX-965]], [[FIX-968]], [[FIX-972]], [[FIX-943]]. <!--id:FIX-1002-->
- [x] 🟠 S — **The six donor-rollup arm tables have no autovacuum overrides and no vacuum tail** — refresh_official_donor_rollup_incremental() bulk-rewrites six tables twice daily by DELETE+INSERT (official_donor_rollup_mv, official_donor_totals, official_small_dollar_rollup, official_sector_affinity_rollup, treemap_individuals_rollup, official_donor_bracket_totals) and vacuums none of them, so the [[FIX-943]] standing bulk-rewrite vacuum rule is unenforced on the path that violates it most often. None of the six carries a per-table autovacuum override, while financial_relationships, financial_entities, entity_connections, entity_tags, donor_party_rollup_mv, proposals, votes, enrichment_queue and external_source_refs all do. At the cluster default (threshold 50 plus 0.2 x reltuples) the two large arms only autovacuum at 201,196 and 185,409 dead tuples, and a budget-truncated run of 400 recipients produces only about 64k, so they can go many days without one: measured 2026-08-08, last_autovacuum on official_donor_rollup_mv, official_donor_totals and official_small_dollar_rollup was 2026-08-02, and by 08-08 they read 19.9 / 18.6 / 18.6 percent dead at 73.7 / 32.5 / 31.7 percent all-visible. Over that same 08-02 to 08-08 window the incremental rollup went from 3.06 to roughly 108 seconds per recipient -- the [[FIX-884]] mechanism (a heap page loses its all-visible mark if any tuple on it is dead, so index-only scans degrade to per-row heap fetches). A manual VACUUM (ANALYZE) of all six on 08-08 20:46 took 8.4s total and returned every one to 100 percent all-visible. Fix shape: per-table autovacuum_vacuum_scale_factor 0.05 and autovacuum_analyze_scale_factor 0.02 on all six, matching the existing convention, plus a scheduled VACUUM (ANALYZE) after each rollup window in the style of jobids 6 / 30 / 31. Note a vacuum tail cannot live inside the procedure: VACUUM cannot run in a transaction block and a plpgsql body is always inside one, even immediately after a COMMIT. Cross-ref [[FIX-943]], [[FIX-884]], [[FIX-969]], [[FIX-1002]]. <!--id:FIX-1003-->
- [x] 🟠 M — **Route the scheduled donor rollup to the bulk regime -- cron 24 uses the slow path while the fast one is live** — Measured on prod in the same week on the same instance: donor_rollup_rebuild_bulk() ([[FIX-974]]) cleared 9,086 recipients in 926s -- 0.102 s/recipient, data_sync_log 4b0e6d00, 2026-08-07 02:14 -- while cron jobid 24's per-recipient refresh_official_donor_rollup_incremental() did 600 recipients in 12,622s on 08-07 09:00 (21.0 s/recipient) and roughly 108 s/recipient by 08-08. That is 206x at the good end and over 1000x at the bad end. The fast path is live on prod and the scheduled job does not call it. Consequence beyond speed: a budget-bounded incremental run does not converge, because the durable watermark only advances when a sweep COMPLETES with zero chunk failures. last_indexed_at has been pinned at 2026-08-06 06:39:22 while every run since has closed status=partial, so the dirty set is rebuilt from the same stale watermark every firing. Bounding the run ([[FIX-1002]]) caps how long one firing can degrade the site but makes convergence strictly worse; the regime split is the piece that actually finishes the work. Deliberately NOT built alongside FIX-1002: its necessity is gated on the first firing after the [[FIX-1003]] vacuum. If the incremental path returns to its 2026-07-31 rate of 1.35 s/recipient with the arms at 100 percent all-visible, a 647-recipient dirty set costs about 15 minutes and this is unnecessary. Decide from that measurement, not from this bullet. Cross-ref [[FIX-974]], [[FIX-973]], [[FIX-1002]], [[FIX-1003]], [[FIX-969]]. <!--id:FIX-1004-->
- [ ] 🟠 S — **donor_rollup_rebuild_bulk()'s _drb_* staging tables have no vacuum owner — FIX-1004 would activate them** — [[FIX-1003]] gave vacuum ownership to the six arms the INCREMENTAL path rewrites, plus the chord matviews and pipeline_state. It deliberately did not touch the four persistent staging tables the BULK path rewrites, because cron 24 does not currently call it: `_drb_targets` (10 pages), `_drb_chunk_fe` (432), `_drb_donor` (860) and `_drb_fe` (35,118). Catalog-derived from `donor_rollup_rebuild_bulk()`'s write set on prod 2026-08-09; all four are relkind='r', none carries an autovacuum override, and none has ever had a manual VACUUM (`last_vacuum` NULL on all four). They read clean today (0% dead, 100% all-visible) only because the bulk path last ran 2026-08-07 and autovacuum happened to catch up. [[FIX-1004]] routes cron 24 to `donor_rollup_rebuild_bulk()`, which turns these into twice-daily bulk-rewrite targets with no owner — i.e. it recreates exactly the [[FIX-943]] violation [[FIX-1003]] just closed, on a different set of tables, and `_drb_fe` at 35,118 pages is larger than four of the six arms. Fix shape: fold the four into the same treatment before or with [[FIX-1004]] — scale-factor overrides on `_drb_fe`/`_drb_donor`/`_drb_chunk_fe` and a threshold-led override on the tiny `_drb_targets` (the trigger is threshold + scale_factor x reltuples, so on a 10-page table the default threshold of 50 is the whole trigger and a scale factor alone changes nothing). `supabase/tests/verify_fix1003.sql` derives its arm set by walking the call tree from `refresh_official_donor_rollup_incremental()`, so extending that walk to `donor_rollup_rebuild_bulk()` makes the test cover these automatically. Cross-ref [[FIX-943]], [[FIX-884]], [[FIX-1002]]. <!--id:FIX-1005-->
- [x] 🟠 S — **Parked bulk sweep at chunk 16/32 has a sweep_target OLDER than the incremental watermark — resuming may leave a gap** — Flagged during [[FIX-1003]], deliberately not acted on. `pipeline_state.donor_rollup_bulk_sweep` on prod reads `{"mode":"dirty","chunks":32,"chunk_cursor":16,"sweep_target":"2026-08-08 03:35:26.632984+00","sweep_started_at":"2026-08-08 20:15:18.36423+00"}` — a bulk sweep parked half-done. Its `sweep_target` (2026-08-08 03:35:26) is now OLDER than `donor_rollup_watermark.last_indexed_at` (2026-08-08 22:59:39), which the incremental path advanced past it on the 2026-08-09 09:00 firing. The unexamined question is what resuming from chunk 16 actually means once the incremental path has moved beyond the target the sweep was planned against: the remaining 16 chunks were computed against a recipient set frozen at the older target, so resuming may re-rebuild recipients the incremental path has already done (wasteful but harmless) or — the case that matters — skip recipients dirtied between the two timestamps, leaving a silent correctness gap in the rollups rather than closing one. Its own `data_sync_log` row is status='reaped'. Decide before touching it: either (a) discard the parked state and let the incremental path converge on its own, now that [[FIX-1002]] bounds each firing and [[FIX-1003]] keeps the arms unbloated (the 2026-08-09 firings did 247 and 1,447 recipients to completion at ~5.7 s/recipient), or (b) recompute the sweep against the current watermark rather than resuming a stale plan. Do NOT simply resume from chunk 16. Related but distinct from [[FIX-1004]], which is about which regime cron 24 should call, not about this parked state. Cross-ref [[FIX-944]], [[FIX-965]]. <!--id:FIX-1007-->
- [x] 🔴 M — **FEC bulk writers rewrite ~95% unchanged rows every Sunday — skip no-op upserts** — The weekly FEC file is a cumulative SUPERSET, so every Sunday run re-upserts the WHOLE cycle, but MEASURED on prod 2026-08-09 only 0.5-6.3% of the rows written are new (`created_at` histogram, cycle 2026 `financial_relationships`: 6,841-86,818 new rows per weekly run against 1,375,438 total; individual donor entities 15k-45k new against 3.42M). The other ~95% were byte-identical re-upserts that still cost a FULL write. WHY THAT IS EXPENSIVE: `financial_relationships` takes **exactly zero** HOT updates (`n_tup_upd` 4,911,666 / `n_tup_hot_upd` **0**), because the unpredicated `financial_relationships_updated_at` btree (159 MB) indexes a column the `set_updated_at()` BEFORE UPDATE trigger changes on EVERY update — so HOT is impossible by construction and every upsert rewrites all 16 indexes (4,855 MB) and leaves a dead tuple. `financial_entities` carries the SAME trigger but does NOT index `updated_at`, which is exactly why it gets 27.06% HOT and FR gets 0.00%. Fix: `BulkUpsertSpec.skipUnchangedRows` in `packages/data/src/lib/direct-pg-upsert.ts` emits `ON CONFLICT ... DO UPDATE SET ... WHERE (any SET column IS DISTINCT FROM EXCLUDED)`, so an unchanged conflict row is left completely alone — no heap write, no index maintenance, no dead tuple, no WAL, no `updated_at` bump. The predicate always covers EXACTLY the SET list (never a caller-chosen subset: a SET column outside the predicate could change silently). Wired into all four fec-bulk writers; the donor writer additionally re-reads the ids that RETURNING no longer emits (an unchanged row returns no row) on the same direct-pg connection, which also closes a pre-existing gap where [[FIX-754]]'s mid-stage resume never got RETURNING rows for chunks committed by an earlier run. MEASURED before/after, local prod-clone, same table, same 4,000-row chunk, 4 alternating slices per variant, source rows pre-materialised so only the upsert is timed: `financial_relationships` 6,941 ms -> 1,289 ms per chunk (1.735 -> 0.322 ms/row, **5.39x**), `financial_entities` 1,377 ms -> 433 ms (0.344 -> 0.108 ms/row, **3.18x**), with `rows_written = 0` on every skip slice confirming the no-op. Gate impact: a from-zero cycle-2026 indiv run costs 348.9 min of writer work at the prod pg_stat_statements means (840,338 FE rows @ 11.246 ms + 1,375,438 FR rows @ 8.349 ms, both from 4,000-row chunk samples) against `fec-backfill.yml`'s 350-min cap — it does NOT fit. With this fix it is ~123 min (65% headroom), and even assuming the donor stage gets ZERO benefit it is ~200 min (43% headroom), so the FR half alone clears the 25%-headroom bar. Cross-ref [[FIX-995]], [[FIX-999]], [[FIX-943]]. <!--id:FIX-1008-->
- [x] 🟠 S — **fec_bulk donor writer overwrites the authoritative all-cycle total_donated_cents with a cycle-only value every run** — Surfaced by [[FIX-1008]]'s skip-unchanged measurement, and it directly caps that fix's payoff on the donor stage. `upsertIndividualDonorsBatch` writes `total_donated_cents` = the donor's aggregate FOR THE CYCLE BEING INGESTED. The post-cycle `rebuild_financial_entity_donation_totals()` ([[FIX-269]]) then overwrites it with the live SUM across ALL cycles — which is the authoritative value, and the reason that rebuild exists at all ('replaces the lossy per-cycle overwrite that left multi-cycle individuals with only the latest cycle's total'). So for every multi-cycle donor the pipeline writes a value it KNOWS is wrong, and repairs it minutes later. Two costs. (1) It is pure write thrash on the platform's hottest table. (2) It defeats [[FIX-1008]]'s predicate: the stored all-cycle value never equals the cycle-only value being written, so those donors are rewritten every single week no matter what. MEASURED on prod 2026-08-09 (sample of 2,576 individual donors with donation rows): **75.8% are single-cycle**, so ~24% of donors are permanently un-skippable for this reason alone — [[FIX-1008]]'s donor-stage skip rate is bounded at ~72% (0.758 x ~0.95 unchanged) instead of ~95%. The machinery to fix it already exists and is already correct: [[FIX-700]]'s `skipAggregateOverwrite` drops `total_donated_cents`/`total_received_cents` from the INSERT column list entirely, so an existing donor keeps their current aggregate and a new donor takes the `BIGINT NOT NULL DEFAULT 0`; it is currently applied only to SCOPED runs. Direction: apply it to unscoped runs too and let the totals rebuild be the single writer of that column. Care needed on one point before doing it — confirm the rebuild covers every donor an unscoped run can introduce, including donors whose only rows are in a cycle the rebuild's predicate does not reach, otherwise a brand-new donor could sit at 0 until the next full rebuild. Cross-ref [[FIX-1008]], [[FIX-700]], [[FIX-269]]. <!--id:FIX-1009-->
- [ ] 🟡 S — **FIX-999's below-cursor-count invariant does not converge — re-scope now that FIX-1008 makes the re-pass cheap** — Correction to the design sketched in [[FIX-999]], found while sizing it 2026-08-09. The proposed completeness guard was: store the count of rows at or below the content-key cursor, and on resume re-run that slice if the count differs. That guard is correct but, on this data, it is equivalent to no cursor at all. The sort key must be the conflict arbiter (`donor_fingerprint`, or `(from_id, to_id)`) to be stable across a republish, and those key spaces are effectively RANDOM with respect to newness — a donor who first appears this week gets a fingerprint uniformly distributed over the whole range. So a republished superset almost always adds rows BELOW the cursor, the below-count always differs, the slice is always re-run, and the re-run slice IS all the progress the cursor bought. Net saving on a mid-file cursor: zero. Nor can the hole be closed by probing the DB for which below-cursor keys already exist — that finds rows that are ABSENT but is blind to rows that are PRESENT WITH A CHANGED VALUE (a donor who gave again), so it would silently drop real updates. What actually closes it is [[FIX-1008]]: with `skipUnchangedRows`, re-running the below-cursor slice costs an arbiter probe plus a predicate evaluation and writes nothing, MEASURED at 5.39x cheaper on `financial_relationships` and 3.18x on `financial_entities`. That removes the need for the below-count bookkeeping, the existence probe, and the sweep ordering entirely — the correct resume becomes 'replay the whole file, let the DB skip the no-ops'. It also removes the ORIGINAL motivation: with [[FIX-1008]] a from-zero cycle-2026 indiv run fits one dispatch (~123 min of writer work, 65% headroom, vs 348.9 min before), so the cursor no longer has to survive a republish for the cycle to converge. Recommendation: leave [[FIX-999]] OPEN but re-scope it to 'confirm from a completed post-FIX-1008 dispatch whether a content-keyed cursor is still needed at all' rather than building the offset-to-key conversion on spec. Do not build the below-count guard as originally specified. Cross-ref [[FIX-999]], [[FIX-1008]], [[FIX-754]]. <!--id:FIX-1010-->
- [ ] 🟠 M — **fec_bulk has no freshness watch at all — invisible to the FIX-977 registry while FIX-998's hold is on** — Carried over from the cc-prompt-49 investigation and re-confirmed 2026-08-09; filed rather than fixed because the clean version is a registry change, not a one-liner. `list_scheduled_rollup_pipelines(90)` returns 13 pipelines and NONE of them is `fec_bulk`: the census selects on `metadata->>'source' = 'pg_cron'` and fec_bulk's sync-log rows carry a NULL source (it runs from GHA, not pg_cron). So fec_bulk could stop ingesting for a month without any monitoring surface noticing. That is not hypothetical right now — [[FIX-998]] deliberately holds fec_bulk off the nightly until a completed in-budget run proves [[FIX-995]], and that hold is itself invisible: nothing distinguishes 'intentionally held' from 'silently broken'. `nightly_cron` reports the PHASE, not the pipeline, so the fec-phase row tells you the phase ran, not that fec_bulk ingested anything. The real freshness signal already exists and is cheap: `pipeline_state.fec_indiv_watermark` carries a per-cycle `last_modified`, and FEC republishes weekly, so 'watermark for the active cycle is more than ~10 days behind FEC's published Last-Modified' is a precise, low-false-positive staleness test. Two candidate shapes, deliberately not chosen here: (a) widen the registry census so a GHA-sourced pipeline can register with its own cadence expectation instead of being filtered out by the `pg_cron` source predicate — the right fix, but it touches the [[FIX-977]] registry contract and every consumer of it; (b) a standalone watermark-freshness check reporting through the existing daily canary, which is smaller but adds a second monitoring path for one pipeline. Prefer (a) if the registry is being touched for other reasons anyway. Whichever lands must treat an intentional hold as a distinct state from staleness, or it just trades a silent failure for a standing false alarm. Cross-ref [[FIX-977]], [[FIX-998]], [[FIX-995]]. <!--id:FIX-1011-->
- [ ] 🟡 S — **financial_entities carries 310 MB of never-scanned indexes that every non-HOT update still maintains** — Found in the [[FIX-1008]] write-surface census. A non-HOT UPDATE inserts a new entry into EVERY index on the table, whether or not that index's columns changed — so an index nothing reads is still paid for on every write. `pg_stat_user_indexes` on prod 2026-08-09 (counters cumulative, `stats_reset` NULL, so this is the full post-cutover window) shows five `financial_entities` indexes with **idx_scan = 0**: `financial_entities_donated_positive` 145 MB, `financial_entities_recipient_count_idx` 70 MB, `financial_entities_individual_state_idx` 61 MB, `financial_entities_canonical_name_type` 19 MB, `financial_entities_canonical` 15 MB — **310 MB across 5 of 19 indexes**, never once scanned. The donor stage does 840,338 upserts per cycle and ~73% of its updates are non-HOT, so this is real write amplification on the pipeline's most expensive stage, and it competes for a 256 MB `shared_buffers` that the working set already dwarfs. `financial_entities_individual_state_idx` deserves particular attention: it is an EXPRESSION index over `(metadata->>'state')`, and `metadata` is rewritten by the donor upsert on every conflict, so it re-evaluates the expression on every non-HOT write and it is a HOT blocker in its own right. Do NOT batch-drop these on the strength of idx_scan alone — verify each against the read paths first, because a zero-scan index can still be (a) a uniqueness constraint, (b) an ON CONFLICT arbiter, or (c) serving a route that has simply not run since the counters started. None of these five is unique or an arbiter, which is what makes them candidates. Note the contrast on the other table: `financial_relationships_usaspending_unique` is also idx_scan = 0 at 485 MB but MUST stay — it is the usaspending pipeline's conflict arbiter, and that pipeline is manual-dispatch-only since [[FIX-740]]. Cross-ref [[FIX-1008]], [[FIX-884]]. <!--id:FIX-1012-->
- [x] 🟠 S — **fec_bulk bulk-rewrites financial_relationships with no vacuum owner — FIX-975 counted it as OWNED via a path cron never calls** — The [[FIX-943]] standing rule is that a script which bulk-rewrites a table ends by vacuuming what it rewrote. `runFecBulkPipeline()` bulk-rewrites `financial_relationships` and `financial_entities` on every Sunday nightly and every `fec-backfill.yml` dispatch, and issues no VACUUM at all — `grep -rn VACUUM packages/data/src/pipelines/fec-bulk/` returns zero hits. `financial_entities` is covered anyway by [[FIX-975]]'s scheduled `fe-vacuum-analyze` (jobid 30, Sun+Wed 02:00 UTC). `financial_relationships` is NOT: prod `cron.job` on 2026-08-09 carries NINE `VACUUM (ANALYZE)` jobs — jobids 6, 30, 31, 32, 33, 34, 35, 36, 37, covering entity_connections, financial_entities, donor_party_rollup_mv and the six [[FIX-1003]] donor-rollup arms — and `financial_relationships` is not among them, despite being the largest and most index-heavy table on the instance (448,249 pages, 10.3M rows, 4,855 MB across 16 indexes per [[FIX-1008]]). FIX-975's own header block asserts `financial_relationships vacuum_count 5 -> 96.8% OWNED (FIX-974)`, and that was true when written — but the owner is `donor_rollup_rebuild_bulk()`, which cron jobid 24 does not call, and getting it called is precisely what [[FIX-1004]] is still open to do. So FR's vacuum ownership is nominal: its only owner is a code path nothing currently schedules, which is why the FIX-975 census counted it as covered and no later pass rechecked. MEASURED after the 2026-08-09 fec-backfill dispatch (GHA run 31322394244, cycles=2026, status complete 16:55:48Z, 1,711,563 rows processed, ~269k actually written post-[[FIX-1008]]): FR `last_vacuum` = 2026-08-06 00:56:29 — three days stale and predating the run entirely — with 39,687 dead tuples and relallvisible 423,758 / relpages 448,249 = **94.5% all-visible**. `financial_entities` by contrast was vacuumed at 16:55:22, 26 seconds before the pipeline closed, and reads 98.7%. Honest scoping: 0.38% dead is not urgent today, and the reason it is that low is [[FIX-1008]]'s skip-unchanged predicate — this run wrote ~269k rows where a pre-1008 run would have written ~2.2M, so it left roughly an eighth of the dead tuples. That is an argument for filing now rather than later, because the mitigation is a write-VOLUME reduction and not an ownership fix: it evaporates the moment a from-zero cycle, a genuinely-changed republish, or a new cycle's first ingest lands full write volume. FR's own override (`autovacuum_vacuum_scale_factor=0.02`) still permits ~206k dead tuples before autovacuum fires, and [[FIX-884]] is the measured precedent for what a few percent dead does to this exact table's index-only scans (0.9% all-visible -> 34,534 Heap Fetches, 20.5s of a 22.1s query). Fix shape: an `fr-vacuum-analyze` cron job in the style of jobids 6/30/31, scheduled AFTER the Sunday FEC window rather than before it, so it follows the rewrite it exists to clean up. Size it first — FIX-975 measured financial_entities at 83.9s and FR has ~2.1x its pages, so this is the expensive one and it must land off-peak per the no-heavy-prod-ops-during-active-hours rule. A pipeline-tail VACUUM instead of a cron job is the weaker option and should not be the primary: `fec-backfill.yml` can be SIGTERM'd at its 350-min cap and the nightly at its 150-min cap, and a tail that does not run on the kill path is exactly the gap FIX-943's precedent paragraph describes — the scheduled job is unconditional. Cross-ref [[FIX-943]], [[FIX-884]], [[FIX-975]], [[FIX-974]], [[FIX-1004]], [[FIX-1008]], [[FIX-1003]]. <!--id:FIX-1013-->
- [x] 🟠 S — **R2 cache freshness compares our upload time to FEC's publish time — a republish mid-run can pin stale bytes for a full ingest cycle** — `downloadWithR2Cache` in `packages/data/src/pipelines/fec-bulk/util.ts` decides cache freshness with `r2Fresh = r2Lm >= fecLm`, where `r2Lm` is the R2 OBJECT's own LastModified — i.e. when WE finished uploading — and `fecLm` is FEC's publish time. Those answer different questions, and the object timestamp answers the wrong one. The race: HEAD FEC at T0 and get the old Last-Modified; download the old bytes; FEC republishes at T1; our upload of the OLD bytes completes at T2 > T1. R2 now holds superseded content stamped NEWER than FEC's current file, so `r2Fresh` is true and every subsequent run is served the stale copy without ever re-reading FEC. NEAR-MISS MEASURED 2026-08-09 — the sequence happened, only the upload did not. `fec-backfill.yml` dispatch (GHA run 31322394244) HEADed indiv26.zip at 15:57:17Z and got `Sun, 02 Aug 2026 15:45:35 GMT` / etag `...-238`; FEC republished at **16:03:26 GMT** (live HEAD: `Last-Modified: Sun, 09 Aug 2026 16:03:26 GMT`, etag `"9167f97b6dfbd1c58e4f4f50997b8c2f-246"`, Content-Length **2,061,619,898**); the run's R2 uploads drained 16:54:40-16:55:32Z, comfortably after T1. It did not fire only because indiv26 took the CACHE branch that run (R2 `fec/2026/indiv26.zip` LastModified 2026-08-06T06:41:27Z, 1,995,728,131 bytes) and a cache hit returns `r2UploadPromise: null`, so nothing was re-uploaded. Had the sizing gone the other way — indiv26 fetched from FEC and uploaded at ~16:55 — the bucket would now hold the Aug 2 bytes stamped 16:55, newer than FEC's 16:03, and every run until the next publish would be served them. Four of the six cycle-2026 files DID re-upload with today's timestamps (cm26 15:55:54, weball26 15:55:53, cn26 15:55:54, ccl26 15:57:17), so the upload path is live and routinely lands inside the window. BLAST RADIUS IS ONE INGEST CYCLE, NOT PERMANENT — and that is the right way to size it. A cache HIT does not re-upload, so `r2Lm` stays frozen at T2 rather than ratcheting forward; FEC's next weekly publish (Sundays ~15:20 UTC) produces a Last-Modified later than T2 and freshness resolves itself with no intervention. So the cost is bounded at one full ingest cycle of silently-stale donor data — which is EXACTLY the outcome [[FIX-903]] exists to prevent (prod donor data a full week behind FEC, one real ingest per two weeks), arriving through the cache layer instead of the watermark. Same failure, different door, and unlike FIX-903 this one leaves no signal: the run logs `complete`, the row counts look plausible, and only the `fec_bulk_run_state` lm value betrays it. FIX SHAPE — compare like with like, and add size. Store FEC's OWN reported headers as R2 object metadata at upload time (`x-amz-meta-fec-last-modified`, `x-amz-meta-fec-content-length`, `x-amz-meta-fec-etag`) and make freshness `stored_fec_* == live_fec_*`, so the comparison is FEC-reported-then vs FEC-reported-now and the R2 object's own timestamp drops out of the decision entirely. Content-Length is the cheap belt-and-braces and should NOT be omitted as redundant: today's two files are 1,995,728,131 vs 2,061,619,898 bytes — **65,891,767 apart, 3.3%** — so a size check alone would have caught this instance, and it costs one integer compare. CAUTION on ETag: R2's object ETag is NOT comparable to FEC's, because multipart chunk sizes differ between our uploader and theirs — our cached indiv26 reads `"44e6967bdbad3e73b09964f1e7677b28-119"` against FEC's `-238`/`-246` for the same class of file. That is precisely why the stored value must be FEC's etag as a metadata string, never the object's own. Cross-ref [[FIX-903]], [[FIX-192]], [[FIX-1010]], [[FIX-995]]. <!--id:FIX-1014-->
- [x] 🟡 S — **fixes-sync.mjs carries a literal NUL byte, so every repo-wide grep treats the FIXES-workflow script as binary** — `scripts/fixes-sync.mjs` builds a cache key as ``${trunk}<U+0000>${sha}`` where the source was meant to carry the two-character escape `\0`. What is actually stored in the file at **byte offset 8362 is a literal U+0000 control character**. Node parses it fine — it is a legal character inside a template literal and the cache key behaves exactly as intended — so nothing fails and nothing has ever surfaced it at runtime. **The entire cost is in search.** ripgrep, `git grep` and the Claude Code Grep tool all sniff for NUL to classify a file as binary, so any repo-wide content search that would match this file returns a binary-file notice instead of the matching lines. Measured 2026-08-09: `grep -n 'closes-as' scripts/fixes-sync.mjs` returns `Binary file scripts/fixes-sync.mjs matches`, and the same query through the Grep tool returns `binary file matches (found \0 byte around offset 8362)`. Reading the file with `node -e` or the Read tool works normally, so it is not corrupt — it is simply invisible to the one access pattern people actually use on it. **Why this is worth more than its size suggests:** this is the script that owns the whole FIXES workflow — the `Fixes:` / `Closes:` / `Verified[FIX-NNN]:` trailer grammar, the closure vocabulary, the [[FIX-461]] trunk-ancestry guard, the done.log column format. It is the single most likely file in the repo to be grepped for 'how does trailer X actually work', and it is the single file in the repo that silently refuses to answer. Every future session of this workflow pays the toll: the real behaviour has to be recovered by reading the file whole or decoding it through node, and a session that greps, gets a binary-file notice and moves on may reasonably conclude the pattern is absent. Encountered exactly that way twice in the 2026-08-09 closure session. **Fix:** replace the literal control character with the escape sequence `\0`; or, cleaner and leaving no NUL in the file at all, use a separator that cannot collide with either operand — both are ref/sha-shaped, so `|` or `::` is unambiguous. One character either way. **Verify:** `node -e "console.log(require('fs').readFileSync('scripts/fixes-sync.mjs').indexOf(0))"` must print `-1`; then `grep -c 'closes-as' scripts/fixes-sync.mjs` must return a count rather than a binary-file notice; then `pnpm fixes:test` and `pnpm fixes:check` to prove the trunk-ancestry cache still behaves. Worth sweeping for the same typo elsewhere in the same breath — `grep -rlP '\x00' --exclude-dir=node_modules --exclude-dir=.git .` over tracked text files. Found while auditing the trailer grammar during the FIX-1004/999/944/920 closure pass. Cross-ref [[FIX-1001]] (the four-digit regex widening, same file), [[FIX-314]], [[FIX-369]], [[FIX-461]]. <!--id:FIX-1015-->
- [ ] 🟠 M — **FIXES.md is both hand-edited and machine-edited — derive status from done.log instead of flipping checkboxes in place** — Filed 2026-08-09 out of the cc-prompt-55 trunk-guard work; this is the real fix, of which the guard is only the detector. **The class.** docs/FIXES.md carries two kinds of content under one writer contract: bullet prose, which only Craig edits by hand, and the leading checkbox, which fixes:sync flips in place. An in-place machine edit of a hand-edited file is the entire concurrent-write class — syncFixesMd reads the whole file, maps every line, and writes the whole file back, so any hand edit that lands inside that window is silently reverted with no conflict, no error, and a diff that looks intentional. docs/done.log does NOT have the problem, and the reason is structural rather than lucky: it is append-only, and appends from two sessions merge where in-place edits do not. **Proposal.** Make done.log the single source of truth for status: FIXES.md holds prose and the id marker only, and fixes:check COMPUTES the checked set from the log rather than reading it back out of the markdown. Then FIXES.md is only ever hand-edited, done.log is only ever appended, and the class disappears rather than being detected. **Two known bugs die with it.** (1) fixes:sync silently no-ops its checkbox flips on a CRLF working copy — it normalizes CRLF on read but the bullet regex still has to match, and a mismatch is indistinguishable from nothing-to-do. (2) The state-in-two-places reconciliation that produced checkboxesFlipped: 2 against four closures on 2026-08-09; with status derived there is nothing to reconcile. **Cost, honestly.** A one-time migration of the current checkbox state into the log for any bullet whose [x] has no completion row, plus updating every reader of [x] — fixes-clean.mjs (which selects source bullets by checkbox), fixes-archive.mjs, fixes-housekeep.mjs and session-check.mjs. Do NOT change done.log format as part of this; the append-only contract is what makes the whole thing work. **Relationship to the guard.** scripts/lib/trunk-guard.mjs now aborts any of these scripts when origin/main or HEAD moves between their read and their write, which converts the silent revert into a loud refusal — necessary, and strictly weaker than removing the in-place write. Keep the guard afterwards regardless: fix:add still appends to FIXES.md and fixes-archive still rewrites it. Cross-ref [[FIX-461]], [[FIX-462]], [[FIX-361]], [[FIX-314]], [[FIX-369]], [[FIX-1015]]. <!--id:FIX-1016-->
- [x] 🔴 S — **pg_cron background-worker starvation: 'job startup timeout' has killed 29 firings, incl. 12 of 12 on the FIX-1003 arm vacuums** — Measured on prod 2026-08-11 (read-only). `cron.job_run_details` holds **29 lifetime firings that never reached their command**, all with `return_message='job startup timeout'`, spread over 16 distinct jobs from 2026-07-26 to 2026-08-10. This is not incidental scheduling noise — it is a standing background-worker shortage, and it silently disables whole remediations. **Receipts.** (a) The six FIX-1003 arm-vacuum jobs (jobids 32-37: odr-mv / treemap-individuals / odt / osdr / osar / odbt): **2 firings each, 0 successes, 12 failures, 12 startup timeouts, `last_success` NULL for all six.** FIX-1003's vacuum ownership has never once executed on prod. (b) `donor-rollup-refresh` (jobid 24): 33 runs, 23 ok, 10 failed, 5 of them startup timeouts. (c) Bursts, not one-offs: 08-10 11:05-11:18 (all six arm vacuums, each overlapping `entity-connection-stats-rebuild` + `rebuild-ec-incremental-mon`) and 08-10 14:05-14:18 (six again, up to 5 concurrent jobs). Earlier: 07-26, 07-27, 08-03, 08-04 (x3), 08-05 (x2), 08-06 (x3), 08-07, 08-08 (x2). (d) The launcher is visibly behind, not just the workers: `treemap-individuals-vacuum-analyze` (scheduled :10) and `odt-vacuum-analyze` (scheduled :12) both have `start_time = 2026-08-10 14:12:47.405328` — the same non-scheduled second. **Mechanism (pg_settings).** `max_worker_processes = 6` (configuration file) while `cron.max_running_jobs = 32` (default) — pg_cron is configured to promise 5x more concurrent jobs than the postmaster can ever supply. `max_parallel_workers = 2`, `max_parallel_workers_per_gather = 1`, `max_parallel_maintenance_workers = 1`, `shared_buffers = 256MB`, `max_connections = 60`. On a fully idle box (2026-08-11 02:59, nothing scheduled running) `pg_stat_activity` already shows **3 of the 6 slots permanently held**: two `wait_event_type='Extension'` bgworkers (pids 1187/1188) plus `LogicalLauncherMain`. That leaves 3. The moment a query goes parallel — and both 6h EC rebuilds do — `max_parallel_workers=2` takes two more, leaving pg_cron **one** slot to launch into. Note autovacuum workers are a separate allowance and are NOT implicated. **Honest counter-evidence:** three startup timeouts (07-26 09:00, 08-06 06:00, 08-06 06:30) had **zero** overlapping cron jobs, so cron-vs-cron contention is not the whole story — parallel-query and extension workers are part of the same 6-slot budget. **Proposed remediation, NOT applied (postmaster-context GUCs need a restart — Craig's call).** (1) Raise `max_worker_processes` 6 -> 12; this is the actual fix and everything else is mitigation. (2) Lower `cron.max_running_jobs` 32 -> 4 so the scheduler stops promising capacity the cluster cannot honour. (3) No-restart mitigation available today: move the 11:05-11:18 / 14:05-14:18 arm vacuums off the Mon+Wed 6h EC rebuild windows via `cron.alter_job` by name (overlaps [[FIX-969]] deconfliction). (4) Make `check_cron_job_health()` ([[FIX-968]]) escalate 'zero lifetime successes' as a distinct, louder condition than 'missed a run' — six jobs that have NEVER succeeded currently read the same as one that skipped once. **Blocks [[FIX-1013]]:** adding a seventh vacuum job for `financial_relationships` would just startup-timeout like the other six. Surfaced by the [[FIX-1018]] investigation; see docs/audits/2026-08-11-fix1018-diagnosis.md sections 1 and 4. <!--id:FIX-1022-->
- [x] 🟠 S — **financial-entity-totals-incremental pg_cron job has never been enabled — total_donated_cents is refreshed monthly, not weekly** — Surfaced by [[FIX-1009]]'s coverage gate, measured on the local prod clone 2026-08-12: `SELECT jobname, active FROM cron.job` returns `financial-entity-totals-incremental | 0 9 * * 2 | f`. Migration `20260704000000_fix702_726_incremental_financial_entity_totals.sql` created BOTH totals jobs PAUSED on purpose (the FIX-704 discipline — register, run one supervised off-peak CALL, then enable), and `20260705000200_fix736_recipient_count_fr_derived.sql` line 730 later enabled `financial-entity-totals-reconcile` — but nothing ever enabled the INCREMENTAL one. So the weekly dirty-set refresh of `financial_entities.total_donated_cents` / `total_received_cents` has never run in the automated path, and since FIX-702/726 removed the pipeline's inline `rebuild_financial_entity_donation_totals()` call the ONLY automated writer of those columns is the monthly `reconcile_financial_entity_totals()` (1st of month, 12:00 UTC). Consequence: an existing donor's aggregate can be up to ~31 days stale after a Sunday FEC ingest, where the design intended ~2 days. [[FIX-1009]] is unaffected by design — it keeps the INSERT value so a brand-new donor is correct on arrival and needs no rebuild — but every EXISTING multi-cycle donor now waits for the monthly sweep instead of the weekly one. Before enabling: this is a prod pg_cron change in a shared 09:00 UTC window that already carries `donor-rollup-refresh` (09:00 daily) — check for overlap and the [[FIX-943]] bulk-rewrite vacuum rule first, and confirm the reconcile has been running clean. Verify on PROD before acting; the `active=false` reading is clone-measured and prod's cron.job may have been altered by hand in a supervised run. Cross-ref [[FIX-1009]], [[FIX-702]], [[FIX-726]], [[FIX-736]], [[FIX-269]]. <!--id:FIX-1024-->
- [x] 🔴 S — **No external request-path monitor — the 08-11 outage ran 14h undetected while GHA laundered the signal into 'cancelled'** — **Shipped in this session; filed for the record and for the residual gap it does NOT close.** The 2026-08-11 outage ran **14 hours** before a human noticed. The signal to catch it already existed and was structurally incapable of paging anyone. **Measured 2026-08-12 from this workflow's own run history:** during the outage window the five `platform-snapshot` runs at 08:28, 09:31, 10:27, 11:17 and 12:00 UTC all hung against the unreachable site and were cut off by their `timeout-minutes: 5`. **GitHub reports a timeout-expired job as `cancelled`, not `failed`, and sends no notification for a cancelled run** — verified on run 31478090511, which started 09:31:46 and ended 09:36:49 (5m03s) with conclusion `cancelled`. Five consecutive cancelled runs spanning exactly the degraded window, zero emails. That is the whole finding: this is **alerting on an existing signal, not new monitoring**. **What shipped:** a `request-path-probe` job added to `.github/workflows/platform-snapshot.yml` (Craig's call — no new scheduled workflow; GHA cron is unreliable at tight cadences). It runs independently of the snapshot job, curls three surfaces verified 200 + `X-Vercel-Cache: MISS` on 2026-08-12 so none can answer from the CDN while the DB is unreachable (`/`, `/officials`, `/api/officials/<id>/responsiveness`), retries 3 x 20s with `--max-time 25`, and **exits non-zero itself** rather than hanging into a timeout. **The API endpoint is `/responsiveness` and NOT `/summary` deliberately: `/summary` FAILS OPEN** — its own header documents that the `summary: null` variants (kill switch, spend cap, no data, **DB error**) return unstamped 200s, so it is blind to exactly the outage this job catches; `/responsiveness` returns 500 on a DB error (route.ts:36, :111). Both verified against prod. Probe verified end-to-end in both directions before commit: 3/3 200s exit 0, unreachable host exits 1 with the `::error::` annotation. **Known limit:** the two page routes are not proven to fail closed on a DB-down-but-Vercel-up partial outage; on the 08-11 failure mode (Cloudflare 522, origin unreachable) every endpoint fails, but a partial outage rests on `/responsiveness` alone. Three properties are load-bearing and must survive future edits: (1) `concurrency` was moved from workflow level onto the `trigger` job only — a workflow-level `cancel-in-progress` makes the whole RUN `cancelled` and re-launders the signal; (2) the probe carries no concurrency group at all; (3) its worst case (~3.5 min) sits far inside `timeout-minutes: 10`, so it can only ever conclude `success` or `failure`. **The residual gap, stated plainly:** GHA cron does not honour its own schedule. Configured `*/10`, the **observed** gaps on 2026-08-11..12 were 38, 50, 56, 63, 87, 107 and 153 minutes (playbook D2 applied to GHA — read observed starts, never cron expressions). So detection latency is about **one hour, not minutes** — a ~10x improvement on 14 hours at zero new cost, and **not** minutes-scale monitoring. True minutes-scale detection means an external uptime service (e.g. UptimeRobot's free 5-minute checks): zero repo code, entirely in that service's dashboard, Craig can set it up whenever he wants. **Also unresolved:** GitHub routes scheduled-workflow failure mail to the account that last committed the workflow file, currently `Civitics Platform <civitics.platform@gmail.com>` (96c2204c). If that inbox is not watched the alert is decorative. Cross-ref [[FIX-281]], [[FIX-1021]], [[FIX-1022]], [[FIX-969]]. <!--id:FIX-1026-->
- [x] 🔴 M — **Weekly-MV regression: financial_entities has no vacuum owner covering the Sunday FEC window, and refresh_spending_totals rewrites 127,728 unchanged rows a week into it** — **Diagnosed and partially fixed in this session — the write amplifier is closed, the schedule gap is closed, and the causal claim is left falsifiable rather than asserted.** The 2026-08-11 07:00 UTC firing of `refresh_derived_mvs('weekly')` ran **21,802 s** and died on the 6h `statement_timeout` against a baseline series of **1,253 / 1,779 / 2,781 / 2,774 s**. **[[FIX-1021]] bounds the blast radius; this bullet is about why the units got slow.** **What the measurements rule OUT (all prod, quiet box, 2026-08-12 22:50-23:30 UTC).** (a) *Not contention.* `data_sync_log` and `cron.job_run_details` are both clean from 06:39:22 (rule-taggers-daily ended) to 13:03 — nothing else was on the box. The weekly was the FIRST domino, not a victim; the 13 startup timeouts at 07:30-12:00 are its consequence. (b) *Not the units drifting.* `chord_industry_flows_mv` measures **185.5 s** today against **205.1 s** measured 08-10 during the [[FIX-953]] apply — no drift. (c) *Not work_mem.* The procedure does `SET work_mem = '128MB'` against a role default of 256MB, and the unit-1 sort needs 216MB, so this looked like a spill cliff. It is not: **198.3 s at 128MB vs 185.5 s at 256MB, identical plan, zero Memoize evictions.** (d) *Not a plan flip.* Forcing the planner off the Memoize path (`enable_memoize=off`) gives a hash join at **155.5 s** — faster, not slower. **What the evidence points AT.** Four of the five weekly MVs pivot on an **Index Only Scan on `financial_entities_pkey`** — 815,293 Memoize'd loops on the chord_industry plan, 3,670,461 rows on the hash-join plan. Every one reads `Heap Fetches: 0` **only because `financial_entities` is currently all-visible**. And the weekly job sits at the **furthest possible point from a vacuum**: `fe-vacuum-analyze` (jobid 30) ran `0 2 * * 0,3`, so it fired **Sun 08-09 02:00 and not again until Wed 08-12 02:00** — while the Sunday FEC ingest (observed **15:55-16:55 and 22:28-23:43**) and the Mon 08-10 [[FIX-953]] merge both landed *inside* that gap. **The Sunday firing precedes the ingest it exists to clean up by ~14 hours.** [[FIX-884]] is this instance's measured precedent for exactly this access shape, and the same night [[FIX-1013]]'s one-shot vacuum of `financial_relationships` reproduced the mechanism at full strength on a sibling table: **7,952 ms -> 365 ms, Heap Fetches 129,415 -> 0, buffer reads 60,883 -> 439** (21.8x / 139x). **The self-reinforcing part.** Unit 6, `refresh_spending_totals()`, is an unbounded whole-table UPDATE of `financial_entities` — **127,728 rows rewritten every week, of which ZERO would change** (measured), across **19 indexes / 1,403 MB**. So the weekly job bloats the very table its own MVs index-only-scan, and its vacuum owner never covers the window. **What shipped:** (1) `refresh_spending_totals()` gains the [[FIX-1008]] skip-unchanged predicate — same rows read, no-op rows no longer written; (2) jobid 30 altered in place by name (playbook D3, preserving its run history) to `0 2 * * 0,1,3`, adding a Monday firing **after** the observed Sunday window while keeping Sunday for Saturday's `fec-backfill` dispatches. **What this does NOT establish, and the test that would.** The stats collector was reset by the 08-11 23:26 restart and `financial_entities` has since been vacuumed, so the degraded state **cannot be reproduced** — `pg_stat_statements` holds zero REFRESH rows and every `last_vacuum` reads NULL. The visibility mechanism is therefore a strong inference from plan shape + schedule receipts + the FIX-884/FIX-1013 precedents, **not a before/after of the incident itself**. **Falsifiable prediction:** with jobid 30 now firing Monday 02:00, the next Tuesday 07:00 weekly should complete in **~15-45 min**; if it blows up again with `financial_entities` freshly vacuumed, this diagnosis is wrong and the remaining suspect is unit-level cost under cold cache (`shared_buffers` = 256MB against a 3.5GB FR heap). Note jobid 10 is **paused** until that run is deliberately scheduled. Full writeup: `docs/audits/2026-08-12-weekly-mv-regression.md`. Cross-ref [[FIX-1021]], [[FIX-1013]], [[FIX-884]], [[FIX-1008]], [[FIX-943]], [[FIX-953]], [[FIX-1022]]. <!--id:FIX-1027-->
- [ ] 🟠 M — **14 of 15 procedures cannot close their own data_sync_log row on cancel — WHEN OTHERS does not match query_canceled** — **Found while fixing [[FIX-1021]]; the census is catalog-derived, not a hand-kept list.** PL/pgSQL's `EXCEPTION WHEN OTHERS` **matches every error type EXCEPT `query_canceled` and `assert_failure`**, and `statement_timeout` raises `query_canceled` (57014). So a procedure that opens a `data_sync_log` row with `status='running'`, COMMITs it, and relies on a trailing `UPDATE` to close it will have that `UPDATE` skipped entirely when the 6h axe (or any `pg_cancel_backend`, or a client disconnect) lands — the error blows through the handler and out of the procedure, leaving the row stranded `running` until the nightly reaper finds it. **This is not hypothetical and it is not one procedure.** It is exactly what happened to the 2026-08-11 07:00 `refresh_derived_mvs('weekly')` row, which sat `running` for **15 hours** until `reap_stale_sync_log` closed it at 08-12 04:08:28. **Census, prod 2026-08-13:** of the **15** procedures that write a `running` row AND use transaction control, **14 cannot close it on cancel** — `donor_rollup_rebuild_bulk`, `rebuild_entity_connection_stats`, `reconcile_donation_edge_orphans`, `reconcile_donor_party_rollup_orphans`, `reconcile_donor_rollup_orphans`, `reconcile_entity_connection_stats_orphans`, `reconcile_financial_entity_totals`, `refresh_donor_party_rollup_incremental`, `refresh_financial_entity_totals_incremental`, `refresh_official_donor_rollup_incremental`, `refresh_sector_affinity_from_tag_changes`, `refresh_treemap_individuals_global`, `run_entity_connections_rebuild`, `run_rule_taggers`. Only `refresh_derived_mvs` handles it, as of [[FIX-1021]]. Reproduce the census with `pg_get_functiondef(p.oid) LIKE '%WHEN query_canceled%'` over that set. **This is very likely the shared root cause of the stranded-`running` population** that [[FIX-990]] keeps re-discovering (two EC rows stranded from 08-10 alone), the stranded `treemap_individuals_global_refresh` row, and the recurring `fec_bulk` `reaped` rows — every one of those is a job that was cancelled or killed, not one that crashed. **Consequences beyond tidiness:** (1) a stranded `running` row makes the pipeline look alive to anything reading `data_sync_log`, which is what `list_scheduled_rollup_pipelines()` and `check_cron_job_health()` ([[FIX-968]], [[FIX-977]]) read for freshness — so a killed job masks itself as an in-flight one for up to the reaper's 60-minute stale window; (2) the reaper's own note is explicit that elapsed-since-`started_at` is an **upper bound, not a measurement**, so every reaped row permanently loses its true runtime; (3) advisory locks taken with `pg_try_advisory_lock` are released by session teardown so stampede guards still hold — that part is fine. **Fix shape, mirroring what [[FIX-1021]] shipped:** add `WHEN query_canceled THEN` **by name** to each per-unit/per-chunk handler, record the cancel, `EXIT` the loop rather than continuing (the box has just proven it cannot finish one unit), and let the existing trailing `UPDATE` close the row as `partial`. **Verified locally in FIX-1021 that this works:** the `statement_timeout` timer is disarmed once it has thrown, so the bookkeeping `UPDATE` after the catch does run — the repro closed the row with `canceled=true` and 4 units named as skipped. Note the handler CANNOT be an outer block wrapping the loop: PL/pgSQL forbids `COMMIT` inside a block that has an `EXCEPTION` clause, so it must go on the existing inner per-iteration block. Do these in priority order by blast radius — `refresh_official_donor_rollup_incremental` and `run_entity_connections_rebuild` are the two that actually get cancelled in practice. **⏳ PROGRESS 2026-08-13 (cc-prompt-60): 2 of 14 done — FIX STAYS OPEN.** Migration `20260813010000_fix1028_query_canceled_handlers.sql` fixed the two named above as highest blast radius: `refresh_official_donor_rollup_incremental()` and `run_entity_connections_rebuild()`. Both bodies were taken from prod `pg_get_functiondef()` and edited in place, so the migration cannot silently revert an intermediate definition. **Verified locally by repro, not by inspection:** with `SET statement_timeout='4s'` the EC procedure logged `[chunk] rebuild_entity_connections_donations — CANCELED`, the `CALL` returned normally instead of raising 57014, and its row closed itself `status=partial, canceled=true, elapsed_seconds=4`; with `'8s'` the donor procedure logged `chunk 2 CANCELED` / `PARTIAL (canceled) — 32 of 6995 recipients, resumable at chunk 3` and closed `stop_reason=canceled`. **Two things found while doing it that the bullet did not anticipate.** (1) The EC procedure's existing per-window handler carried the comment *'WHEN OTHERS catches a statement_timeout cancel too'* — the exact inversion of the trapping rule, now corrected in place; a future census should grep for that claim as well as for its absence. (2) **A cancelled `mode='full'` EC rebuild was leaving autovacuum STRANDED OFF on `entity_connections`**: the procedure COMMITs `autovacuum_enabled=false` before the chunk loop and re-enables it after, and the un-caught cancel jumped straight over the re-enable. That is precisely the [[FIX-884]] mechanism (2026-06-28 run cancelled at its cap → flag off ~4 weeks → 9.5M dead tuples, all-visible 0.9%, index-only scans degraded to per-row heap fetches); [[FIX-885]]'s startup reconcile heals it on the NEXT run, but the cancelling run now cleans up after itself. This makes the EXIT-and-fall-through shape load-bearing rather than merely tidy. (3) The donor procedure needed one extra invariant the generic idiom does not imply: its resume cursor must NOT advance past a CANCELLED chunk. A FAILED chunk may advance it (sweep_failures then blocks the watermark and the whole set is retried), but a cancel routes to the `partial` branch which does not set sweep_failures, so an advanced cursor there would permanently SKIP the recipients whose work had just rolled back — the cursor write is now gated on `v_canceled IS NULL`, and the repro confirms the cursor stopped at chunk 1's end while chunk 2 (recipients 33..239) was the cancelled one. **Remaining 12, unchanged:** `donor_rollup_rebuild_bulk`, `rebuild_entity_connection_stats`, `reconcile_donation_edge_orphans`, `reconcile_donor_party_rollup_orphans`, `reconcile_donor_rollup_orphans`, `reconcile_entity_connection_stats_orphans`, `reconcile_financial_entity_totals`, `refresh_donor_party_rollup_incremental`, `refresh_financial_entity_totals_incremental`, `refresh_sector_affinity_from_tag_changes`, `refresh_treemap_individuals_global`, `run_rule_taggers`. Cross-ref [[FIX-1021]], [[FIX-990]], [[FIX-968]], [[FIX-969]], [[FIX-944]], [[FIX-885]], [[FIX-884]]. <!--id:FIX-1028-->
- [x] 🔴 M — **Prod postgres RESTARTED under refresh_derived_mvs(weekly) — the weekly cadence killed the instance, and no PL/pgSQL handler can close the row it stranded** — **Measured live during the supervised [[FIX-1021]] firing, 2026-08-13 (cc-prompt-60), with Craig watching.** The firing was the falsifiable test of FIX-1021's 4200 s weekly budget and the gate for re-enabling pg_cron jobid 10. It did not reach a verdict: prod restarted underneath it. **Timeline, all UTC.** 01:05:09 fired `CALL public.refresh_derived_mvs('weekly')` exactly as cron 10 does — role `postgres`, db `postgres`, inherited `statement_timeout=6h`, budget GUC unset so the compiled-in 4200 s applied (pre-flight asserted all four). 01:05-01:10 units 1-2 (`chord_industry_flows_mv`, `chord_donor_type_party_flows_mv`) both logged `ok`. ~01:10 unit 3 `chord_donor_state_party_flows_mv` began and ran ~35 min, `wait_event_type='IO' / DataFileRead` for most of it. **01:45:27.788819 `pg_postmaster_start_time()` — the instance restarted**, killing the backend mid-unit. **Evidence it was this workload and not an unrelated platform event:** `cron.job_run_details` is EMPTY for the preceding 3 hours and `pg_stat_activity` showed exactly one non-idle backend (the firing) throughout, so nothing else was running to blame or to co-die. Prod settings at the time: `shared_buffers`=256MB, procedure-set `work_mem`=128MB, `max_connections`=60. The chord MVs are tiny as OUTPUT (64-120 kB) — the cost is the aggregation over `financial_relationships` behind them, which is the [[FIX-443]] OOM shape on a cache-starved box. **THREE CONSEQUENCES, each independent.** **(1) The stranded-`running` row has a SECOND root cause that [[FIX-1028]] does not address, partially contradicting that bullet's 'very likely the shared root cause' framing.** A backend killed by a restart dies on FATAL, not `query_canceled` (57014). FIX-1021's by-name handler — and the two FIX-1028 shipped tonight — catch CANCELS only. Nothing in PL/pgSQL can catch its own backend being killed, so the reaper is the ONLY mechanism for this class and must not be treated as a backstop for it. This row sat `running` 48+ min before the reaper's 60-minute window. Any census of stranded rows should therefore partition by cause (cancelled vs killed) before attributing them to the WHEN-OTHERS defect. **(2) FIX-1021's budget is sized against a premise this falsifies.** The predictive guard evaluates BETWEEN units only (playbook C3 — a REFRESH cannot be interrupted from inside), so it is a guard on TOTAL runtime. Unit 3 alone consumed ~35 min of the 70-min weekly budget without returning. A single unit that can outlive the whole budget, or kill the box, sits UNDERNEATH the guard where it cannot act. Re-enabling jobid 10 on the strength of a between-unit budget is therefore not yet justified. **(3) The new per-MV instrumentation produced NOTHING, because it is only durable at the end.** `v_unit_times` accumulates in a local variable and is written solely by the terminal UPDATE, which a killed backend never reaches — so the run that most needed per-unit timings is exactly the run that recorded none. Same shape as the reaper's own 'elapsed is an upper bound, not a measurement' note. **Fix directions, in rough priority.** (a) Confirm the mechanism from the Supabase Analytics logs API around 01:45:27 (OOM kill vs platform restart) before anything else — this bullet asserts correlation and exclusion of other load, NOT a proven OOM. (b) If OOM: make the chord units bounded — the four chord MVs are the only weekly units and they all aggregate the same large table, so consider per-unit `work_mem`, forcing `max_parallel_workers_per_gather=0` for them, or materialising their input once instead of four independent scans. (c) Persist per-unit timings AS THEY HAPPEN (a row or an UPDATE per unit, committed at the existing per-unit COMMIT) so a killed run still leaves measurements — without this, every future incident is unfalsifiable the same way. (d) Only then re-run the supervised firing and re-decide jobid 10. **jobid 10 remains `active=false`; it was never enabled.** **🔬 LOG EVIDENCE 2026-08-13 (Management API / Analytics, same night): the mechanism is `chord_donor_state_party_flows_mv`, and it has now done this TWICE.** **(a) It WAS a crash, not a platform restart — established by controlled comparison against this project's own logs.** `postgres_logs` at this project's verbosity does not carry postmaster start/stop lines in general, so absence alone would prove nothing — except that the 2026-08-11 22:51 restart DOES log the full orderly sequence (`archive command failed with exit code 1` ×3 → `received fast shutdown request` → `aborting any active transactions` → `pg_cron scheduler shutting down` → `terminating connection due to administrator command` → `the database system is starting up` ×4 → `pg_cron scheduler started`). **None of those lines appear at 01:45:27 on 08-13.** All that appears is `the database system is not accepting connections` ×2 at 01:45:28. Two independent confirmations: `pg_stat_bgwriter.stats_reset = 2026-08-13 01:45:27.903088+00`, i.e. **114 ms after `pg_postmaster_start_time()`** — on PG 17.6 the cumulative stats file is persisted across a clean shutdown and discarded only when recovery runs, so a reset at startup means the prior shutdown was unclean; and **no shutdown checkpoint was logged**, though routine `checkpoint starting: time` lines are logged either side (01:40:03 and 01:50:28). No line names a dying process and there is no OOM message — the kernel OOM killer logs outside postgres, so OOM remains the leading hypothesis but is still NOT proven. **(b) The per-unit timings the terminal UPDATE lost are recoverable from `auto_explain`, and they indict one MV.** 08-13 firing: `chord_industry_flows_mv` **189,390.752 ms** (completed 01:08:19), `chord_donor_type_party_flows_mv` **143,557.390 ms** (completed 01:10:42), `chord_donor_state_party_flows_mv` **no completion line at all** — it began ~01:10:43 and the box died at 01:45:27, so it ran **≥34m44s against siblings of ~3 min**, a >10× outlier. Unit 1's 189 s also sits exactly in the 185-198 s band FIX-1021 measured on 08-12, so the box was behaving normally until unit 3. **(c) THE 08-11 LOG-DARK GAP AND THIS INCIDENT DO UNIFY — but on TRIGGER, not on signature; the bullet should not merge them naively.** Same window on 08-11: `cron job 10 starting` at 07:00:00.139, `chord_industry_flows_mv` **262,648.595 ms** (completed 07:04:23), `chord_donor_type_party_flows_mv` **220,533.129 ms** (completed 07:08:04), and **`chord_donor_state_party_flows_mv` again has no completion line.** What follows is the box dying by degrees rather than at once: `cron job 25 job startup timeout` at 07:30:12, `cron job 26 job startup timeout` at 08:15:49 (pg_cron could not launch a worker at all), then postgres stops logging at **08:29:07** and stays dark **14h22m** until an administrator's fast shutdown at 22:51:20. **So both of the last two `refresh_derived_mvs('weekly')` firings stalled on the SAME unit and took prod down — once as a 14-hour wedge ended by a manual restart, once as an outright crash.** The 08-11 gap is therefore no longer a mystery: it was this job. **The endings differ because the box failed differently, and that difference is not the interesting variable — the trigger is.** **Consequences for the fix directions above:** (i) direction (a) is now ANSWERED for the crash-vs-restart question and only the OOM-vs-other question remains open; (ii) direction (b) should be scoped to `chord_donor_state_party_flows_mv` FIRST rather than to all four chord MVs — its two siblings complete in ~3 min every time and are not implicated; (iii) a new direction: **jobid 10 has now failed catastrophically on 2 of its last 2 firings and must not be re-enabled until this one MV is understood**, and note that everything scheduled behind it on a Tuesday inherits the damage (see the FIX-1024 re-file — jobid 13's `job startup timeout` failures at 09:00 on 08-04 and 08-11 sit inside exactly this window). **Retention caveat:** the Analytics API returned nothing for 2026-08-04, so this census covers only ~7 days and cannot say when the regression began. **✅ MECHANISM NAMED AND FIXED 2026-08-13 (cc-prompt-61) — migration `20260813030000_fix1030_chord_donor_state_plan_fix_and_unit_watchdog.sql`; full writeup `docs/audits/2026-08-13-fix1030-chord-donor-state-plan-flip.md`.** **It is a PLAN FLIP, and fix direction (b) above was aimed at the wrong thing** — the four chord MVs do not share a problem, and per-unit `work_mem` / `max_parallel_workers_per_gather` would not have helped. Prod's plan for unit 3 is a `Nested Loop` whose inner side is `Memoize → Index Scan using financial_entities_pkey`, driven by ~950k outer rows; its siblings use `Parallel Hash Join`. It wins on cost by 19% (613,522 vs 756,100 with `enable_nestloop=off`), and that win is manufactured by three compounding defects: **(1) a 298.5× underestimate** of the `financial_entities` filter — `LENGTH(metadata->>'state')=2` is an expression no index or statistics object covers, so it gets the hardcoded `DEFAULT_EQ_SEL` 0.005; prod estimates **10,714** rows of 3,679,374 where the measured truth is **3,198,178 (87.13%)**; **(2) a 41× underestimate** of `n_distinct(financial_relationships.from_id)` — pg_stats says **42,267**, measured truth **1,745,921** distinct donors across 4,183,604 rows, which is what makes Memoize look ~99%-effective; **(3) `random_page_cost = 1.1`** (prod's Supabase default; local Docker and stock postgres are 4) — **isolated one-at-a-time on the clone, this is the SINGLE setting that flips the plan**, and with prod's GUCs replayed the clone reproduces prod's plan node-for-node (602,475 vs 607,984). **The TRIGGER is confirmed as candidate (a), [[FIX-1018]]'s `financial_relationships_donor_rollup_dirty_idx`, built ~03:00 UTC 08-11 — four hours before the first death.** Its predicate covers unit 3's WHERE almost exactly, so it handed the nested loop a cheaper outer path than it had ever had (bitmap startup 17,539 vs 41,217; 953,828 est rows vs 1,519,884), dropping the total below the hash join. Dropping the index in a rolled-back transaction on the clone restores the hash join. **The index is not at fault and is not touched** — it made a pre-existing misestimate reachable. Candidates (b) FIX-1013 vacuum, (c) 08-09 ingest cardinality and (d) definition drift are all excluded (prod's live MV definitions match the migration; siblings got faster after the vacuum). **Measured execution** (`EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)` on the clone): `Memoize Hits: 2,328,993 Misses: 1,854,611 Evictions: 869,096 Memory Usage: 262,145 kB` — a **44.3% miss rate** against a model assuming ~99%, and **256 MB in ONE node**, because Memoize sizes at `work_mem × hash_mem_multiplier` (128MB×2) — i.e. a single plan node may allocate the entire size of prod's `shared_buffers`. **That is the OOM-consistent shape** answering direction (a)'s remaining question as far as it can be answered in-database; no server memory parameter is tuned. Join output estimated 4,722, actual 3,593,976 (761×). **Buffers: 7,752,465 → 514,004 (15.1×), and 7,418,444 → 179,993 (41.2×) on financial_entities alone — landing within 3% of healthy sibling unit 2's 498,015**, with 1.85M scattered pkey descents replaced by one sequential scan. **⚠ PREMISE CONTRADICTED — the clone cannot reproduce this outage by DURATION.** It completes the original in 23.5–37 s, not 35 minutes, because its OS page cache holds the whole 1,632 MB `financial_entities` heap, so prod's physical reads are RAM hits here. The clone reproduces the PLAN and the I/O DEMAND exactly and the WALL CLOCK never — which is why the evidence is buffer counts, not seconds. Standing caveat for future perf repro on this clone. **THE FIX, two parts.** **(i)** The FE filter and state extraction are hoisted into a CTE `AS MATERIALIZED` fence, so FE is scanned once and projected narrow (width 48 vs 163) before the join; with no index on a CTE there is no parameterised inner path, so the nested loop is not merely unattractive but **unavailable** — the fix deliberately does not depend on the planner estimating anything correctly. Output identical (`LENGTH(x)=2` subsumes the `IS NOT NULL` and `<> ''` quals): full symmetric-difference parity, **325 rows before and after, 0 either direction, identical SUM 2,197,387,010.00**. **(ii)** `enforce_derived_mvs_unit_budget()` on a 2-minute pg_cron job (`derived-mvs-unit-watchdog`) cancels a unit outliving **900 s** (GUC `civitics.derived_mvs_unit_budget_seconds`; the procedure now publishes the in-flight unit + pid + start + budget, COMMITted before each unit). **The prompt's prescribed `SET LOCAL statement_timeout` is NOT implementable and this was re-verified, not assumed** — both `SET LOCAL` and plain `SET` report the new value while `pg_sleep(8)` completes under a nominal 2 s cap, because `statement_timeout` is armed once in `start_xact_command()` and a procedure's `SPI` COMMIT never re-arms it ([[FIX-703]]'s finding, still true on PG 17); `SET …; CALL …` from pg_cron fails with *invalid transaction termination*. **A cancel from outside the backend is the only mechanism that can interrupt a running unit**, and it lands in FIX-1021's existing `query_canceled` handler, so nothing about that path is new. **Local proof, both paths.** Post-fix weekly cadence **6/6 `complete`, 85 s**, with units 4-6 measured for the FIRST time (FIX-1021's instrumentation shipped 08-13 and the run that would have recorded them died): industry 26.5 s / donor_type 20.0 s / **donor_state 18.7 s (was unbounded, now 3rd fastest)** / subject 1.2 s / sector_dollars 3.2 s / spending_totals 15.7 s — so **no weekly unit comes within an order of magnitude of 900 s**, which is why that budget is chosen rather than guessed. Forced-timeout path at a 5 s budget: watchdog returned `{"action":"canceled","unit":"chord_industry_flows_mv","signaled":true,"unit_age_seconds":6.7}`, procedure logged `PARTIAL — 0/6 units ok (5 skipped)`, row closed `status='partial'` with the unit named in `error_message` and `watchdog_canceled_unit`, all 5 skipped units listed, **no stranded `running` row**, and the cancel-once guard stopped later polls from touching the bookkeeping. **STILL OPEN, deliberately not shipped:** statistics (1) and (2) remain wrong — the durable follow-up is `CREATE STATISTICS` on `(LENGTH(metadata->>'state'))` plus an `n_distinct` override on `from_id`, both excluded here because they need an `ANALYZE` to arm and both change plan choice prod-wide. The fence works regardless. **🚀 PROD APPLIED 2026-08-13 03:55:29-04:03:15 UTC — and the apply itself caused ~5.5 min of request-path timeouts. See [[FIX-1032]].** Gate was clean at 03:55 (0 non-idle backends, 0 running data_sync_log rows, last cron 03:30 succeeded in 13s, jobid 10 paused, daily cadence 2h away). But an MV definition cannot be replaced in place, so the migration does DROP + CREATE ... AS (which populates), and prod logged `duration: 442115.812 ms` for that CREATE plus `process 13991 still waiting for AccessShareLock on relation 127653 ... after 1000.090 ms`, dozens of 57014 `canceling statement due to statement timeout`, and 08006 `connection to client lost`, from 03:57:43 to 04:03:12 — stopping dead at the apply's end. Two compounding mechanisms: **(1) LOCK** — DROP+CREATE holds ACCESS EXCLUSIVE on the MV name for the whole 442s rebuild, so readers block on AccessShareLock then die at the 8s service_role timeout; **(2) I/O** — the CREATE's seq scan of the 1,632MB financial_entities heap ran on a box up only 2h10m since the 01:45:27 crash, so the 256MB shared_buffers was cold and starved the request path generally, which is why the damage was site-wide rather than confined to /graph chord callers. **Self-resolved, no escalation:** no restart (pm_start unchanged at 01:45:27), 0 errors in the following 4 min, `/` 200 in 2.2s and `/graph` 200 in 0.57s at 04:07. **⚠ ESTIMATE MISS — corrects this session's own numbers.** The apply was predicted at "2-3 min, blocked on that MV's readers", extrapolating the clone's warm 18.7s by the ~7x steady-state factor; the real cold-box factor was **~24x** and the blast radius was site-wide. **So the migration header's "900s is ~3.4x the slowest healthy unit" is warm-cache only — the true cold-box headroom is ~2x** (442s measured). The budget still stands: 442s is worst-case cold, the weekly fires at 07:00 an hour after the 06:00 daily has warmed the same heaps, and a cold false-trip closes the row `partial` and skips the rest, which is the safe direction. The applied migration file is deliberately NOT edited (never modify an applied migration); the correction lives in the audit doc §7 and here. **Post-apply prod state verified:** MV 325 rows / SUM 2,197,387,010.00 (byte-identical to the clone's rebuild); `AS MATERIALIZED` fence present in the live definition; unique index, GRANTs and [[FIX-1003b]] autovacuum reloptions all survived the swap; prod's plan for the new definition is all hash joins, no Memoize; `enforce_derived_mvs_unit_budget()` present, SECURITY INVOKER, EXECUTE only postgres/service_role; cron job 40 `derived-mvs-unit-watchdog` active `*/2 * * * *`, 4 runs, all succeeded, slowest **58ms**. The pre-swap 326 rows / 2,139,715,460.00 vs post-swap 325 / 2,197,387,010.00 delta is **staleness being corrected, not the fix changing output** — unit 3 had not refreshed successfully since before 08-11, and old and new definitions are proven output-identical on identical data. **⛔ jobid 10 REMAINS `active=false` — the supervised verification firing did NOT happen; re-enabling is tracked as [[FIX-1033]].** Tail ops FIX-1023 / FIX-1007 were deliberately NOT executed: Craig held both after this incident, since the 438,094-row delete plus its FIX-943 vacuum tail is the same class of heavy I/O on the same still-cold box and the pre-approval predated this evidence.
