- [ ] 🟠 S — **FIX-1024 re-filed: financial-entity-totals-incremental IS enabled on prod — it has failed its last 3 firings, and 2 of them are collateral from the weekly derived-MV refresh** — **[[FIX-1024]]'s premise is contradicted on prod — same symptom, different bug.** That bullet says jobid 13 `financial-entity-totals-incremental` was created paused and never enabled, so `financial_entities.total_donated_cents` refreshes monthly rather than weekly. It was measured on the local prod clone and the bullet correctly warned to verify against prod first. **Verified on prod 2026-08-13: `SELECT jobid, jobname, active FROM cron.job WHERE jobid=13` returns `active = t`.** The job IS enabled and IS firing on schedule. The stale aggregate is real, but the cause is not a missing enable — **the job is enabled and failing.** `cron.job_run_details` for jobid 13, newest first: runid 233 `2026-08-11 09:00` **failed** after 5m25s, `job startup timeout`; runid 167 `2026-08-04 09:00` **failed** after 10s, `job startup timeout`; runid 115 `2026-07-28 09:00` **failed** after **6h00m11s** with `ERROR: canceling statement due to statement timeout` in `UPDATE public.financial_entities fe SET total_donated_cents = agg.total FROM (SELECT fr.from_id, SUM(fr.amount_ce...`; runid 71 `2026-07-21 09:00` **succeeded** in 7m04s. So the last success was 2026-07-21 and there have been three consecutive failures since — the columns have been effectively monthly-refreshed for three weeks, which is FIX-1024's observed consequence reached by a completely different route. **The two failure modes are distinct and only one is about this job.** (a) runid 115 is a genuine cost problem: the incremental UPDATE ran the full 6h role budget and was cancelled, which means the 'incremental' path is not actually bounded and wants the [[FIX-1002]]/[[FIX-1018]] treatment (dirty-set + wall-clock budget + per-chunk COMMIT) that the donor rollup already has. (b) runids 233 and 167 are `job startup timeout` — pg_cron could not launch a background worker at all, so the procedure never ran a single statement. **That is almost certainly collateral, not this job's fault.** jobid 13 is scheduled `0 9 * * 2` and jobid 10 `refresh-derived-mvs-weekly` is `0 7 * * 2` — the same Tuesday, two hours earlier. On 2026-08-11 the 07:00 weekly stalled on `chord_donor_state_party_flows_mv`, and the SAME `job startup timeout` message hit jobid 25 at 07:30 and jobid 26 at 08:15 before postgres went log-dark at 08:29 (see the weekly-refresh restart FIX). jobid 13's 09:00 failure that morning sits squarely inside that window. **So do not 'fix' jobid 13 by tuning jobid 13.** Order of work: (1) resolve the weekly derived-MV refresh first — while it can wedge the box every Tuesday morning, every job scheduled behind it is unreliable and its failures are unattributable; (2) then re-measure jobid 13 on a healthy Tuesday to see whether runid 115's 6h overrun reproduces; (3) only then decide whether the incremental UPDATE needs bounding. **Also worth noting for the audit trail:** FIX-1024 says nothing ever enabled the incremental job, but prod disagrees, so something DID enable it outside the migration record — worth finding, since an unrecorded prod cron mutation is its own hazard. Correct FIX-1024's clone-measured claim rather than acting on it. - [ ] 🟠 S — **Swapping a materialized view definition via DROP+CREATE holds ACCESS EXCLUSIVE for the whole rebuild — 442s of prod request-path timeouts on 08-13** — Caused a real user-visible incident during the [[FIX-1030]] prod apply, 2026-08-13 03:57:43-04:03:12 UTC. A materialized view's definition cannot be replaced in place, so the FIX-1030 migration did DROP MATERIALIZED VIEW + CREATE MATERIALIZED VIEW ... AS (which populates). Postgres logs: 'duration: 442115.812 ms plan: Query Text: CREATE MATERIALIZED VIEW public.chord_donor_state_party_flows_mv AS WITH donor_states AS MATERIALIZED (...)', alongside 'process 13991 still waiting for AccessShareLock on relation 127653 of database 5 after 1000.090 ms', dozens of 57014 'canceling statement due to statement timeout', and 08006 'connection to client lost'. TWO compounding mechanisms, and the second is the one that made it a whole-site event rather than a chord-page event. (1) LOCK: the DROP+CREATE pair holds ACCESS EXCLUSIVE on the MV name for the ENTIRE 442s rebuild, so every reader of that MV blocks on AccessShareLock and then dies at the 8s service_role statement_timeout. (2) I/O: the CREATE's sequential scan of the 1.6GB financial_entities heap ran on a box that had been up only 2h10m since the 01:45:27 crash, so shared_buffers (256MB) was cold and the scan paid full physical-read cost — which starved the request path generally, not just the blocked readers. This is why the timeouts were not confined to /graph chord callers. ESTIMATE MISS WORTH RECORDING: the same statement measured 18.7s on the warm prod-scale clone. The session extrapolated a ~7x clone-to-prod factor from steady-state weekly unit timings and predicted 2-3 min; the real cold-box factor was ~24x. A warm-cache clone timing is NOT a safe basis for predicting a cold-prod DDL window. DURABLE FIX SHAPE: build the replacement out of line and make the exclusive window milliseconds instead of minutes. CREATE MATERIALIZED VIEW x_new AS <def> WITH NO DATA (instant, no lock on x); REFRESH MATERIALIZED VIEW x_new (populates, still no lock on x, so readers keep being served the old data throughout); then one short transaction that does DROP MATERIALIZED VIEW x and ALTER MATERIALIZED VIEW x_new RENAME TO x. Remember the things a DROP discards and the swap must re-apply: the unique index required for REFRESH ... CONCURRENTLY (rename it too), the GRANTs, the COMMENT, and any per-relation autovacuum reloptions ([[FIX-1003b]] sets these on all four chord MVs — FIX-1030 did re-apply them, but only because it was checked for deliberately). SECONDARY RULE: schedule MV-definition swaps against a WARM box. A box within a few hours of a restart has a cold 256MB shared_buffers against multi-GB heaps and will pay worst-case I/O for any full scan. Cross-ref [[FIX-1030]], [[FIX-1003b]], [[FIX-1021]], [[FIX-884]]. <!--id:FIX-1032-->
- [x] 🟠 S — **Re-enable pg_cron jobid 10 (refresh-derived-mvs-weekly) after a supervised firing passes — weekly chord/sector MVs go stale until then** — Split out of [[FIX-1030]] so the re-enable is not lost when that bullet closes. FIX-1030 shipped the actual fix (the CTE AS MATERIALIZED fence on chord_donor_state_party_flows_mv, plus enforce_derived_mvs_unit_budget() as a per-unit bound) and it is LIVE on prod as of 2026-08-13 04:03 UTC — but jobid 10 was never re-enabled, because the supervised verification firing that FIX-1030's decision gate required did not happen. jobid 10 has been active=false since the 08-11 incident. WHAT IS STALE MEANWHILE: all six weekly units. chord_industry_flows_mv, chord_donor_type_party_flows_mv, chord_donor_state_party_flows_mv, chord_subject_party_flows_mv, official_sector_dollars_mv and refresh_spending_totals have had no successful weekly run since before 2026-08-11 (both firings since ended reaped). Note chord_donor_state_party_flows_mv is the one exception — it was repopulated fresh by the FIX-1030 migration's CREATE at 04:03 UTC on 08-13, so it is currently the FRESHEST of the six, not the stalest. The daily cadence (jobid 9) is unaffected and still active. WHAT RE-ENABLING REQUIRES, in order: (1) a supervised manual CALL public.refresh_derived_mvs('weekly') on a WARM, quiet box — NOT within a few hours of a restart, per [[FIX-1032]]; (2) it completes inside the 4200s total budget with all six per-unit timings recorded in metadata.unit_seconds (the FIX-1021 instrumentation now writes these on every outcome, and FIX-1030 additionally publishes the in-flight unit as it runs, so progress is observable live rather than only at the end); (3) confirm no unit approached the 900s per-unit budget — the cold-box measurement of the fixed unit 3 was 442s, so headroom is ~2x not the ~3.4x the migration header claims; (4) only then cron.alter_job for jobid 10 with active := true, BY NAME not by hardcoded id. A budget or watchdog EXIT that closes the row 'partial' cleanly is the guard WORKING, not a pass — it means stop and diagnose, not re-enable. Worth watching on the first unattended Tuesday: everything scheduled behind jobid 10 inherits any damage it does (see [[FIX-1024]] — jobid 13's job startup timeout failures at 09:00 on 08-04 and 08-11 sit inside exactly this window). **✅ DONE 2026-08-13 04:50-05:02 UTC — supervised firing PASSED, jobid 10 RE-ENABLED.** `status=complete`, **6/6 units, 707.1s**, zero failures, `budget_hit=false`, `canceled=false`. Per-unit on prod: chord_industry_flows_mv **185.7s**, chord_donor_type_party_flows_mv **128.0s**, **chord_donor_state_party_flows_mv 160.3s**, chord_subject_party_flows_mv **2.8s**, official_sector_dollars_mv **112.1s**, refresh_spending_totals **118.2s** — units 4-6 measured on prod for the first time. **The headline: unit 3 completed in 160.3s, sitting BETWEEN its two siblings (185.7 / 128.0) — sibling-class exactly as the fence predicted, against a unit that ran ≥34m44s without returning on 08-13 and never returned at all on 08-11.** Headroom beat both prior estimates: total 707.1s of the 4200s budget (17%), slowest unit 185.7s of the 900s fence (**4.85x**, vs the ~3.4x claimed warm and the ~2x feared cold in the FIX-1032 correction). jobid 10 re-enabled via `cron.alter_job` with the jobid resolved BY NAME per requirement (4); post-close-out jobs 9/10/40 all active, one-off unscheduled, no restart, 0 stranded rows, 0 advisory locks. **⚠ ATTEMPT 1 (04:30) WAS KILLED BY THE OPERATOR, not by the code — worth recording.** `cron.unschedule()` was run 11s in to stop the one-off re-firing, and **unscheduling a RUNNING pg_cron job cancels its worker**: `cron.job_run_details` jobid 41 = `status=failed`, `return_message='job canceled'`, `end_time=04:30:11.130007`. The backend outlived the cancel long enough to finish unit 1 (~207s) and was torn down ~04:35:56 (`connection to client lost` / `Broken pipe` — pg_cron reaches its worker over libpq), which is the un-catchable FATAL class, so the row stranded `running` and needed the reaper. **Ordering lesson: the re-fire risk of leaving a one-off scheduled is 24h away, the unschedule risk is immediate — unschedule AFTER completion.** Attempt 2 did that. **Two things attempt 1 exposed. (a) RESOLVED AS FINE — pg_cron workers ARE visible to the watchdog.** The open worry was that `enforce_derived_mvs_unit_budget()`'s `state <> 'idle'` predicate would drop a NULL-state background worker and leave the 900s fence INERT in the only configuration prod uses. Measured on the live worker: `application_name=pg_cron`, `backend_type=client backend`, `state=active` (NOT null), `query=CALL public.refresh_derived_mvs('weekly');`, and the guard's full predicate evaluates **TRUE**, every clause passing individually. The fence is live for pg_cron-launched runs. **(b) STILL OPEN — [[FIX-1035]]:** the watchdog probes pid liveness only AFTER the budget elapses, so a dead backend is indistinguishable from a long unit — at 04:44 it still returned `within budget, unit_age_seconds: 702.5` for a process gone eleven minutes, and it fooled this session into reporting unit 2 as "running long, heading for the fence" when unit 2 had accrued only ~149s of real work. `current_unit_started_at` freezes at death; growing `unit_age` is arithmetic, not evidence of life. Full writeup `docs/audits/2026-08-13-fix1030-chord-donor-state-plan-flip.md` §10. Cross-ref [[FIX-1030]], [[FIX-1021]], [[FIX-1032]], [[FIX-1024]], [[FIX-1022]], [[FIX-1035]]. <!--id:FIX-1033-->
- [x] 🟠 M — **Two live planner statistics defects on financial_entities / financial_relationships — FIX-1030's fence protects ONE query, nothing protects the next** — Split out of [[FIX-1030]], where both were measured but deliberately not corrected. They are the loaded gun that FIX-1018's index merely pulled the trigger on, and they are still loaded. DEFECT 1 — expression selectivity. The predicate LENGTH(metadata->>'state') = 2 on financial_entities is an expression that no index and no statistics object covers, so the planner falls back to its hardcoded DEFAULT_EQ_SEL of 0.005: prod estimates 10,714 rows of 3,679,374 where the measured truth is 3,198,178, i.e. 87.13 percent. That is a 298.5x underestimate and it is what makes a nested loop's inner side look ~300x smaller than it is. DEFECT 2 — n_distinct. pg_stats reports n_distinct = 42,267 for financial_relationships.from_id (42,600 on the clone, so this is not a clone artifact); the measured distinct-donor count is 1,745,921 across the 4,183,604 qualifying donation rows alone. That is at least a 41x underestimate, and it is what makes Memoize look ~99 percent effective when its real miss rate is 44.3 percent with 869,096 evictions. WHY THIS IS ITS OWN FIX. Together, at prod's random_page_cost of 1.1, these two made a Memoize-backed nested loop price 19 percent BELOW the hash join, and that plan wedged prod for 14h22m on 2026-08-11 and crashed it on 2026-08-13. FIX-1030 fixed the symptom the only way that was safe in one session: a CTE AS MATERIALIZED optimiser fence on chord_donor_state_party_flows_mv, which makes THAT query structurally immune because a CTE has no index and therefore no parameterised inner path. The statistics themselves were left wrong. So any OTHER query joining financial_relationships to financial_entities, or filtering on a metadata expression, can still flip the same way — and the trigger does not have to be exotic: FIX-1018 flipped it by adding one perfectly reasonable partial index whose predicate happened to offer a cheaper outer path. The next such index is not predictable. FIX SHAPE, three steps. (1) CREATE STATISTICS on the expression, PG14+ expression statistics, for LENGTH(metadata->>'state') and probably also for the bare metadata->>'state' on financial_entities, since the two are separate expressions and stats on one do not help the other. (2) Correct n_distinct on financial_relationships.from_id with ALTER TABLE ... ALTER COLUMN from_id SET (n_distinct = ...). Measure count(DISTINCT from_id) over the WHOLE table first, not just the donation subset, then express it as a NEGATIVE value, which postgres reads as a fraction of reltuples and therefore stays correct as the table grows; a positive absolute would go stale the same way the sampled one did. (3) ANALYZE both tables — neither change does anything until the stats are rebuilt. GATE BEFORE LANDING. This changes plan choice prod-wide for every consumer of these two tables, which is precisely why it was excluded from FIX-1030's bounded scope. Take before/after EXPLAIN diffs on the main FR/FE consumers and land only if none regresses: the six donor-rollup arms, run_entity_connections_rebuild, the treemap RPCs including the resumable individuals sweep, the get_*_page consolidating RPCs, get_cohort_top_donors, and the chord cohort paths. Note that making an estimate HONEST usually improves plans, but 'usually' is not a standard this particular code path has earned. OPERATIONAL NOTE. The ANALYZE itself is a full-scan-class cost against a 1,632MB and a 3,502MB heap, so schedule it per [[FIX-1032]]'s warm-box rule and off-peak, not within a few hours of a restart. Cross-ref [[FIX-1030]], [[FIX-1018]], [[FIX-1032]], [[FIX-884]], [[FIX-443]]. <!--id:FIX-1034-->
- [x] 🟡 S — **Unit watchdog cannot tell a long-running unit from a dead backend until the 900s budget elapses — add per-poll pid liveness** — Demonstrated live on prod 2026-08-13 during the first (aborted) [[FIX-1033]] supervised firing. enforce_derived_mvs_unit_budget() ([[FIX-1030]]) checks whether the published backend_pid is still a live refresh_derived_mvs backend ONLY on the branch it reaches AFTER unit_age exceeds the budget. Every earlier poll short-circuits at 'within budget' using nothing but arithmetic on metadata.current_unit_started_at — a timestamp that is FROZEN once the backend dies, so it keeps growing forever and looks exactly like healthy long-running work. Observed: the backend was terminated at ~04:35:56 mid unit 2, and at 04:44 the watchdog still returned {action: none, reason: within budget, unit_age_seconds: 702.5} for a process that had been gone eleven minutes. It also fooled the operator — this session reported to Craig that unit 2 was 'running long at 486s then 638s, heading for the fence' when unit 2 had in fact only ever accrued ~149s of real work. WHAT IS NOT BROKEN, so nobody re-opens it: the post-budget guard itself is CORRECT and was verified — it refuses to cancel when the pid is absent or is not running this procedure, which is the safe direction and protects against pid reuse. Separately verified the same day that a pg_cron worker IS visible to that guard: application_name pg_cron, backend_type client backend, state active (NOT null), query CALL public.refresh_derived_mvs('weekly'), and the guard's full predicate evaluates TRUE against it — so the 900s fence is live for pg_cron-launched runs, which was an open worry and is now closed. THE GAP is purely detection latency for the dead-backend case: today such a run stays 'running' until unit_age crosses the budget (up to 900s of nothing), at which point the watchdog correctly declines to cancel, and the row then waits for reap_stale_sync_log's window (up to 60 min) before anything closes it. A stranded non-terminal row is not cosmetic — it is what poisoned connections_pipeline_healthy in [[FIX-833]], because health checks that order by completed_at see a NULL and sort it first. FIX SHAPE: hoist the pg_stat_activity liveness probe ABOVE the budget comparison so it runs on every poll. If the published pid is gone (or is no longer running this procedure) while the row is still 'running', do not cancel anything — there is nothing to cancel — but close the row immediately as 'reaped' with an error_message naming the last published unit and stating that started_at..close is NOT a runtime, mirroring reap_stale_sync_log's existing FIX-944 wording. That turns a 900s-plus-60-minute blind spot into a 2-minute one and makes the last-known unit durable at the moment of death, which is the same instrumentation gap [[FIX-1030]] consequence (3) was about. Keep the existing post-budget cancel path exactly as it is. Cross-ref [[FIX-1030]], [[FIX-1033]], [[FIX-944]], [[FIX-833]], [[FIX-1028]]. <!--id:FIX-1035-->
- [ ] 🟠 M — **sync-canary-check has been red for 10 days and every failure is a TRUE POSITIVE — the gap is alert delivery, not the checker** — sync-canary-check has failed every morning since 2026-08-04 (last pass 08-03; 10 consecutive red runs through 08-13), which reads as a broken checker. It is not. **Every failure is a true positive, and the red X IS the alarm** — [[FIX-885]] made the DB-health findings exit non-zero precisely because an email alone is not escalation. Layer verdict (playbook E11): not the canary, not the checker logic, not the workflow env/secrets. Two things are actually wrong and neither is the canary. **(1) The conditions it reports are real, and they ROTATE — which is why a week of red looks stuck.** 08-04: vm_degraded on financial_entities at 47.6 pct all-visible. 08-05: donor_rollup_refresh stale 68.49h against a 48h window. 08-08: financial_entities again. 08-09 through 08-13: escalating rollups under the [[FIX-977]] derived registry, latest being entity_connections_rebuild at 356.65h against an escalate-past-210h bound. That last one is a live operational gap in its own right: prod data_sync_log shows entity_connections_rebuild status=reaped on 08-03 and 08-10 (both Monday, jobid 22) with NO row at all on the Wednesday firings (jobid 2) of 08-05 and 08-12, and last status=complete on 2026-07-29. entity_connection_stats_rebuild is reaped since 08-10, donor_party_rollup_refresh since 08-04. Those need their own FIX; they are not canary defects. **(2) A distinct checker-side failure on 08-06 that is NOT an escalation.** That run died with "canary-check failed: data_sync_log query failed: canceling statement due to statement timeout" and wrote no meta row — prod data_sync_log carries canary_check rows for 08-01 through 08-13 with 08-06 missing. This is the one run where the checker itself broke rather than reported. [[FIX-968]] built nightly_check_unavailable for this class but the failure here lands a layer above it. **The notification gap — why no alert reached Craig.** The runs wore status completed/failure, never cancelled, and metadata.alert_sent was true on every escalating run. sendAlert THROWS on a Resend error, so a true value means the Resend API ACCEPTED roughly ten sends to ADMIN_EMAIL. The gap is therefore downstream of the API accept: delivery, spam filing, a wrong ADMIN_EMAIL secret, or an unverified RESEND_FROM sender domain. None of that is checkable from the repo — it needs the Resend dashboard delivery log. Rule out at the same time: GitHub Actions scheduled-workflow failure notifications should have produced ten emails on their own and evidently did not. Feeds the [[FIX-1026]] / [[FIX-969]] residual-alerting story. **Observability defect surfaced while diagnosing (small, real, fix alongside).** The escalation verdict lives ONLY in the stdout JSON, never in the canary_check meta row: writeMetaRow runs BEFORE the failures array is computed, so escalating_rollups, alert_sent and escalated are all absent from data_sync_log.metadata. Diagnosing ten days of red therefore required pulling ten sets of GHA logs when one SQL query should have answered it. Persist the escalation reason and alert_sent into the meta row so the arc is greppable. **Not fixed in session, deliberately:** there is no S-class change available — the checker is behaving correctly, and the real work splits into an alert-delivery investigation needing external dashboards plus a separate rollup-not-completing FIX. Cross-ref [[FIX-234]], [[FIX-885]], [[FIX-944]], [[FIX-968]], [[FIX-978]]. **RE-SCOPED 2026-08-17 (cc-prompt-63), on Craig's delivery evidence — the premise above is half wrong.** This bullet assumed the gap was alert DELIVERY. It is not: Resend delivered every one of the ten alerts, verified in Gmail. So there is no delivery investigation to run and no external-dashboard dependency, which removes the stated reason this was not S-class. **The actual failure is SALIENCE — ten identical daily emails that each say the same thing become invisible, and the eleventh, which mattered, read exactly like the ten that did not.** That is a notification-design problem, not an infrastructure one, and the fix is small and local. New fix shape, three parts: (1) **Severity tiers in the SUBJECT LINE**, so the tier is legible in a notification list without opening anything — today every alert shares one subject and the reader cannot triage without reading the body. (2) **Page on DELTA, not on STATE.** The current design re-alerts every morning for as long as the condition holds, which is what manufactured the ten identical mails; alert on the TRANSITION into a bad state, on a material worsening, and on recovery, and stay quiet while a known-bad state is merely persisting. A daily "still red, day 7" digest line is fine — a daily page is not. (3) **Split the duty between the two existing checkers by what they can actually prove:** [[FIX-1026]]'s probe owns the "site down" tier (request-path reachability, page-worthy immediately), and the canary owns the "data stale" tier (rollup freshness, digest-worthy and escalating only on delta). Conflating them is why a data-staleness signal and an outage signal arrive looking identical. The meta-row persistence item above (escalating_rollups / alert_sent / escalated absent from data_sync_log.metadata because writeMetaRow runs before the failures array is computed) SURVIVES this re-scope unchanged and is still the cheapest single improvement — it is what makes "did this alert fire, and why" a SQL query instead of ten GHA log pulls. Docs-correction style per the `5dbe3405` precedent: the original text is left standing above so the corrected premise is visible rather than silently rewritten. Cross-ref [[FIX-1026]], [[FIX-1052]]. <!--id:FIX-1036-->
- [ ] 🟠 M — **Chunk the growth-prone .in() id-list reads in packages/ (FIX-902 remainder)** — Filed by the [[FIX-902]] audit (docs/audits/2026-08-15-fix902-in-audit.md), which classified all 254 .in() call sites and migrated the 34 growth-prone ones in apps/ only. This is the packages/ remainder: 13 id-list call sites that are growth-prone and still unchunked. **Why it was split off:** fetchChunkedByIds lives in apps/civitics/src/lib/paginate.ts and packages cannot import from apps, so fixing these needs the helper RELOCATED into a package first — that is the open design question here, not the chunking itself. Candidate home is packages/db (beside the query helpers that several of these sites live in); apps/civitics/src/lib/paginate.ts would then re-export it so the 34 existing apps/ call sites and paginate.test.ts do not move. Secondary reason for the split: packages/data runs in the pipelines and the 2026-08-16 nightly was an evidence run that had to execute known code. **Context on scale:** packages/ is in far BETTER shape than apps/ was — 22 of its 36 id-list call sites already chunk at <=200 (RESOLVE_IN_CHUNK, EDGAR_IN_CHUNK, DELETE_CHUNK, IN_CHUNK), because the pipelines learned this lesson repeatedly and wrote it down. **Worklist, highest value first:** (1) packages/db/src/queries/entity-industry.ts:46 fetchEntityIdsByIndustryTag — this one has NO .in() so it is not in the census, but it is the FEEDER for the two treemap sites FIX-902 just chunked, and it is silently TRUNCATED at PostgREST's 1000-row cap out of 8908 health-tagged entities locally. PROVEN LIVE during the audit: GET /api/graph/treemap with entityId + industry_filter=health returns an empty array even though that official has a real health-industry donation, because the qualifying donor is not among the arbitrary 1000 returned; the same call with industry_filter=retail (a sub-1000 tag) returns 16 KB of correct rows. Chunking the callers fixed the 414, NOT the truncation — this needs paging (fetchAllRows shape) plus a stable .order(). This is a live wrong-answer bug on a user-visible surface and should probably be its own commit ahead of the rest. (2) packages/db/src/queries/financial-relationships.ts:55 getTopDonorsByOfficial — builds up to 1000 distinct donor ids from a 1000-row donation read into ONE .in(). (3) packages/db/src/queries/financial-relationships.ts:85 listDonationsByDonor — .in(from_id, ids) where ids comes from an uncapped ilike on display_name; a broad donor-name search matches thousands. (4) packages/data/src/pipelines/congress/bills.ts:291 — .in(external_id, billKeys) over the full deduped per-chamber buffer (BILL_CHUNK_SIZE 500). Note this file's OWN comment at line 241 defines RESOLVE_IN_CHUNK=200 and says the buffer routinely exceeds 200 keys — and the step-1 lookup 50 lines later goes in unchunked. Same file, same lesson, adjacent code: the [[FIX-899]] shape exactly. (5) packages/data/src/pipelines/congress/reconcile-former-members.ts:172 — UPDATE .in(id, diff.staleIds) deactivating departed members. Small per run and it throws on error (fails loudly, not silently), so lowest priority of the writes. (6-13) packages/data/src/scripts/cron-run-anchor-verify.ts lines 76, 94, 139, 140, 170, 171, 247, 318, 325 — muskIds/dcccIds/apIds/ids from name-matched entity lookups with no cap. Diagnostic script, not request-path, so lowest priority overall, but it is where a silent empty result is most likely to be MISREAD as a passing anchor check. **Do NOT bundle a lint rule or codemod banning raw .in()** — still out of scope for the same reason as in FIX-902: 47 of the 254 census lines are legitimate small literal enums and the id-list-vs-enum discrimination is a design question. See [[FIX-901]] for the helper contract and [[FIX-926]] for its concurrency cap. **AMENDED 2026-08-17 (cc-prompt-63): one of the 13 is DONE; 12 remain.** `fetchEntityIdsByIndustryTag` in packages/db/src/queries/entity-industry.ts was promoted out of this bullet and fixed on its own, because it was not a growth-prone `.in()` at all — it was an UNRANGED `.select()` truncating at PostgREST's 1,000-row cap, which makes it the [[FIX-892]] / [[FIX-878]] silent-truncation class rather than the URI-length class, and therefore independent of the helper-relocation design question that blocks the other 12. It is also the only one of the 13 that was serving WRONG DATA to users rather than merely being at risk. Measured on prod before/after through PostgREST (SQL cannot see the cap): health 1,000 -> 9,059 (lost 8,059), finance 1,000 -> 8,428 (lost 7,428), retail 795 -> 795 (unchanged, genuinely under the cap). The user-visible symptom was `industry_filter=health` returning no donations for an official with real health-sector money, because that official's PACs sat outside the arbitrary first-1,000 slice. Note the blast radius is wider than the single tag in the original report: **11 of the 12 industry tags exceed 1,000 entities on prod** (health 9,059, finance 8,428, agriculture 5,479, real_estate 5,294, tech 3,455, defense 2,239, oil_gas 1,999, transportation 1,981, labor 1,664, legal 1,381, lobby 1,293), so every industry filter except retail was truncated. Fixed by paginating with `.range()` plus a deterministic `.order("entity_id")` so page boundaries cannot drop or repeat a row. The remaining 12 sites are unchanged and still blocked on the helper relocation. <!--id:FIX-1037-->
- [x] 🔴 M — **Upstash free-tier command allotment is exhausted — the entire edge rate limiter is failing open, silently, and Upstash is the one vendor platform_usage_snapshot does not track** — **Found by the 2026-08-15 traffic/cost-spike triage (docs/audits/2026-08-15-traffic-cost-spike.md).** Observed verbatim in prod Vercel logs at 2026-08-16 00:28-01:26 UTC, on 92 of 100 sampled requests: '[ratelimit] Upstash error on bucket=entity_leaf — failing open (allow): Command failed: ERR max requests limit exceeded. Limit: 500000, Usage: 500002.' **Every crawl defense the codebase has is a no-op right now.** The FIX-637 entity_pages cap, the FIX-683 entity_leaf cap and the FIX-797 entity_prefetch bucket all route through checkRateLimit in apps/civitics/src/lib/ratelimit.ts, which catches the Upstash error and returns ALLOW. All 100 sampled requests returned HTTP 200; zero 429s. A crawler walking distinct entity UUIDs across /officials, /donors, /proposals and /jurisdictions was proceeding completely unthrottled at the time of measurement, every request a cold cache MISS and a full SSR render. **The fail-open is CORRECT and is not the defect** — it is load-bearing per the module header and it kept the site up. The defect is that it is **unobservable and un-alerted**: nothing anywhere pages when the secondary cost/volumetric layer switches itself off. **Root cause of the blindness.** packages/db/src/platform-snapshot.ts computes usage for anthropic, supabase, cloudflare (R2), github and vercel. **Upstash is absent from all of it** — there is no getUpstashUsage helper, no platform_limits rows, no metric, nothing. So a hard $0-ceiling cost control (design: free tier, auto-upgrade OFF, so exhaustion rate-limits the DATABASE rather than billing) can hit its cap and go fully open with zero signal on the Platform Costs card, zero warning status, and zero auto-trip evaluation. **Fix shape — cheapest durable option in the whole remediation list.** Add an Upstash block to the existing 10-minute snapshot: a getUpstashUsage() helper alongside the existing vendor helpers, platform_limits rows for the command allotment, and updateUsage writes so commands-used rides the SAME warning/critical machinery every other metric already uses. No new cron, no new workflow, no new alerting path. **Sizing note:** 'Usage: 500002' is only 2 over the cap because the counter freezes on crossing, so it carries NO information about WHEN exhaustion happened — the Upstash console is the only source for that, and it bounds the FIX-1040 ordering claim. **Secondary, separable:** reduce commands spent per request (skip the check on cache hits, or coarsen buckets) so the next allotment does not burn at the same rate. Software lever before compute lever — raising the Upstash tier is deliberately NOT proposed. Cross-ref [[FIX-570]] (the limiter this instruments), [[FIX-637]], [[FIX-683]], [[FIX-797]] (the three defenses currently disabled), [[FIX-281]] (the snapshot to extend), [[FIX-880]], [[FIX-1039]], [[FIX-1040]], [[FIX-1041]]. <!--id:FIX-1038-->
- [ ] 🔴 M — **Under Attack mode is load-bearing cost control (~$21/day) holding a crawler that has not backed off — it must not come off before the limiter works, and setting CIVITICS_APP_URL to civitics.com would kill both monitoring jobs instantly** — **Found by the 2026-08-15 traffic/cost-spike triage (docs/audits/2026-08-15-traffic-cost-spike.md). REVISED 2026-08-16 — the original bullet claimed UA mode was 'inverted' and not working; that was wrong and is retracted in full. Craig authorized this edit.** **What the original got wrong and why:** it dated UA mode to docs/CLOUDFLARE.md's 2026-08-08 posture row (Craig actually enabled it **2026-08-15 22:00 UTC**), and it read a 4-row `vercel logs` sample — capped and sampled, a shape sample and never a volume measure — as evidence the crawl was unaffected. Both errors pointed the same wrong way. **What is actually true, measured via the new `node scripts/cf-analytics.mjs --hours 24`:** the crawl began **2026-08-15 06:00 UTC** and ran machine-flat at **7,163-7,562 requests/hour for 16 hours**, essentially all on www.civitics.com, walking distinct entity UUIDs across /officials, /donors, /proposals, /jurisdictions, /institutions — the exact [[FIX-637]]/[[FIX-683]] crawl shape, every request a cold origin render (zone cache hit rate ~0.1 pct). **Under Attack mode then worked as a step function at exactly the enablement hour: 21:00 UTC = 7,313 x HTTP 200 / 0 x 403; 22:00 UTC = 445 x 200 / 6,850 x 403; 23:00 UTC = 28 x 200 / 7,268 x 403. Origin-reaching traffic fell ~99 pct.** **The cost this is holding back.** Because billing day 15 contained only ONE crawl hour, the triage's first cost pass understated the exposure ~11x. Rebuilt from Cloudflare request counts: ~\$1.23e-4 per origin-reaching crawl request, so ~\$21/day / **~\$640/month** unmitigated, against a ~\$31/month run-rate. **So UA mode is currently the single largest cost control on the platform, and it is temporary scaffolding, not a fix.** **The crawler has NOT backed off** — still 7,033-7,336 req/hour, now absorbed by Cloudflare instead of Vercel. Turn UA mode off before [[FIX-1038]] lands and the burn resumes within the hour. **~1 pct still leaks to origin** (28-86 x 200/hour, independently matching ~1 req/min in `vercel logs`), and nothing throttles that residual because the limiter is fail-open. NB the original bullet inferred a **challenge-solving / headless crawler** from a 403-vs-200 comparison plus a Speed Insights step; **both legs are withdrawn** — a ~1 pct leak is ordinary challenge bypass, not a JS-capable client, and the \$0.65 Speed Insights line was a fixed once-per-cycle charge (Vercel billing cycle is Aug 14 - Sep 14), not usage. **THE LIVE LANDMINE — unchanged by the revision, and now MORE urgent because UA mode is staying on.** .github/workflows/platform-snapshot.yml is the ONLY workflow that calls the app hostname (verified by grep; nightly.yml, sync-canary-check.yml, rebuild-entity-connections.yml and audit.yml do NOT), and both its jobs use secrets.CIVITICS_APP_URL with a fallback to the .vercel.app host. They are green today ONLY because that secret is unset or points at .vercel.app. docs/CLOUDFLARE.md section 5 states Cloudflare 403s every scripted probe to civitics.com including browser-UA curl, and this session measured exactly that. **The moment anyone sets CIVITICS_APP_URL to https://civitics.com, the platform snapshot AND the [[FIX-1026]] request-path probe both die** — and per FIX-1026's own header a timed-out probe reports as 'cancelled', which GitHub does not notify on. That blinds the platform during the next incident. Pin the fallback, or document the constraint at the secret. **THE CRAWLER IS IDENTIFIED — a targeted rule is now possible.** Cloudflare security events (user-supplied, ~30k mitigated) name it: **`meta-webindexer/1.1`**, UA `Mozilla/5.0 (Windows NT 10.0; Win64; x64) AppleWebKit/537.36 ... (compatible; meta-webindexer/1.1 (+https://developers.facebook.com/docs/sharing/webmasters/crawler))`, **ASN 32934 (Facebook, Inc.)**, IPv6 2a03:2880:f800:35::, Host www.civitics.com, mitigated by Rule ID `iuam` (i.e. Under Attack mode itself, not the WAF rule). A single named UA on a single ASN is the easy case: a **targeted WAF rule on that UA or ASN would let UA mode come off** without giving the crawler back the whole site — that is the cheapest exit and it should be evaluated before the general exit plan below. **What it is walking:** `/graph?entity=<uuid>` — **44,689 of 147,611 requests in 24h (~30 pct), the single largest path by a wide margin**, the rest a long tail of distinct entity UUIDs. Two consequences, both filed as [[FIX-1042]]: `/graph` gets **NO rate-limit bucket at all** in middleware.ts, and robots.txt's `Disallow: /*?` ([[FIX-513]]) did not stop it. **Exit plan — what must be true before UA mode goes off:** (a) the rate limiter working again ([[FIX-1038]]) **and** `/graph` actually bucketed ([[FIX-1042]]), since 30 pct of this crawl targets a route the limiter never inspects; (b) the 'Common Exploit Paths' WAF custom rule verified — **now PARTIALLY validated by this incident**: the same 24h window shows `/klex.php`, `/file.php` and `/index/function.php` scanner probes all returning **403**, so the rule is live and doing its job on the traffic class docs/CLOUDFLARE.md section 2 credits it with; section 2's real test (watch Vercel Fluid CPU after UA mode goes off) still stands. **Also promotes two docs/CLOUDFLARE.md Pending checkboxes into tracked work:** 'Turn Under Attack mode off' and 'Add the FIX-799 Skip rule'. The FIX-799 skip rule is DEPRIORITISED by this triage — H3 (RSC amplification) was killed on evidence (invocations per edge request 1.70 vs a 1.63 median; fluid CPU per invocation FELL 41 pct), so it is a soft-navigation UX fix, not a cost fix. **Separable exposure, filed here for context, NOT the incident cause:** the .vercel.app origin answers 200 with no protection at all. H2 was KILLED — the only 8 of 100 log rows on that hostname were our own cron and probe — so this is defence-in-depth, ranked last. If it is ever done, note that the request-path-probe job sends NO auth header and would break under any Host restriction, unlike the snapshot job which carries CRON_SECRET. Cross-ref [[FIX-513]], [[FIX-637]], [[FIX-683]], [[FIX-797]], [[FIX-799]], [[FIX-1026]], [[FIX-1038]], [[FIX-1040]]. <!--id:FIX-1039-->
- [x] 🟠 S — **The rate limiter's fail-open path logs once per bucketed request, and nothing durably records a 429 or a fail-open anywhere — the app cannot answer 'was the limiter firing?' after the fact** — **Found by the 2026-08-15 traffic/cost-spike triage (docs/audits/2026-08-15-traffic-cost-spike.md).** Two coupled observability gaps on the edge limiter, both surfaced by the same incident. **(1) Per-request log line on the failure path.** apps/civitics/src/lib/ratelimit.ts:168 emits console.warn('[ratelimit] Upstash error on bucket=...') inside the catch, so once Upstash is exhausted EVERY bucketed request produces a Vercel observability event. Measured in prod logs 2026-08-16 00:28-01:26 UTC: 92 of 100 sampled requests carried the warn. **Size it honestly before fixing it:** at the observed ratio this is worth roughly $0.03-0.30/day — Observability Events is a $2.87/month projected line. This is a hygiene fix, NOT a cost fix, and it should not be sold as one. Its real value is that a per-request log line makes the failure mode expensive to leave running for weeks. Fix shape: sample or rate-limit the warn (once per bucket per N seconds per instance), or replace it with a counter. **THE TENSION IS LOAD-BEARING: silencing this log without adding the [[FIX-1038]] Upstash alarm makes the fail-open MORE invisible, which is the actual defect. Ship the alarm first or together — never this alone.** **(2) No durable record of 429s or fail-opens.** rateLimitResponse in apps/civitics/middleware.ts:144 returns the status and logs nothing. public.abuse_events — the [[FIX-880]] observe-only substrate built for exactly this — holds **0 rows** for the last 12 days on prod (verified: SELECT count(*) FROM abuse_events WHERE occurred_at >= now() - interval '12 days'), so either recordAbuseEvent is not wired to the limiter path or ABUSE_EVENT_PEPPER is still absent from Vercel env (a known pending item). The consequence: the ONLY way to answer 'was the limiter firing during the incident?' is Vercel logs, which retain about an hour and cap the CLI at 100 rows. This triage had to reconstruct the whole incident from billing counters because of it. **RateLimitResult already carries the right primitive** — the `limited` boolean exists specifically to distinguish a real 429 from a fail-open allow ('so callers can log/observe quota pressure without breaking', per the type's own comment) and NOTHING reads it. Wire it: count fail-opens and 429s per bucket, and land them somewhere queryable — abuse_events is the obvious home and already exists. **Explicitly NOT in scope: the fail-open semantics do not change.** It is load-bearing, it kept the site up during this incident, and it is correct. The defect is that it is silent. Cross-ref [[FIX-570]], [[FIX-637]], [[FIX-683]], [[FIX-797]], [[FIX-880]], [[FIX-1038]], [[FIX-1039]]. <!--id:FIX-1040-->
- [ ] 🟠 S — **platform_usage_snapshot has two blind spots that made this incident un-reconstructable — the cost breakdown is silently truncated to top-8, and Observability Events has no quantity metric at all** — **Found by the 2026-08-15 traffic/cost-spike triage (docs/audits/2026-08-15-traffic-cost-spike.md) — defects in the forensic instrument itself, surfaced by trying to use it.** **(1) The cost breakdown is always truncated, and a service can vanish from the record by rank change.** packages/db/src/platform-snapshot.ts:425 builds vercel_breakdown.services with .slice(0, 8). Measured on prod: jsonb_array_length is **8 on every single day** of the retained window — the cap is ALWAYS binding, never slack. On billing day 15 a 'Speed Insights Data Points' line entered the top 8 and silently **displaced 'Function Invocations' out of the stored payload**. So a service line can disappear from the audit trail with no error, no flag and no residual, purely because something else outranked it — and the 30-day retention means the history is then gone for good. This is the [[FIX-969]]-class 'no silent caps' defect applied to the cost record. Fix shape: store all non-zero service lines (there are ~9-10, this is not a size problem), or keep the top-8 for display but persist a total-minus-listed residual so truncation is at least detectable. **(2) Observability Events — the line that triggered this whole investigation — has NO quantity metric.** mapChargeQuantity in packages/db/src/vercel-usage.ts:129 returns null for it (the header even names Observability in the not-tracked list), so cost is the ONLY signal and the quantity is uninstrumented. That is why this triage could report 'observability-event COST rose 3.7x' but could never state an event count or a unit, and why premise 'observability events bill per ingested event' remains UNVERIFIED. Add the mapping, or record explicitly that no quantity is available so the gap is visible on the card rather than inferred. **(3) Stale header, corrected here for the next reader.** vercel-usage.ts documents billing/charges as returning 'only ever a trailing ~7-day window (it ignores from)'. **No longer true** — prod reads window_days=15 on 2026-08-15, incrementing by exactly one per calendar day since 08-03, i.e. it now honours from and returns month-to-date. The projection arithmetic is unaffected and still correct, but the comment misleads anyone reasoning about resolution. **The resolution limitation is worth documenting where the readers are.** Because values are cumulative MTD and step ONCE PER DAY at ~06:00-07:00 UTC (midnight Pacific — Vercel's ChargePeriodStart days are Pacific days), consecutive 10-minute snapshots are byte-identical to 16 decimal places. **The 10-minute snapshot cadence buys ZERO additional resolution for any Vercel metric.** An incident can only ever be localised to a calendar day, never to an hour. The series IS differentiable into per-day usage (that is how this whole audit was built) but nobody reading the card would know either fact. Cross-ref [[FIX-281]], [[FIX-644]], [[FIX-648]], [[FIX-969]], [[FIX-1038]]. <!--id:FIX-1041-->
- [x] 🔴 S — **The single most-crawled route on the site has no rate-limit bucket at all — /graph is 30 pct of attack traffic and middleware never inspects it, and robots.txt Disallow /*? did not stop the crawler** — **Found by the 2026-08-15 traffic/cost-spike triage (docs/audits/2026-08-15-traffic-cost-spike.md) once the crawler was identified.** **(1) The bucket gap.** getRateLimitBucket in apps/civitics/middleware.ts matches /api/search, /api/graph/narrative, /api/graph, the write paths, then LEAF_PAGE_RE (jurisdictions|districts|officials) and ENTITY_PAGE_RE (jurisdictions|officials|proposals|institutions|agencies|districts|meetings|donors|initiatives|investigations). **The PAGE route /graph matches none of them** — the "/api/graph" test is a startsWith on the API prefix and does not cover the page — so getRateLimitBucket returns **null** and no check is ever made. Measured against Cloudflare for the 24h to 2026-08-16 01:55 UTC: **/graph accounted for 44,689 of 147,611 requests (~30 pct), the single largest path on the zone by a wide margin**, every one carrying a query string of the form "?entity=UUID". So roughly a third of a hostile crawl targeted the one route the limiter structurally cannot see — and it would STILL be unlimited after [[FIX-1038]] restores Upstash. **This is a gap in the [[FIX-637]]/[[FIX-683]]/[[FIX-797]] design, not a regression:** those FIXes enumerated the entity-detail families by name and /graph is not an entity-detail family, so it was never in scope. It is now the most-attacked surface. **Fix shape:** add /graph to the bucketed set. Choose deliberately — it is a distinct cost class from the entity pages (client-heavy, and query-string-keyed rather than path-keyed), so it likely wants its own bucket rather than being folded into entity_pages, and the identifier should probably incorporate the "entity" query param since that is what makes each request unique. Confirm the actual render cost first: /graph did NOT appear in any "vercel logs" runtime sample during the incident while the entity pages did, which suggests it may be statically served and cheap per request — **if so the correct fix may be a cheap bucket or none at all, and the real finding is that nobody knew either way.** Measure before tuning (playbook: do not tune on vibes). **(2) robots.txt Disallow /*? did not stop it.** robots.txt (apps/civitics/public/robots.txt) carries the rule "Disallow: /*?" from [[FIX-513]], written precisely to stop query-string crawling after the 2026-06-07 ClaudeBot incident (~430k invocations). The crawler here is **meta-webindexer/1.1** (ASN 32934, Facebook Inc.) and it walked "/graph?entity=UUID" anyway, having fetched /robots.txt 88 times in the same window. Two candidate causes and they need distinguishing before anything is changed: (a) the crawler ignores robots.txt, or (b) **it does not support the star mid-path wildcard** — wildcard support in robots.txt is a Google/Bing extension, NOT part of the original standard, so a conforming-but-basic parser reads "Disallow: /*?" as a literal path and matches nothing. If (b), then **the FIX-513 defense is not portable across crawlers** and the durable fix is an explicit "Disallow: /graph" (a literal prefix every parser handles) alongside the wildcard, plus a named "User-agent: meta-webindexer" block. That is a meaningfully broader conclusion than 'one bot misbehaved' and is the reason this is filed rather than folded into [[FIX-1039]]. Cross-ref [[FIX-513]], [[FIX-637]], [[FIX-683]], [[FIX-797]], [[FIX-1038]], [[FIX-1039]]. <!--id:FIX-1042-->
- [x] 🟠 S — **apps/civitics and packages/db unit tests were never run by CI — 141 assertions sat green-by-assumption for months** — **Found while adding the [[FIX-1038]] limiter tests.** .github/workflows/tests.yml ran exactly one test step: 'pnpm --filter @civitics/data test'. apps/civitics carries 12 pure test files under src/ (browse registry, scope-tree, graph-compiler, cursor codec, legacy, browse-state, cdn-cache, entity-lens, graph-cohort, paginate, public-payload, abuse-events) and a working run-tests.mjs discoverer with a 'test' script in its package.json — **CI never invoked any of it**. Measured locally at the time of filing: 141 assertions across 13 suites, all passing, none of them enforced by any gate. packages/db was worse — it had no test runner and no 'test' script at all, so nothing in the package that owns the Supabase clients, platform-usage math, ai-pricing and the snapshot writer could be covered even in principle. **Why it matters beyond the missing coverage:** a test file that CI does not run is worse than no test, because it reads as protection in review. The FIX-1038/1040 limiter work would have shipped deny-cache and fail-over tests into exactly that hole. **Fixed in the same commit as FIX-1038:** two blocking steps added to tests.yml ('Unit tests (app)' and 'Unit tests (db)'), a run-tests.mjs added to packages/db mirroring the other two byte-for-byte, a 'test' script + a tsx devDependency added to packages/db (tsx was already resolvable in apps/civitics and packages/data but not there). **Follow-up worth considering, NOT done here:** packages/auth, packages/graph and packages/ui have no suites and no runner either, so the same silence applies to them by omission rather than by miswiring. Cross-ref [[FIX-458]] (which scoped this workflow), [[FIX-749]] (which added the app suites that were never run), [[FIX-1038]], [[FIX-1040]]. <!--id:FIX-1043-->
- [x] 🔴 M — **Cloudflare edge volume as the leading cost signal, plus day-over-day burn-rate alerting** — The 2026-08-15 crawl (docs/audits/2026-08-15-traffic-cost-spike.md) burned ~\$21/day for 16 hours and NOTHING PAGED. The alert system was healthy — the problem was structural, in what it watched. Monthly-cumulative thresholds cannot see a \$21/day burn for days; the one fast leading signal (FIX-642) watches Supabase db_connections; the FIX-648 fluid-cost signal reads Vercel data whose finest resolution is ONE DAY (consecutive 10-minute snapshots are byte-identical, so the cron cadence buys zero resolution on any Vercel metric). What actually stopped the burn was a vendor email read by a human, ~16h in. **D1 — Cloudflare becomes a first-class snapshot service.** Since FIX-1038 shipped scripts/cf-analytics.mjs we know the GraphQL Analytics API is scriptable with the token already in the env, and it is the ONLY near-real-time script-readable counter in the stack — every downstream dollar (Vercel invocations, Upstash commands, observability events) follows edge request volume. New shared module packages/db/src/cloudflare-analytics.ts (the CLI script stays as the CLI face); three platform_limits rows; one GraphQL call per snapshot tick. **THE METRIC IS ORIGIN-REACHING REQUESTS, NOT TOTAL EDGE REQUESTS.** originResponseStatus != 0 (available on this Free zone; probed 2026-08-16) means the request reached Vercel. Those are the ones that cost money (~\$1.23e-4 each, measured) and keying on them makes every layer SELF-LIMITING: while a mitigation absorbs a crawl, origin volume collapses and the alarm correctly goes quiet. Measured: 7,302 origin-reaching at 21:00 UTC on 08-15, 36 at 23:00 UTC, with edge volume unchanged at ~7,300 both hours. **THE THRESHOLD IS DERIVED, NOT PICKED.** Census of all 147 complete hours this Free zone retains before the crawl onset (2026-08-09 03:00 to 2026-08-15 05:00 UTC; retention is 1w1d = 8 days, which is why the census is a week and not longer): p50 77, p75 141, p90 283, p95 841, p99 1508, max 2218 origin req/hr. The unmitigated crawl: min 7158, p50 7233, max 7548. A 3.2x GAP separates the busiest legitimate hour ever recorded from the quietest crawl hour, so anything in [2500, 7000] classifies both sets perfectly. Chose 3000 = max-legit x 1.35: zero false positives across all 147 legitimate hours, 2.4x below the crawl floor so it also catches a crawl at 40 pct of this one rate, and ~\$8.85/day if sustained (~27x a baseline day 0.33 of consumption). NB this reads the last THREE complete hours per tick, not one: GHA cadence measured over 200 runs / 6.8 days is p50 46 min, p75 60, p90 87, max 155 — a single-hour read would silently skip hours on 26 pct of runs. **D2 — burn-rate layer.** The 08-15 audit established the spike was 3.7x baseline by hand-writing a window function over the cumulative MTD series; nothing automated it. New get_platform_daily_cost_deltas RPC (jsonb extraction stays in the DB — ~150 snapshot rows/day, large payloads) plus packages/db/src/burn-rate.ts. Fires only when BOTH conditions hold: delta >= \$1.00 absolute floor AND delta >= 3x the trailing-7-day median. Neither alone works — a pure multiple pages on every ordinary day once a quiet week drags the median down, a pure floor misses a burn starting from an elevated baseline. Differentiates CONSUMPTION (EffectiveCost minus the Pro subscription line), because the flat \$20/31 daily accrual compresses the spike day from 3.7x to 1.9x. Routed through the existing platform_alert_state edge-trigger machinery with new keys: new signals, not new substrate. Cross-ref [[FIX-1038]], [[FIX-1039]], [[FIX-1041]], [[FIX-1045]], [[FIX-1046]]. <!--id:FIX-1044-->
- [x] 🔴 M — **Closed-loop auto-mitigation: the cron raises the Cloudflare security level itself and reports what it did** — Craig explicit choice: on a sustained origin-volume spike the platform should ACT, then tell him — not ask him to act. On 2026-08-15 Under Attack mode cut origin-reaching traffic ~99 pct within one hour of enablement (21:00 UTC 7,313 x 200 / 0 x 403; 22:00 UTC 445 x 200 / 6,850 x 403), worth ~\$21/day. The mitigation works. What was missing was the detection and the hand on the switch, and both are mechanisable. New packages/db/src/cf-mitigation-loop.ts, run from the same Cloudflare reading [[FIX-1044]] collects so the decision and the number behind it can never disagree. **TRIGGER:** origin-reaching >= 3000/hr in >= 2 DISTINCT complete clock hours within a rolling 6h window. **ACTION:** PATCH security_level to under_attack. **REVERT:** automatic after 6h to the level it found, then re-trip on fresh evidence if the burn resumes (Under Attack mode is a dam, not a fix — it 403s every scripted client and challenges search crawlers, so leaving it up indefinitely has its own cost, and the revert is what makes the loop safe to leave armed). **SIX SAFETY RAILS, every one covered by tests that fail if the rail is removed.** (1) ESCALATE-ONLY: only ever raises, and only reverts an escalation IT made. If the zone is already at or above the target when the trigger fires it records NO TRIP AT ALL — claiming a level it did not set is exactly what would later make it auto-revert a human decision. (2) SUSTAINED MEANS MEASURED-SUSTAINED: the CF hourly bucket is already an hour of evidence, and the count is keyed on the BUCKET TIMESTAMP, never on tick count — at the measured p50 46-minute GHA cadence the cron re-reads the same bucket most ticks, so counting readings would let one bad hour trip it alone. Detection latency ~2-3h, ~\$1.80 of burn, accepted deliberately. (3) AUTO-REVERT WITH RE-TRIP. (4) DEBOUNCE: evidence is consumed on every transition and a fresh trip needs 2h since the last revert, so it cannot flap more than once per revert window. (5) FAIL-SAFE: any CF API error records, alerts, and takes no further action — and critically, a FAILED write records NO trip, because a phantom trip record would make the next run LOWER the zone security on the strength of a write that never landed. (6) KILL SWITCH: new cf_auto_mitigation switch plus CF_AUTO_MITIGATION_ENABLED env hard-kill disarms the WRITE while leaving detection, metrics and alerting fully live — disabling the loop can never make the platform blinder than before it shipped. Deliberately the ONLY kill switch with no auto_trip_threshold_pct: a cost metric must never be able to switch off the machinery that defends against cost. State lives in pipeline_state.cf_mitigation_loop, following the FIX-1040 precedent (durable signals are state TRANSITIONS, not per-request rows; abuse_events does not fit — it needs node:crypto and an admin client). Every transition emails via a new renderMitigationEmail template, debounced on the ACTION so a persistent condition reports once. **SHIPS ALERT-ONLY.** Measured 2026-08-16: the existing CLOUDFLARE_API_TOKEN does GET security_level fine (200, under_attack, modified 2026-08-15 22:03 UTC) but PATCH returns 403 code 9109, and GET /user/tokens/{id} is also refused so the scope cannot be read directly — it is inferred from the shape of the write failure. USER ITEM: mint a token with Zone Settings:Edit (keep Analytics:Read) and add CLOUDFLARE_API_TOKEN + optionally CLOUDFLARE_ZONE_ID to the Vercel env; the loop starts acting the moment it exists, no deploy. Documented in docs/CLOUDFLARE.md (how to tell an automatic change from a manual one) and docs/OPERATIONS.md (how to disable it). Cross-ref [[FIX-1039]], [[FIX-1040]], [[FIX-1044]]. <!--id:FIX-1045-->
- [x] 🟠 S — **The mitigation loop proves its own Cloudflare write scope instead of discovering it mid-incident** — Follow-up to [[FIX-1045]], found while confirming Craig token edit. **THE GAP:** the loop only ever learned whether its Cloudflare token could WRITE at the moment it first tried to — i.e. during a live burn. isScopeError() classifies a 9109 correctly and the loop degrades to alert-only and emails, so it fails SAFE, but the question "is this thing actually armed?" was unanswerable until the one moment the answer had to be right. A token minted without Zone Settings:Edit, later rolled, re-scoped to the wrong zone, or expired stayed invisible until the mitigation was already needed. That is the same class of silent failure that made 2026-08-15 cost what it did — a hard control switched off with no signal. **THE PROBE:** twice a day, issue a PATCH of security_level with THE VALUE THE ZONE ALREADY HAS. 200 proves Edit, 9109 proves Read-only. Cloudflare accepts it, changes nothing, and does NOT move modified_on — verified by hand across the permission change on 2026-08-16, pinned at 2026-08-15T22:03:19Z through no-op PATCHes on both sides of it. There is no safer write available; every other zone setting would be a real change. The caller contract is load-bearing and enforced by making currentLevel a REQUIRED argument rather than something the probe fetches itself: pass anything other than a value just read from that zone and the probe becomes a real mutation. **WHY TWICE A DAY AND NOT EVERY TICK:** an idempotent PATCH still writes a Cloudflare AUDIT LOG entry, and the audit log is one of the three documented ways to tell an automatic change from a manual one (docs/CLOUDFLARE.md). At the measured cron cadence, probing every tick would produce ~40 no-op entries/day and bury exactly the signal it exists to protect. Cached in pipeline_state.cf_mitigation_loop.scope_probe with a 12h interval. Staleness is harmless for CORRECTNESS — before a real trip the loop attempts the actual write regardless and that write is authoritative — so a stale cache can only make the REPORTED status lag, never cause a wrong action. A DISARMED loop never probes (a no-op write is still a write) and a THROWN probe keeps the prior verdict rather than inventing a pessimistic one that would suppress a real mitigation. **ALSO: the trip threshold is now env-overridable** via CF_TRIP_ORIGIN_REQ_THRESHOLD, mirroring LEADING_DB_CONN_THRESHOLD ([[FIX-642]]) and LEADING_FLUID_USD_THRESHOLD ([[FIX-648]]) which are overridable for exactly this reason: an alarm nobody has watched fire is an alarm nobody should trust, and before this the only way to exercise the write path end-to-end was to wait for a real crawl. Set it to 50 and the next two breached hours trip for real, email, hold 6h and auto-revert. A non-numeric or non-positive value is IGNORED rather than obeyed — an env typo must never be able to set the threshold to 0 and trip the loop on every quiet hour. The EFFECTIVE threshold rides in the snapshot payload and the card shows "threshold OVERRIDDEN to N/hr" in amber whenever it differs from the derived 3000, because a verify-run value left in place is its own incident. **Card wording now distinguishes four states** — armed+verified, ALERT-ONLY, armed (unverified, i.e. a pre-FIX-1047 snapshot or never probed), and disarmed — where before "armed" meant only that the kill switch was on. **PREMISE CORRECTED WHILE INVESTIGATING:** a vercel env pull comparison appeared to show Vercel holding a DIFFERENT Cloudflare token than the local files. That was wrong and the check was invalid — Vercel cannot read encrypted values back, and all 40+ sensitive vars returned an identical 11-character redaction placeholder. Never diff a Vercel secret against a local one this way; compare token IDs via /user/tokens/verify instead. Cross-ref [[FIX-1045]], [[FIX-1039]]. <!--id:FIX-1047-->
- [x] 🟢 S — **Upstash usage is readable from the management API — the leading half of FIX-1038, and the allotment is MONTHLY not daily** — **Shipped, filed retroactively for the record.** The code landed in sha 90404380, which carries only 'Fixes: FIX-1044, FIX-1045, FIX-1046' — this work was swept into that commit from a concurrent session's working tree (see the shared-index hazard) and therefore had no trailer, no bullet and no done.log row of its own. Nothing to build; this bullet exists so the audit trail is not missing a shipped capability. **What [[FIX-1038]] left open.** That FIX could only detect exhaustion AFTER it happened: the repo held just the per-database REST credentials, and Upstash exposes command counts solely through its management API. So the Platform Costs card alarmed at the instant the limiter died, never before. A management key (UPSTASH_EMAIL + UPSTASH_API_KEY) has since been minted, closing the gap. **What shipped.** getUpstashUsage() in packages/db/src/upstash-usage.ts reads GET /v2/redis/database/id and GET /v2/redis/stats/id, cached 5 min, two GETs per snapshot tick that do NOT draw on the Redis command allotment. platform_usage upstash.period_commands is now source=api and live every tick; included_limit is refreshed each tick from the vendor's own db_request_limit so a tier change self-corrects, the same self-correcting pattern FIX-351 uses for Supabase disk size. scripts/upstash-db-id.mjs resolves UPSTASH_DATABASE_ID by joining the management API's endpoint field against UPSTASH_REDIS_REST_URL, because the two are different identifiers and the UUID is not derivable from the hostname. **THE ALLOTMENT IS MONTHLY. The 2026-08-15 triage assumed daily and that was wrong.** Vendor-confirmed: db_request_limit 500000, total_monthly_requests 498964, daily_net_commands 1. The consequence is the whole reason the FIX-1038 fail-over matters: one 16-hour crawl did not cost a day of rate limiting, it cost the REST OF THE BILLING CYCLE, and daily_net_commands 1 against 498964 monthly is that fact in two numbers — nothing is being spent today because nothing CAN be. Database created circa 2026-06-13 (creation_time 1781337093), so if Upstash bills on the creation anniversary the reset is circa 2026-09-13. **Until then the per-instance memory fail-over is the ONLY per-IP limiting in production**, which retroactively makes that change load-bearing rather than defensive. Confirm the exact reset date in the console. **Two counters, and they disagree by design.** At one instant the billing counter read 498964 of 500000 while enforcement was already refusing with Usage 500002. Billing LAGS enforcement, so upstash.limiter_degraded stays driven by the PING probe and never by this percentage — never read 'under quota' as 'limiter working'. This also forced critical_pct from 100 down to 95 in migration 20260816070000: a 100 percent trigger on a counter that appears to stop below the cap is structurally unreachable, and the row would have sat at 'warning' through a total outage. **Security note.** UPSTASH_API_KEY is UNSCOPED — Upstash documents per-privilege keys as a roadmap item, not a shipped feature — so it can delete databases and is strictly higher-privilege than the REST token. Server-only, never NEXT_PUBLIC_. The helper issues GETs only, never logs the key, and never reads or surfaces the read_only_rest_token the detail endpoint returns (there is a test asserting that). **Pending operator steps:** UPSTASH_DATABASE_ID into .env.local.prod, all three management vars into Vercel, and migrations 20260816030000 plus 20260816070000 pushed to prod — until then both Upstash rows render nothing. Cross-ref [[FIX-570]], [[FIX-351]], [[FIX-1038]], [[FIX-1040]], [[FIX-1044]]. <!--id:FIX-1048-->
- [x] 🟠 S — **platform_limits touch-ups: R2 right-size, first-cent overage alert, gross-overage alignment** — Three config-and-correctness items following [[FIX-1044]]/[[FIX-1046]]/[[FIX-1047]]/[[FIX-1049]]. (1) R2 storage limit 10 GiB to 24 GiB — the FEC pipelines legitimately outgrew the seed and the row had been latched critical at 163% since, holding any_critical true on its own and detecting nothing; 24 GiB of R2 is about 0.36 USD/mo so this limit is a growth alarm, not a spend cap. (2) Vercel overage alerting becomes email-at-the-first-cent, page-at-10-USD. warning_pct is an INTEGER column and one cent of the 20 USD credit is 0.05%, so the first-cent band is not expressible on the dollar row — and warning_pct 0 is worse than a rounding error because the band test is pct >= warning_pct, which would park every row including a zero-valued one in the warning band forever. Added a 0/1 companion row vercel.overage_present on the upstash.limiter_degraded pattern (included_limit 1, warning 1, critical 101 so it can only email, never page), written from computeVercelBilling().billable_overage_mtd_usd — ACTUAL month-to-date, not the projection, because the projection scales by daysInCycle/windowDays and would read about 22 USD from one ordinary day of usage, false-firing in the opening days of most months. The dollar row keeps the projection and moves its page from 20 USD to 10 USD. (3) summary.total_overage_cost aligned: it was never computed from Vercel gross list cost (it is the sum of per-metric quota overage at vendor list price and has never read EffectiveCost), but it did still count vercel.origin_transfer_bytes at 0.15 USD/GB above 1 TiB — consumption that [[FIX-1046]] already draws against the 20 USD credit, so the same bytes were billable under two definitions. Latent rather than visible only because the account sits at 3.1 GB of a 1 TiB allowance. Vercel now contributes exactly one figure to that scalar, its credit-aware billable overage, MTD to match every other contributor. <!--id:FIX-1050-->
- [x] 🟠 M — **EC-stats and the afternoon vacuum wave both start INSIDE the EC rebuild's 6h window — 191 pg_cron job-startup-timeout failures, and mwp=12 did not clear it** — Measured 2026-08-17 (cc-prompt-63 phase 0). PREMISE CORRECTION: the Monday burst is NOT statement timeouts. Over 08-10..08-17 prod logged 191 pg_cron failures with return_message 'job startup timeout' and only THREE 'canceling statement due to statement timeout' (08-10 08:00, 08-10 09:00, 08-11 07:00). A startup timeout is pg_cron failing to launch a background worker within its ~10s window (most failures land at exactly 10-12s), not a query being killed. THE MONDAY 08-17 SHAPE: jobid 22 rebuild-ec-incremental-mon ran 08:00->14:00 (21,615s = the 6h ceiling exactly, FIX-985), closed its own data_sync_log row as status=partial with 4,011,180 rows and error 'canceled — rebuild_entity_connections_co...' (so FIX-1028's query_canceled handler IS working); jobid 24 donor-rollup-refresh ran 09:00->10:50 partial 'budget exhausted — resumable' (FIX-1002/1021 working). Inside that window: jobid 16 entity-connection-stats-rebuild FAILED at 11:00 after 10s; the 11:05 and 11:18 vacuum slots failed while 11:10-11:16 succeeded but ran 30-90x their normal duration (93s/33s/60s vs 0-2s); the 12:00 donor-rollup failed; and at 14:05-14:18 ALL SIX vacuum jobs failed. TWO FINDINGS THAT CHANGE THE FIX SHAPE. (1) Monday 08-17 was the FIRST REAL TEST of max_worker_processes=12 with jobid 16 active — the 2026-08-12 audit named exactly this test, having noted the Wednesday 08-12 run was confounded because jobids 2 and 16 were paused by hand for that day. jobid 16 failed the test. mwp=12 is live (pg_settings source=configuration file) and did NOT clear the contention. (2) THE FAILURE WINDOW OUTLASTS BOTH HEAVY JOBS BY OVER TWO HOURS: failures run 09:xx to 16:08 while the EC rebuild ended 14:00 and the donor rollup 10:50, and nothing in data_sync_log runs 14:00-16:08. So the 6h-squatter story does not close. Worker-slot arithmetic does not close either: only 3 bgworkers are baseline (pg_cron launcher, pg_net, logical replication launcher) against mwp=12, with max_parallel_workers=2. The watchdog jobid 40 fires exactly 30x/hour throughout with no backlog accumulation, so it is a victim not an amplifier. The resource actually exhausted 14:00-16:08 is NOT established from catalog evidence and needs the Supabase Analytics postgres logs. IT HAD USER-FACING IMPACT: the IRS-990 job at 15:28-15:31, inside the window, died on 'Could not query the database for the schema cache' — PostgREST was degraded, not just cron. MOVES APPLIED THIS SESSION (migration, alter_job by NAME per playbook D3 to preserve run history): entity-connection-stats-rebuild 0 11 * * 1,3 -> 0 16 * * 1,3, and the afternoon vacuum wave (odr-mv, treemap-individuals, odt, osdr, osar, odbt) 14:05-14:18 -> 17:05-17:18. Reasoning for the first is correctness as much as contention: EC-stats reads entity_connections and currently starts at 11:00 with the rebuild still three hours from its ceiling, so it is both starved AND reading a half-rebuilt table; 16:00 is after the 14:00 ceiling with margin. Reasoning for the second: 14:0x sits exactly on the rebuild's cancel-and-rollback exit, and it went 0-for-6 on Monday. NEITHER MOVE TOUCHES jobid 9/10, so the FIX-1033 constraint holds — the daily/weekly derived-MV pair still share one advisory lock and the watchdog's single-slot in-flight state stays safe; no per-run keying needed yet. THESE MOVES ARE NECESSARY BUT LIKELY NOT SUFFICIENT, because of finding (2): they cannot explain the 14:00-16:08 tail. Cross-ref [[FIX-985]], [[FIX-1022]], [[FIX-1028]], [[FIX-1033]], [[FIX-1002]], [[FIX-971]]. <!--id:FIX-1052-->
- [x] 🟡 S — **recipient_count_reconcile is gated behind an EC rebuild that no longer completes — the 937h staleness is a downstream symptom, not a scheduling defect** — Answers the open question in [[FIX-994]], which framed this as a correlation/cadence-detection problem. Measured on prod 2026-08-17. The chain is structural, not schedule-related. (1) The recipient_count_reconcile data_sync_log row is written by the PROCEDURE reconcile_recipient_count(). (2) That procedure has EXACTLY ONE caller anywhere in the prod catalog: run_entity_connections_rebuild(). No cron job CALLs it directly — jobid 14 financial-entity-totals-reconcile calls reconcile_financial_entity_totals(), a different procedure, and jobids 15/18/19/23 call the other orphan sweeps. (3) run_entity_connections_rebuild() is jobid 2 (Wed 08:00) and jobid 22 (Mon 08:00), and it has NOT run to completion since 2026-07-29: 08-03 failed, 08-05 failed at 43s, 08-10 failed, 08-12 was paused by hand during the incident response, 08-17 ran the full 6h and was canceled at the ceiling. So the reconcile arm sits downstream of a rebuild that no longer reaches its tail. THE RULING-OUT MATTERS: the schedule was not removed, the job was not disabled, and no migration dropped the caller — jobid 14 ran normally on 08-01 12:00 and wrote its own financial_entity_totals_reconcile row that same minute while writing NO recipient_count_reconcile row, which is the discriminating observation. This is therefore NOT an S-class reschedule and was deliberately not 'fixed' by re-enabling anything; it resolves when the EC rebuild can finish (see [[FIX-1052]] and the FIX-985 6h ceiling). OPEN SUB-QUESTION, deliberately not over-claimed: the last recipient_count_reconcile row is 2026-07-05 but the EC rebuild last SUCCEEDED 2026-07-29, so either the call was added to run_entity_connections_rebuild() after that date or it is conditional on a branch the 07-29 run did not take. Resolve by reading the procedure body's call site before treating the 07-05 date as a rebuild-completion proxy. Cross-ref [[FIX-994]], [[FIX-985]], [[FIX-978]], [[FIX-977]]. **✅ RESOLVED 2026-08-18 (cc-prompt-66) — REDIRECTED TO [[FIX-1059]]. THE OPEN SUB-QUESTION IS ANSWERED AND THE ANSWER INVERTS THE BULLET.** This bullet's premise (2) — "that procedure has EXACTLY ONE caller anywhere in the prod catalog: run_entity_connections_rebuild()" — is wrong, and the bullet was right to flag it as needing verification before the 07-05 date was treated as a rebuild-completion proxy. Read on prod 2026-08-18: run_entity_connections_rebuild() mentions reconcile_recipient_count exactly TWICE and **both are inside a comment** ("recipient_count is reconciled out-of-band (reconcile_recipient_count(), monthly post-VACUUM)"). There is no CALL. A catalog-wide search over pg_get_functiondef for every function and procedure in public returns two hits: the procedure itself, and that comment. No cron job invokes it either. THE REASON IS RECORDED AND DELIBERATE: migration 20260705000200_fix736_recipient_count_fr_derived.sql states "Its cron job (ec-recipient-count-reconcile) is unscheduled below — the routine FR orphan sweep is now owned by financial-entity-totals-reconcile", and executes SELECT cron.unschedule(jobname) FROM cron.job WHERE jobname = 'ec-recipient-count-reconcile'. [[FIX-736]] repurposed the procedure as an FR-based BREAK-GLASS full recompute and left it callerless ON PURPOSE. So the last recipient_count_reconcile row being 2026-07-05 is not a symptom of anything — it is the last firing before a deliberate retirement, and the arm is NOT gated behind the EC rebuild. Fixing the rebuild would not have made it run, and closing this on the back of a successful rebuild would have been a false close. The stale comment that caused the misreading is corrected in migration 20260818000000. THE REAL DEFECT IS THE DETECTOR, filed as [[FIX-1059]]: [[FIX-977]]'s watch registry and canary-check.ts still watch this pipeline and escalate on its staleness, with no way to mark a pipeline RETIRED — so a deliberate retirement is indistinguishable from a breakage and pages forever. Cross-ref [[FIX-1059]], [[FIX-736]], [[FIX-1056]]. <!--id:FIX-1053-->
- [x] 🟡 S — **nightly-sync rows never leave status='triggered' — 104 unclosed rows back to 2026-04-30, permanently indistinguishable from a hung dispatch** — Found 2026-08-17 while reconciling the stuck-running data_sync_log rows named in the cc-prompt-63 ledger. THE LEDGER PREMISE WAS WRONG in a useful way: there is no stuck 2026-07-27 fec_bulk 'running' row on prod. What exists instead is a much larger, older population — 104 rows with pipeline='nightly-sync' and status='triggered' and completed_at IS NULL, running continuously from 2026-04-30 to 2026-08-17, i.e. essentially every nightly dispatch ever made. These are the GHA dispatcher's own rows, opened when the workflow is triggered and never closed by anything; the actual work reports separately under nightly_cron and the per-stage pipeline names, which DO close correctly. WHY IT MATTERS RATHER THAN BEING COSMETIC: every freshness/liveness detector that scans data_sync_log for unclosed rows has to special-case or silently swallow this population, and a genuinely hung dispatch would be indistinguishable from the 104 benign ones. It also means the reaper has 104 permanent candidates it either skips or re-examines forever. Note the contrast with the reaped rows, which are correct post-[[FIX-979]]: 30 rows across fec_bulk/nightly_cron/refresh_derived_mvs/etc carry status='reaped', completed_at IS NULL and a reap_note spelling out that the span is an upper bound on reaper latency rather than a runtime — that is exactly the shape [[FIX-971]] asked for, and it is working. Fix shape, cheapest first: either have the dispatcher close its own row on workflow completion (it now has GITHUB_RUN_ID in metadata per FIX-971, so the GHA API can supply the conclusion), or stop opening a sync row for the dispatch at all and let nightly_cron be the only record, or give the dispatch a distinct terminal status so 'triggered' means 'dispatch acknowledged' rather than 'in flight'. Prefer the last if the row has diagnostic value. Cross-ref [[FIX-971]], [[FIX-979]], [[FIX-963]], [[FIX-962]]. <!--id:FIX-1054-->
- [x] 🟠 S — **IRS-990's first real Monday firing failed on PostgREST schema-cache errors — the FIX-904 extraction works, the run did not** — [[FIX-904]] was reopened 2026-08-11 with an explicit condition: it stays open until its first real Monday firing produces a durable verify. That firing happened 2026-08-17 and FAILED. GHA run 32042442990 (workflow irs990.yml, schedule '0 15 * * 1'), started 15:28:40Z, 2m42s, conclusion failure. WHAT THE FIX GOT RIGHT: the extraction is proven. The job fired on its own weekly schedule, entirely independent of the Sunday isWeekly gate inside the fec phase, which is precisely the stranded-behind-a-calendar-gate defect FIX-904 existed to close. The mechanism works. WHAT FAILED IS UNRELATED TO THE FIX: three consecutive PostgREST failures with the same message — '[sync-log] Could not create log entry: Could not query the database for the schema cache. Retrying.', then 'IRS 990 pipeline threw: officials load failed: Could not query the database for the schema cache. Retrying.', then the mark-killed cleanup step hit it too ('[mark-killed] data_sync_log query failed: ...'). So the pipeline could neither open its sync row, nor read officials, nor clean up after itself. THE TIMING IS THE POINT: 15:28-15:31 UTC sits inside the 09:xx-16:08 saturation window characterised in [[FIX-1052]], during which 191 pg_cron background-worker launches were failing. This is the first evidence that the Monday saturation degrades the REQUEST PATH and not merely scheduled work — PostgREST could not serve a schema-cache query. That materially raises the priority of the deconfliction work, which had been reasoned about purely as a cron-completion problem. Secondary observations from the same log, not causal: two 429 'Too Many Requests' warnings retrying the pnpm/action-setup download, and a pre-existing pnpm ENOENT warning creating the supabase bin symlink. Neither aborted the job. NEXT READ: the Monday 2026-08-24 firing, after the FIX-1052 schedule moves have had a week to act.
